  - Opens a real window via `oswindow::app::launch`; every clickable thing is a `Target` whose box is recorded by the renderer as it paints, so a click and the eye read one frame rather than two expressions that can disagree.
  - Fixed a navigation bug the wiring exposed: the app kept a `grid_columns: 16` while the renderer derived its own column count from the window width, so arrow-Down moved by 16 while the eye followed a row of a different length, and Page Up/Down jumped through a grid that did not exist. Both now come from one `Layout` computed from the live window size and never remembered.
  - Fixed a scroll bug of the same shape: `render_grid` worked out a scroll row under `&self`, used it for one frame and threw it away, so `grid_scroll` was never what the screen showed and the wheel could not move it. The wheel now scrolls whichever list the pointer is over — block list or grid — with a separate fraction accumulator each.
  - A narrow window drops the sidebar and detail panels rather than squeezing them into unreadable slivers, and their hit boxes vanish with them.
  - Enclosing marks (Me) are now classified, so the category label the detail panel could always print is finally reachable by some codepoint.
- [x] Camera / Webcam Viewer (apps/camera: camera device management with 3 simulated devices, video frame capture, photo gallery with grid/list/filmstrip views, video recording with duration/bitrate tracking, camera settings (brightness/contrast/saturation/exposure/white balance/zoom), 8 image filters (grayscale/sepia/negative/blur/warm/cool/high-contrast), self-timer (3s/5s/10s), multi-camera switching, histogram overlay, 122 tests)
  - [x] **Windowed** (apps/camera: `app::launch`, a `Layout` solved from the live window size every frame, hit boxes recorded by the drawing pass, and a clock that arrives through `Event::Tick`). This is the forty-sixth application in the wiring campaign. `main` was a script: it made a `CameraApp`, ticked a few simulated frames, took two photographs nobody saw, rendered a tree into a `Vec` and bound it to `let _`. Every pixel it produced was discarded, no click or key ever reached the program, and it still exited zero — with four sidebar panels, eight image filters, a self-timer, a recording session and a gallery sitting underneath with nothing to run in. Wiring it found eight faults. (1) **The layout was compile-time furniture** — a 260 px sidebar, a 100 px strip, a 48 px toolbar — subtracted from whatever window it was handed, so a 200 px-wide window gave the viewfinder a *negative* width. `Layout::solve` derives every pane from the live size now and gives up the strip before the sidebar and the sidebar before the viewfinder, which is never given up at all. (2) **The toolbar walked a cursor along the strip with literals**: `tx += 80` after the word "Camera", `tx += 160` after the camera name. A longer device name ran into the mode buttons; a shorter one left a hole. Every item is measured now, and one that does not fit is dropped rather than drawn off the edge. (3) **Every label was drawn with no `max_width` at all**, so a long camera name wrote over the mode buttons and a long status message over the photo count; all text goes through `bounded` or `centred`. (4) **The button height came from the font and not from the toolbar**, so in a window shorter than the toolbar wanted, the buttons were painted past the bottom edge of the window entirely — found by the size sweep before a single mutation was written, which is the whole argument for sweeping sizes rather than checking one. (5) **`tick` had no caller** outside `main`'s simulation and the tests: the running program never advanced its own clock, so the recording timer, the self-timer and the flash were all frozen. It is driven by `Event::Tick` now and answers `Idle` when nothing moved, so a still camera does not repaint thirty times a second. (6) **Filters were chosen by matching each digit to a filter *by name*** in a second list kept in step with the first by hand. (7) `#![allow(dead_code)]` covered the whole crate, and two palette entries no picture used were dead behind it. (8) **Fourteen tests asserted `!cmds.is_empty()` and nothing else** — a frame that drew one grey rectangle would have satisfied every one of them. The suite is 122 tests, of which 35 sweep 19 states across a 56-point grid of window sizes: nothing painted outside the window, no unbounded run of text, clips balanced, every hit box inside the window and clickable, every control drawn in some state and reachable in the state that draws it, and the shutter meaning the same thing by mouse and by space bar. The mutation table is 33 rows; the first sweep caught 30 of them, and **both survivors were faults in the tests rather than in the table.** One was pure lesson 103 in a new dress: the device panel's test asked `probe::rect_of` where `Target::Resolution(idx)` was and then clicked there, so a drawing pass that recorded every row under its *neighbour's* target answered the question with the same offset it had made the mistake with — finding and clicking cancelled out and a panel where every row chose the setting above it passed. It finds the row by the words drawn in it now, which is the only thing in the frame a user can actually read and the one question the picture cannot answer with its own error; the filter panel had the identical blindness, was fixed the same way, and gained a new mutation that shifts every row's payload by one to prove it. The other survivor was not a fault at all: widening the strip's clip from the strip to the whole window changes nothing, because `fits` is a *floor* of whole steps and the last tile's right edge lands a whole pad inside the strip at every size in the grid — the clip is belt to the braces of the count. That row was removed with the reason recorded in the table rather than given an owning test, which would have been a claim of coverage that does not exist.
- [x] Pomodoro Focus Timer (apps/pomodoro: **windowed** — `app::launch`, `Frame` hit boxes, `Layout` from the live window size, and a heartbeat that runs fast while a block does and slow while one does not. `main` built the app and dropped it, so four screens, eight settings and a focus log were all unreachable; wiring it up surfaced four more faults that only a running clock and a pointer expose. (1) **It counted its own ticks instead of the time.** `tick()` took no argument and subtracted exactly one second per call, so a 25-minute block measured *frames the loop delivered*, not minutes, and ran long by however late the loop was — silently, and cumulatively across a set. It advances by `elapsed_ms` now and carries the sub-second remainder, verified over 2400 consecutive ticks. (2) **It did not know what day it was.** `current_day` was the string literal `"2026-05-18"`, so every session anyone would ever run landed in one bucket: the daily goal was a goal-since-launch and the streak — whose entire subject is *consecutive days* — could not exceed one. The day now comes from the machine's clock and rolls at midnight while the app is open. (3) **The streak counted rows, not days.** It walked `daily_stats` backwards counting `goal_met`, but a day with no sessions leaves no row, so Monday and Wednesday sat adjacent in the vector and a skipped Tuesday was invisible; `DayStats` holds a `Date` rather than a `"YYYY-MM-DD"` string now, because string comparison cannot answer "is this the day before that one". (4) **Nothing was clickable and nothing fit.** Four tabs at a fixed 120px needed 496px of a 600px window; the progress ring was a constant 100px radius whatever room was left; the eight settings rows were drawn at a fixed 32px stride from a fixed origin, so the last of them sat under the status bar on a short window — reachable with `Down`, invisible once reached. Every control is a hit box now, the log scrolls under the wheel and stops where its last row reaches the bottom line rather than `len - 1`, and the settings values gained `‹ ›` steppers that take the click off the row they sit on. Classic Pomodoro technique with customizable work/break durations, round tracking per set, daily statistics with goal tracking, streak counting, task labeling, 7 ambient sound options, auto-start configurable, focus log with timestamped entries, settings editor, notification overlay, 4-screen UI (Timer/Stats/Log/Settings), 84 tests)
- [x] Network Scanner (apps/netscan: IP scanning with CIDR/range/individual parsing, port scanning with 115 service mappings, host discovery with ping/ARP/TCP connect methods, 4 scan profiles (Quick/Full/Custom/Stealth), network topology visualization, scan history with host diff detection, Wake-on-LAN magic packets, WHOIS lookup, traceroute simulation, CSV/JSON export, bandwidth estimation, 82 tests)
- [x] Screen Magnifier (apps/magnifier: **windowed** — accessibility zoom at four modes (fullscreen, lens, docked top, docked bottom), zoom 1.5x-20x over ten presets, nine colour filters, three tracking settings, a crosshair, a ruler, a pixel-colour readout and a help sheet; 109 tests). Wiring it found ten faults on top of the missing window, and the ones that matter are the ones only a window can show. (1) `main` built a `MagnifierApp`, dropped it and exited: nothing was ever magnified and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **The layout was two constants.** `width: 800.0, height: 600.0` were set once in `new` and assigned by nothing thereafter, and every rectangle in the drawing pass came from them, so in any window that was not exactly 800x600 the toolbar, the readout and the help sheet drew off the edge or floated in the middle. `Layout` is derived from the live window size each frame and never stored. (3) **There was no pointer input at all** — no mouse handler, and `update_mouse` existed with no caller. Lens mode, whose entire premise is a lens that follows the pointer, could not move, and the `[H]elp [M]ode [T]rack [F]ilter` toolbar was a picture of buttons. Every control is a hit box recorded by the pass that draws it. (4) **Keys arrived as strings nothing produced**: `handle_key(key: &str, ctrl: bool, shift: bool)` matched `"Left"`, `"F1"`, `"="`, where a window delivers a `KeyEvent` carrying a `Key` and a `pressed` flag — so not one arm could ever have fired, and with no `pressed` check every keystroke would have counted twice. (5) **The lens could not be resized, provably**: the four `"Left" if shift` arms sat *below* the four `"Left" if !ctrl` arms, and Shift-Left has `ctrl` false, so the earlier arm always won and the later four were unreachable — which the compiler would have said, but for the `#![allow(dead_code)]` on line 1. (6) **The colour readout could never be dismissed**: `show_color_picker` was set true by `pick_color_at_center` and set false nowhere in the program, so once shown it covered the bottom-right corner for the life of the process — and it was laid over the toolbar it exactly overlapped. It is a toggle now, and a band in the layout rather than a panel on top of one. (7) **Pausing paused only the picture.** `enabled` was read in `render` and nowhere else, so a paused magnifier still zoomed, panned, cycled filters and picked colours; it just showed you none of it. (8) **The lens showed somewhere other than where it was**: `render_lens` positioned the lens at `mouse_x/mouse_y` and filled it from `center_x/center_y`, which are the same number only while tracking follows the mouse — in `Manual` tracking the lens sat under your hand and showed a different part of the screen. (9) **The ruler measured in the wrong direction and drew a horizontal bar**: `screen_distance` *divided* the length by the zoom where the endpoints are screen coordinates and the on-screen length is the product, and the drawing ignored `end_y` altogether, painting a two-pixel bar from `min_x` to `max_x` in screen coordinates inside a window that is not the screen. (10) **The magnified view could hang off the screen**: `move_center` clamped the *centre* to the screen rectangle rather than the region around it, so at a corner three quarters of the view sampled out of bounds and came back black. Two absences rather than bugs: the app asked for no clock, so the smoothing `smooth_edges` promised could never have eased anything — the seventh application of `known-issues.md` lesson 47, "an app that keeps time but never receives the clock" — and only six of the ten zoom presets had a key, 6x, 15x and 20x being reachable solely by stepping. One hundred and four deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/magnifier/mutate.py`). Six survived the first sweep and every one was a weak test rather than a spare guard, all six of the same shape — **a test that asks the program what something means and then checks the program went there is self-consistent and green however wrong the program is**. (a) `a_click_aims_at_the_screen_point_under_the_pixel_it_landed_on` computed its expectation with `screen_at`, which is the function the click handler itself calls; it now works the point out from the region the pane is *showing* (`magnified_source`), so the two sides of the assertion no longer share a body. (b) `the_lens_sits_over_the_part_of_the_screen_it_is_showing` recomputed the placement with the same `source_rect`/`window_point` pair the placement uses; it now reads the lens out of the frame the window drew and asks what is *behind* it — the screen point the life-size view shows at the lens's centre, through `screen_point`, which is `window_point`'s inverse and not the function the placement is made of — and guards that the pointer is over somewhere else, without which "at the pointer" and "over what it shows" are the same assertion. Its first rewrite was still vacuous and survived the same mutant a second time, which is worth recording: it asked `screen_at(centre of the lens)`, and `screen_at` reads the *lens*, whose source region is by construction centred on the picture's centre — so it answers "the centre" wherever the lens happens to be. **An inverse is only a check if it goes back through a different mapping than the one under test**; this one went back through the same one. (c) `the_bands_are_dropped_whole_in_the_stated_order` compared the observed order against `BAND_DROP_ORDER`, the constant that produced it, and so passed for every order; the order is written out literally now, with the constant checked against it — the same fault, in the same test, as pipes. (d) `the_tracking_key_walks_the_three_settings` had it too, deduping against `TRACKINGS.len()` rather than naming the three. (e) `the_filter_reaches_the_pixels_the_window_draws` was an `assert_ne!` on two lists of fills, satisfied by *any* difference at all including a block moving one pixel; it now checks each block's colour against `255 - c` component by component. (f) `a_paused_window_says_so_instead_of_drawing_a_stale_picture` only looked for the substring "paused", which the header prints while running too; it now requires the words "Magnifier paused" and, more to the point, requires that the picture actually stop — saying "paused" over a frozen screenshot is precisely the fault, since the user cannot tell a paused magnifier from one whose screen stopped changing. The second sweep found two more of exactly the same kind, which is the argument for sweeping twice. `a_simulation_filter_leaves_a_grey_exactly_as_grey_as_it_was` is new, because putting an offset into a dichromacy matrix survived `every_filter_maps_the_corners_of_the_colour_cube_somewhere_sensible` — the corners **cannot** see it, black being the zero vector that survives any matrix at all and white saturating, so a row summing to 1.4 still comes back 255; only a grey in between can tell, and a row summing to anything but one is a filter that changes the brightness of every pixel, which is simulating something nobody has. And `a_click_inside_the_lens_is_read_against_the_lens_and_not_the_pane_behind_it` was asking `screen_at` directly, which does no hit-testing, so deleting the lens's `f.hit(Target::Magnified, …)` did not fail it — the click test of all tests has to go through the hit boxes and the event path, and it does now. Two further findings came out of the sweep rather than out of a test. The lens body was **written twice** — `lens_rect` and `showing_pane` each computed it, which is the exact shape of fault 8 and is why a mutation could hit one copy while the test read the other; there is one `lens_in` now, and the drawing, the hit box and the reading all come from it. And one mutant was dropped as provably equivalent rather than papered over: the colour matrix's `sum.clamp(0.0, 255.0) as u8` cannot be told from `sum.min(255.0) as u8`, because a Rust float-to-integer `as` cast saturates at *both* ends, so the lower clamp is unobservable; `mutate.py` records why it is not a mutation. The seventh app through the **C-CENTRING-IS-NOT-A-BOUND** campaign (`known-issues.md`), which added thirteen tests and twenty-nine mutation rows and found four more faults, two of them of kinds the campaign had not met before. The two the new containment suite caught on its first run were **both hidden by a clip**, which is the recurring lesson: `Frame::clip` pushes its command unconditionally, so a bound only the clip enforces is invisible to any test that reads the drawing commands. One was the pane's half-pixel seam filler — deliberately a hair oversized so rounding between adjacent blocks cannot leave a background-coloured seam, but the last block of each row and column has no neighbour to cover and spent its half pixel outside the pane. The other was not an overflow but a **panic**: `draw_help` clamped its column split into `left..=right`, and a sheet narrower than its own two margins inverts them, which `f32::clamp` answers by panicking rather than by clamping. The third fault is the one worth carrying: **the status line had never been drawn, at any window size, since it was written.** It was stacked under the coordinate reading behind `line_h + status_h <= info.h`, and that guard is never true — two rows want 2.55x the font at this face and `Layout` gives the info band at most 2.28x at every window height from 40 to 2160, coming closest 1.41px short at h=309 — so every "Picked #89B4FA" and "Ruler cleared" the app has ever produced went nowhere. No containment test can see that, structurally: a run that is never emitted is trivially inside every band it might have left. What pointed at it was a mutation that *survived*, and the general lesson is that a survivor does not always mean a missing test — sometimes it means the code it broke is unreachable. The app's own doc comment had been saying so all along ("the status line **beside** it"), which is cheaper evidence than a sweep; the status now shares the reading's row, right-aligned against the colour swatch and held to its own half of it. The fourth was the header's title/reading split, where two independent fractions (`w * 0.5` and `w * 0.45`) run through each other on a band that is narrow but *tall*, since the reading's font follows the band's height and the window's, and neither shrinks when the width does. Three inert clamps were removed and replaced with proofs at the line, including a pair that cancelled each other — a legibility floor that could not bite, and a cap that existed only to undo the floor's one real effect. The final sweep left five survivors and not one was a missing test in the ordinary sense -- each was a test aimed at a fault of the wrong *kind*, which is the lesson the app ends on. Three were owned by the sheet's containment test and could never have been caught by it. A row drawn in a strip too short for its own line does not leave the sheet: the type is sized *from* the strip, so the overhang is at most eight points and the sheet's own title and footer margins absorb it -- the rows land on **each other**, and two runs on top of one another are both still inside the panel and both unreadable, so the assertion had to be about overlap rather than escape. The column split taken from the panel's width instead of the column's moves the keys and the description **together**, so they stay consistent with each other and the pair simply slides into the left margin -- correct against the panel, wrong against the column, and the fix was to measure runs against the box that actually has the claim on them. And `push_text`'s empty-box refusal had become unreachable from any window size *because* the centring rewrite succeeded: every column is now measured from a rectangle some earlier refusal already emptied, so the arm is real but no layout can reach it. Widening the sweep until it manufactured a zero limit would have been testing the layout in order to test the helper; it is tested on the helper instead, with the both-halves case that stops an emptied `push_text` from passing a test named for guarding it. The other two survivors were **genuine equivalent mutants**, and the sweep is what proved it. The sheet's `inner.is_empty()` early return is a fast path in front of four `intersect`s that each already refuse a negative-width box -- deleting it changes not one command in the frame -- and the comment beside it had called it "the bound", which is worse than no comment, because a reader who believed it would feel free to loosen the guards that actually hold. The per-row `intersect` is inert for a different reason: `room` is measured down from the sheet's bottom, so no row can hang off it. That is the grade-C shape below, so it did not get a comment recording the coincidence -- it got a test that fails the moment an edit to `room` or `top` makes the cut load-bearing, which is the point at which someone should be told. Window-wiring baseline 88 -> 87.
- [x] Dictionary & Thesaurus (apps/dictionary: **windowed** — word lookup with definitions, pronunciation, etymology, synonyms, antonyms and clickable cross-references; 30 entries; ranked search across words, definitions, examples, synonyms, antonyms and related words; history, favourites, featured word; 5 screens, 74 tests). Wiring it found twelve faults on top of the missing window. (1) `main` built a `DictionaryApp`, dropped it and exited: no definition ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **There was no pointer input at all** — not a partial hit test, no hit test: the tab bar, every result row, every history and favourites row, the favourite button and the “[Enter] View full entry” prompt were painted and inert. (3) The status bar advertised `[/] Search` while the handler read `"/" | "f" if ctrl` — a guard on a `|` pattern applies to *both* alternatives, so plain `/` fell through to `_ => {}` and only Ctrl+/ worked. (4) `Down` walked the selection to `len - 1` while the renderer drew `.take(visible)` rows from index zero and nothing ever scrolled, so on any search with more hits than fit **the selection left the screen and `Enter` opened a word the reader could not see**. (5) `Up`/`Down` were handled for `Screen::History` and never for `Screen::Favorites`, so `favorites_scroll` was frozen at 0 and **`Enter` on the favourites screen always opened the first favourite**, whichever row you thought you were on. (6) `detail_scroll` was written by `Up`/`Down` and read by nothing, and the sections were guarded by `dy + 20.0 < y + h`, so on a long entry **the etymology was silently dropped and no key could bring it back**. (7) The layout was a constant: `width`/`height` were set to 800x600 in `new` and never assigned again, there was no resize path and `render` took no size — five 110px tabs needed 574px, the status bar drew its right-hand hint at `width - 250` (a negative x below 250px wide), and the word-of-the-day card was 100px tall with its prompt drawn at `y + 152`, outside it. (8) `word_of_day_index` was initialised to `0` and never assigned, so **the word of the day was permanently the first entry**; there is no wall clock reachable from a window yet (`known-issues.md` `C-NO-CALENDAR-CLOCK-FOR-APPS`), so it is now a *featured* word the reader steps through, which is what the code can honestly do. (9) A blanket `#![allow(dead_code)]` on line 1 was hiding `is_favorite`, `PartOfSpeech::short` and `DictEntry::related` — all written, none reached. (10) Escape in the search box cleared `search_active` while leaving `screen == Search`, and the typing branch was gated on both, so **the keyboard did nothing until Escape was pressed a second time**. (11) The doc claimed “200+ common words” over a list of fifteen. (12) Making the cross-reference chips clickable exposed the data behind them: **of the 265 words the entries named in their `related` lists, exactly one was a word this dictionary has**, so the entry screen's only navigation feature got you out of one entry in thirty. The search was also quadratic for no reason — five sequential passes over the whole dictionary, each asking `results.contains(&i)` about every entry it looked at, to produce an order one pass and a stable sort produce exactly; doing it in one pass made room for the two fields the old search never read, antonyms and `related`. The word list is now thirty entries, seven of them added because `PartOfSpeech` has ten variants and the old list used three, and the `related` lists name siblings on purpose (57 live links, every entry reaching at least one other). Twelve deliberate mutations of the production code check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/dictionary/mutate.py`); two survived the first sweep and both were real faults rather than weak tests — a search field whose hit box answered `Go(Search)` on the only screen it is drawn on, so every click that could reach it was a no-op, and a clip with nothing to trim because the list stopped on an exact row boundary. The list now draws one row into the strip below the last whole one, so a list continuing past the fold looks like it does, and the clip cuts that row's hit box off at the pane's edge so the half never drawn cannot be clicked. Window-wiring baseline 92 -> 91.
- [x] Podcast Manager (apps/podcast: RSS subscription management, episode listing with playback simulation (speed 0.5x-3x), download queue with progress, playlists/up-next queue, 12 category types, per-episode notes and timestamp bookmarks, OPML import/export, listening statistics, playback history, sidebar+episode list+now-playing UI, 75+ tests)
- [x] Disk Imager (apps/diskimager: create/write disk images (raw/ISO/compressed), ISO 9660 filesystem browsing with file tree, checksum verification (SHA-256/SHA-1/MD5), drive detection with type classification, write verification, bootable USB detection (Legacy/UEFI/Hybrid), progress tracking with speed/ETA, recent images, safety confirm dialog, 112 tests)
- [x] Internet Radio Player (apps/radio: streaming internet radio with 20 preset stations across 15 genres, station browser with search/filter, favorites management, playback controls with volume/mute, sleep timer with auto-shutoff, recording simulation with file naming, 8-band spectrum visualization, station metadata display, genre-based browsing, 50+ tests)
- [x] Whiteboard Drawing App (apps/whiteboard: freehand pen/brush drawing, shape tools (line/rectangle/ellipse/arrow), text annotations, 12-color palette, 5 stroke widths, fill toggle, layer management (add/delete/toggle visibility), undo/redo with full action history, eraser tool with proximity detection, canvas pan (middle-click) and zoom (scroll wheel), grid overlay, keyboard shortcuts for all tools, coordinate display, 40+ tests)
- [x] Kanban Board (apps/kanban: multiple boards with column management (Backlog/Todo/In Progress/Review/Done), cards with title/description/labels/priority/due date/assignee/checklists/comments, WIP limits per column with visual warnings, filtering by label/priority/assignee/search, sorting by priority/due date/creation/title, swimlanes, card archive/restore, board statistics with bar charts, JSON import/export, keyboard navigation, 129 tests)
  - [x] `[E]` **A card can be chosen, and everything modelled has a key** (2026-09-27) -- nothing in the running program ever chose a card (only the tests set one), so Enter, P, M, B, Ctrl+D and Ctrl+A did nothing in the window. Up/Down choose a card and the board follows it, Left/Right choose the level card in the next column, and the chosen column is marked. The open card edits its title and description (E, D -- from what is there), takes a comment signed by the login name (C) and a checklist item (L), and ticks one (Tab, Space). The archive restores a card to the column it came from (kept in the boards file, format 2; format 1 still reads); columns rename (R), collapse (Z), sort by the next order (Shift+T) and are removed when empty (Shift+Delete); the board list makes a board (N) and shows its cursor; the filter bar sets a priority, an assignee and a label (Ctrl+P, Ctrl+U, Ctrl+L). Swimlanes are still kept and never drawn.
- [x] World Clock (apps/worldclock: **windowed** — `app::launch`, `Frame` hit boxes, `Layout` from the live window size, and a once-a-second tick. Five things a world clock could not do before it had a pointer and a clock: (1) **it told the time.** The instant was the literal `1_721_044_800`, so every city read noon on 15 July 2024 forever; it now comes from the machine's clock, with the user's deliberate shift held in a second field so the tick cannot silently discard it. (2) **it drew a frame** — `main` built the struct and dropped it. (3) **it took a click** — six header buttons, thirty picker rows and every card were drawn and inert, and the pin/home glyphs appeared only once *already* set, which is a control that can be turned off and never on; all are targets now, joined by a remove ✕. (4) **it scrolled** — `scroll_offset` was read by both views and assigned by nothing, and `picker_scroll` only ever assigned zero, so 21 of the 30 cities were reachable only by typing a search. (5) **it fits its window** — list columns were the constants 16/200/380/520/620/740, off the right edge at 800px and huddled left at 1900px; they are fractions now, the button run slides then scales, and the UTC readout is what gets dropped, since it restates each card's own offset line while the buttons are the only pointer route to another view. 30 cities across all time zones, grid/list views, analog/digital display, day/night indicators, timezone picker with search, 12h/24h toggle, 85 tests)
- [x] Minesweeper (apps/minesweeper: **windowed** — beginner, intermediate and expert boards laid out from whatever window they are given, first-click-safe mine placement, flood-fill reveal, flags and chording from the keyboard or from three mouse buttons, a mine counter, an `MM:SS` clock that counts time rather than wakings, and two header chips that deal a fresh board and step the level; 122 tests). Wiring it found nine faults on top of the missing window, and the first of them is the whole program. (1) **`main` ran a scripted game and printed it.** It dealt a board, applied a fixed list of moves and wrote the result to standard output — the app drew a board and answered clicks for its whole life without ever depending on the window system, so there was nothing for a user to click, and there was no `Event::` of any kind anywhere in the file. (2) **Every installation dealt the same board.** The seed was the literal `42`, directly contradicting the doc comment beside it, and `restart` used `seed + 1` — which is not a fresh draw but one step along a single stream, so every board after the first was a fixed function of the first. The opening seed comes from `seed_from_system` now and each board after it from `rng.next_u64()`. (3) **The clock counted its own wakings.** `tick()` took no argument and added exactly one second per call, so the timer reported how often the window happened to wake the app — a fact about the window, not about the game (`known-issues.md` lesson 45). It takes `elapsed_ms` now and stores milliseconds, the displayed seconds being computed rather than kept as a second copy that can disagree. (4) **The board decided the size of the window.** `window_width()` told the window what to be, which is backwards for a program that has to live in a window the user drags; there is a `Layout` derived from the live window size every frame and stored on nothing. (5) **Two counters were kept in step by hand, and drifted.** `revealed_count` and `flags_placed` were incremented at each call site, and the losing click counted the mine it uncovered while `reveal_all_mines` uncovered every *other* mine and counted none — so after a loss the tally was short by the mines on the board, and `is_cleared` was a `>=` against that tally. Both are derived from the cells now, and so is `is_cleared`. (6) **`place_mines` was an unbounded loop**: rejection sampling that redraws until it lands on a free cell, which has no bound on a board whose free cells have nearly run out. It shuffles the legal candidates and takes from the front. (7) **The crate did not pass the lane clippy gate at all** — 71 errors, which six blanket `#![allow]`s did not cover; it is rewritten to pass it, with `Step`/`Dir` enums in place of `isize` casts and no raw indexing or raw integer arithmetic anywhere. (8) **A flag was refused before the first click**, silently, which is the first thing a cautious player does with a corner they mean to come back to. (9) **A board could be painted outside the window it belongs to**: a one-pixel floor on the cell size drew a nine-cell row nine pixels wide in a one-pixel window. A window with no room for a board now draws none, rather than drawing a wrong one. 137 deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/minesweeper/mutate.py`). Nothing survived, but ten mutants were caught by a test other than the one that claims the ground, and that verdict — *caught, but not by its owner* — is the one this app is worth recording for, because on the first pass **I only looked for survivors and skips and did not read it at all**. It sorted into three piles, none of them "the sweep was wrong". **Three were guards standing behind duplicates of themselves and all three are deleted** (`known-issues.md` lesson 51): `chord` opened with `status != Playing`, whose `Ready` third is refused by the very next line (a `Ready` board has no revealed cell on it, so "this cell is not a revealed number" already covers it); `flood_reveal` re-checked `|| cell.is_mine`, which no mine can reach, the seed being a cell `reveal` has already found safe and every other cell pushed being the neighbour of a cell whose count is *nought* and therefore safe by the definition of that count; and `chord` broke out of its loop once the game ended, where `reveal` opens with `is_over` and so returns `Ignored` without touching a cell — an optimisation wearing a rule's clothing, and the sweep could not tell the two apart, which is what being inert means. **Six were blind tests.** Three chord tests were refused by a guard standing *in front of* the one they aimed at and so never reached their subject; the chip test was true by construction, chips being placed by subtracting their own width from the right edge, so they cannot overlap or leave the header at any width; and the footer test read the strings without ever reading where they were, so a footer laid out in a heap passed it. The remaining pile is the new lesson. **`known-issues.md` lesson 54 — a fixture in which two different quantities happen to be equal cannot tell them apart** — was written from three instances found here, all in tests that read as obviously adequate. The index test ran on a 9x9 board, where `rows()` and `cols()` are the same number, so `row * cols + col` and `row * rows + col` agree everywhere; it uses Expert now (30x16) and asserts the two are different before relying on it. The clock test ticked 250, 250 and 2500 ms and asserted 3000 — and one second per call, three calls, is also 3000, so the right rule and the wrong one gave the same answer; four ticks summing to 2825 now disagree with 4000 in the milliseconds and, floored, in the seconds. And the click test aimed at cell 5,3, which on this board straddles the diagonal, so reading a click as `hit_test(y, x)` reflected the point straight back into the cell it came from: sixty-odd other tests failed on that mutation and the one test whose whole subject is *where a click lands* did not. It clicks four cells spread across the board now, and no single reflection fixes four. Two more were `known-issues.md` lesson 52 — a test built out of the thing it is testing. `the_board_keeps_its_share_of_a_window_that_is_getting_shorter` measured the board's height against `BOARD_SHARE`, the constant that sets it, so the share could be dropped from 0.55 to 0.15 and the test moved with it; the claim is written out as a literal half now, which is what makes it a claim. That fix alone did not work, and why is worth more than the fix: the rewritten test *still* passed the mutant, because **`BOARD_SHARE` does not set the board's height at all** — the board gets whatever the header and footer leave, and the share only decides the budget those two bands must fit inside before one of them is dropped. It therefore has no effect whatever on a window tall enough for both, and every height the test used was such a window, so the two versions laid out identically and an exact assertion over five sizes was blind. The heights that matter are 60 to 90 pixels, where the real share drops a band and the mutant keeps it and the board falls from 56 pixels to 14. **A constant that only takes effect at the edge of a range can only be tested at that edge**, and a fixture chosen from comfortable, realistic window sizes is guaranteed to miss it — which is the same failure as lesson 54 seen from the other side: there the fixture's numbers made two rules agree, here they kept the rule from applying. And `the_box_a_cell_records_is_the_box_it_was_painted_in` compares `probe::rect_of` against `cell_rect` — but `rect_of` returns the box the drawing pass got *from* `cell_rect`, so both sides move together and a column read as a row is invisible to it. That is not a fault in the test: agreement between hit box and ink is a real property and belongs there. *Where the grid puts a cell* is a different claim and now has its own test, which asserts that moving along a row goes across and moving down a column goes down. The same shape appeared once more in the layout: `no_cell_is_ever_drawn_outside_the_window` cannot see a board slid up over the header, because a board over the header is still inside the window — it was caught only by two chip tests, and they failed because the board had buried the chips' hit boxes, which is a symptom rather than the rule. Staying inside the band now has its own test alongside staying inside the window. One survivor was the harness's own fault rather than the suite's: deleting an element from `NEIGHBOURS` does not compile, `[(Step, Step); 8]` being a fixed-size array, so it was replaced by a substitution that turns the bottom-right neighbour into the cell itself. The sweep also produced **`known-issues.md` lesson 55 — an unbounded loop in a test helper turns every fault it depends on into the same silence** — which is a recurrence rather than a discovery, and that is the point of it. Three tests walked the cursor with `while a.cursor() != (r, c) { … }`, and *five* separate mutations of the movement code — a step that stays put, an `Up` that goes down, a `Left` that goes up, a move that reports it moved without moving, and a move that uncovers as well as moving — every one produced the identical verdict: a 240-second hang. Five faults, one non-answer, twenty minutes of wall clock to learn nothing, and the sweep stayed green throughout, because a program that hangs really is a program that is wrong and the harness scores a hang as a catch. What is lost is invisible in the summary: a mutation sweep's whole product is the mapping from a fault to the test that owns it, and a hang maps every fault to the same place. `apps/maze` had already hit this once and the finding went into its roadmap entry without being promoted to a lesson, which is part of why it reappeared in a suite written afterwards. The walk is one bounded helper now — `rows + cols + 8` steps, then a panic naming the cursor's actual position — taking the step as a closure so the keyboard test can walk with arrow keys through the whole event path while the others walk with `apply`, without the loop, or the bound, being written twice. And one mutation had to be chosen with care rather than written the obvious way — making `flood_reveal`'s "already uncovered" test `if false` makes the flood run forever, and a hanging binary scores as *caught* while teaching nothing, so the mutation flips the test to `== CellState::Revealed`, which makes the flood wash over the flags and is a wrong answer a player would see. Window-wiring baseline 84 -> 83.

- [x] Habit Tracker (apps/habits: daily/weekly habit tracking with check-in toggles, 9 categories with icons, streak tracking (current/best), completion rates (7d/30d/all-time), 364-day contribution heatmap, archive/restore, statistics dashboard, category filtering, 5 sample habits with simulated history, 116 tests)
- [x] Mind Map (apps/mindmap: radial node layout with central root, add child/sibling/edit/delete subtree, expand/collapse branches, zoom/pan navigation, node coloring with 8 colors, search with highlighting, undo/redo, reparent nodes, auto-layout, keyboard-driven interaction, 70+ tests)
- [x] Personal Finance (apps/finance: budget tracking across 12 expense categories, 5 screens (dashboard/transactions/budgets/accounts/reports), multiple account types, transaction entry with amount/category/date, budget progress bars, expense breakdown chart, savings rate calculation, category filtering, search, 60+ tests) — *"transaction entry" was not true until 2026-09-25: nothing could enter anything and nothing was kept; see `known-issues.md` → `TD-C-FINANCE-IS-A-VIEWER-OVER-SAMPLE-DATA`, fixed that day (forms, a kept ledger, the clock's today, 105 tests)*
- [x] Chess (apps/chess: **windowed** -- a board sized square from whatever window it is given and centred in what the header and the panel leave, full legal move generation with castling, en passant and promotion, an opponent whose alpha-beta search runs on a tick so that "Black is thinking" is a state a frame can be drawn in, rank and file labels and an information panel that are each dropped whole when the window cannot pay for them, a New game button, and a cursor you can walk with the arrows or put anywhere with one click; 145 tests). Wiring it found eleven faults. (1) **`main` was `let _app = ChessApp::new();`** -- it built the opening position, dropped it and exited, so no piece ever reached a screen and no click ever arrived at the game that reads clicks. (2) **`render(&self) -> Vec<RenderCommand>` took no width and no height at all** and painted the same 852x612 picture into every window there was. (3) **The board was drawn from `SQUARE_SIZE = 64.0` and two offset constants, and the click was resolved by `square_at`, a free function of the same three** -- so the two agreed with each other in every window and with the screen in exactly one. There is no inverse of the mapping now: the drawing pass records a `Target::Square(row, col)` per square and `handle_mouse` asks `hit_test`, so a board painted wrongly cannot be clicked rightly. The row flip is checked against the ink -- White's back rank must be *below* Black's -- because it is the one part of the mapping a reader cannot verify by inspection and the part every "is it clickable" test passes regardless of. (4) **The search ran inside the click handler.** `click_square` called `ai_turn`, so an alpha-beta search to depth 3 ran before the click returned and no frame was drawn while it did; `thinking` was a state no player could ever see. It runs on `Event::Tick` now, and the tick arm is answered by whether a search actually ran rather than by a second copy of the "is it Black's turn" test (lesson 92). (5) **Nothing in the panel was clickable**, so New game was `Ctrl+N` and nothing else -- keyboard-only at exactly the moment a player reaches for the mouse, which is when the game has just ended. (6) **The move list grew downward without a bound** and painted off the bottom of the window after about thirty moves. The rows are counted from the room the panel has, and it shows the last pairs rather than the first, which is the end a player watching a game is looking at. (7) **Eleven crate-level `#![allow]`s**, and beneath them 68 unchecked integer operations and 14 array indexings -- the crate had never been built with the lane gate on. Every one is fixed structurally: `Board::get`/`set` go through `slice::get`, so "off the board" is an answer the generators need rather than a guard bolted in front of an index; `Pos::offset` and `Pos::ray` replace five hand-written `r += dr; c += dc` loops that each carried their own copy of the bounds test. (8) **`b'a' + col as u8` was written at five sites**, each of them able to name a square that does not exist -- `{` for the file after h. `file_char` and `rank_char` return `Option<char>`, and the five sites are one. (9) **`piece_square_value` computed an index and then asked whether it was past the end**; `Pos::index` returns `Option<usize>` now, so the question is asked where it can be answered. (10) **`ai_choose_move` asked `moves.is_empty()` and then indexed `moves[0]`** -- one `first()?` answers both, and the copy no test could reach is gone (lesson 92). (11) **`MoveRecord` was recorded and never read**: the struct's `mv` and `captured` fields had no reader anywhere, which `dead_code` had been switched off to permit. Writing the tests found a twelfth: **`Layout::solve` measured "Arrows/Enter: Navigate" to decide whether the panel was worth drawing while the panel drew "Arrows/Enter: Move"** -- a column sized for a line it does not contain. The strings are named constants now, measured and drawn from one place. The 73-row mutation sweep found two more that the tests alone had not. (13) **`click_square`'s guard read `game_result != Ongoing || self.thinking || side_to_move != White`, and the middle clause was the third copy of the last one** -- `update_game_state` derives `thinking` as `result == Ongoing && side_to_move == Black`, so past the first clause the two say the same thing. Deleting either left every test passing, which is what lesson 92 predicts of a condition written twice. (14) **`minimax`'s leaf negated its evaluation when `!maximizing`** while the mate scores three lines below it are White-relative in both arms -- two scales compared against each other. It had never mattered because the only caller searches `AI_DEPTH - 1` = 2 ply from a maximising root, so every leaf it reaches is a maximising one; raising the depth by one would have started mixing them. The leaf returns `evaluate(board)` now, and a test asserts that the score does not depend on which side is choosing. Window-wiring baseline 61 -> 60.
- [x] Sudoku (apps/sudoku: **windowed** — a 9x9 board laid out from whatever window it is given, a backtracking generator that guarantees exactly one answer at three difficulties, pencil marks, undo/redo capped at 500 moves, five hints, conflict highlighting, an `MM:SS` clock that counts time rather than wakings, per-difficulty statistics, and a keypad and a row of header chips that make the game playable with the pointer alone; 155 tests). Wiring it found eleven faults on top of the missing window, and the first two are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = SudokuApp::new();`** — it generated a puzzle, dropped it and exited, so the most expensive thing this program does was done once, for nobody, and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **The layout was a picture of a window rather than a window.** `CELL_SIZE` was 52 pixels, the grid origin was a constant, and `render` computed `total_width`/`total_height` *from the cell size* — the drawing pass told the window what size to be, which is backwards for a program that has to live in a window the user drags. `Layout` is derived from the live window size every frame and stored on nothing. (3) **Nothing but a cell was clickable, and clicking a cell was all a mouse could do**: no way to enter a digit, take a hint, toggle notes, undo, pause or start a new game without a keyboard. That is not a playable program; it is a keyboard program with a picture attached. There is a keypad band and a row of chips now, every one of them a hit box recorded by the pass that draws it. (4) **The clock counted its wake-ups, not time.** `handle_tick` ignored its `elapsed_ms` and did `elapsed_secs += 1` — and nothing generated ticks at all, so the header read `00:00` for the length of every game (`known-issues.md` lesson 45, for the third time in this campaign). It stores milliseconds now, the displayed seconds being computed rather than kept as a second copy that can disagree. And **the test agreed with the bug**: it ticked `elapsed_ms: 1000` and asserted one second, which is the one input on which counting wake-ups and counting time give the same answer. (5) **`given` meant two different things** — the puzzle's own clues, *and* a lock set on any cell a hint filled in — so `given_count` grew as hints were spent, and "how many clues does this puzzle have" and "which cells may I not edit" had one answer between them. They are an `Origin` and a derived predicate now. (6) **`hints_remaining` was a counter maintained in three places**: decremented by `use_hint`, incremented by `undo`, decremented again by `redo`. Drop the oldest entry off the capped undo stack and the three stop agreeing — and `redo`'s decrement was unguarded besides, so it could underflow. It is derived from the cells. (7) **A redo could not win the game.** `check_completion` was called from `input_digit` and from `use_hint` but not from `redo`, so redoing the last digit left a finished grid sitting at `Playing` for ever. (8) **A win or a pause locked out the buttons that undo them**: `handle_mouse` returned unless the status was `Playing`, and the pause guard in `handle_key` stood in front of `F2` — so a paused game could not be restarted and a won one could not be clicked at all. (9) **The pause hid nothing.** The board stayed on screen with "Paused" printed over it, so the only things paused were the clock and the keyboard, and not the part a player pauses in order to stop looking at. (10) **The bounds check on a square was a wrap.** `at`, `put`, `cell` and `write` all reached the array through `idx`, which is row-major, and then asked `get` whether the index was in range — but a column past the ninth *is* in range; it is the next row's cell. `at(grid, 0, 9)` handed back `(1, 0)` while its doc comment promised `0`, and only the last row's overflow was ever caught. (11) **A label could start above the box it labelled**: `centred_in` clamped the horizontal offset and left the vertical one alone, so a line taller than its box centred to a *negative* offset — the header's 22.5pt heading in a 28pt half-band began six tenths of a pixel above the top of the window, and the screen edge took the difference. On top of all that the crate carried `#![allow(dead_code)]` and eight more crate-wide allows and did not pass the lane's clippy gate at all; all nine are gone. 197 deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/sudoku/mutate.py`). **The first sweep caught 161, left 15 survivors, reported 20 caught by a test other than the one that claims the ground — the largest wrong-owner pile this campaign has produced — and skipped one.** Every one of the fifteen was a real hole. They sorted into four piles, none of them "the sweep was wrong". **Three survivors were faults in the program rather than in the suite: guards standing behind duplicates of themselves, all three deleted** (`known-issues.md` lesson 51). `count_solutions_inner` opened with `found >= limit` and also ended its loop with `total >= limit`; either alone bounds the search, so breaking one changed no answer — the loop keeps the bound, and the one case the entry guard really decided, a limit of *none*, is answered at the door in `count_solutions`. `move_selection` clamped to the last row and column, and `select` already refuses a row or column off the board — and refusing leaves the selection where it was, which is exactly what the clamp produced. And `use_hint` tested `cell.fixed() || cell.value == solution`, where a clue's value *is* the answer it was cut from, so the second half refuses clues by itself; `a_clue_holds_the_answer_it_came_from` is the new test that keeps that reasoning honest, walking a generated puzzle and asserting it of every clue. **Nine tests were passing for the wrong reason, and three new lessons came out of them.** **`known-issues.md` lesson 58 — a witness that more than one rule explains tests only the strongest of them** — was written from the candidates test, which struck out a digit in the row, one in the column and one in the box for cell `(0, 0)` and asserted each was struck. Its row and column witnesses were `(0, 1)` and `(1, 0)`, which genuinely are in that row and that column — and are also both inside cell `(0, 0)`'s own box, so the box scan alone did all three jobs, and deleting the row scan and deleting the column scan both survived while the messages `"the row was ignored"` and `"the column was ignored"` had never once been about the row or the column. Sudoku's three rules overlap by construction at the corner of a box and the fixture sat in the overlap; the witnesses are `(0, 5)` and `(5, 0)` now — same row, same column, different box. **`known-issues.md` lesson 59 — a fixture with no asymmetry cannot notice that two things were swapped** — came from three of them at once. The pencil-mark test set *all nine* marks and asserted nine texts in nine distinct places, which is true of `nrow = slot / 3, ncol = slot % 3` and equally true of it transposed, and true as well of a loop that draws the marks which are *not* set, there being none left to draw; three marks — 1, 2 and 4 — answer both questions, because "2 is to the right of 1" and "4 is below 1" are statements a transposition breaks and "only three texts were drawn" is one an unfiltered loop breaks. The difficulty cycle asserted that three steps come home and that three distinct labels are seen, both of which hold of the cycle running backwards; the three steps are named individually now. And the history-cap test made 502 changes by toggling one mark on and off, so every entry was identical and dropping from the back looked exactly like dropping from the front. Making that count *odd* fixed the arithmetic and left the symmetry exactly where it was — which the second sweep then said out loud, below. What the test actually needed was two changes that *differ*: one toggle of mark 1, then a full history's worth of toggles of mark 2, so that mark 1's toggle is the single entry which has to fall off the end. Undo the whole history afterwards and mark 1 is still set, where a history that forgot its *newest* would have rewound all the way to bare. **`known-issues.md` lesson 57 — "is it drawn?" is not "is it drawn *there*?"** — came from the band test, whose name promised an order and whose twelve assertions were all `shows(...)` predicates with not one coordinate among them: the sweep drew the footer across the top of the window and slid the keypad underneath it, and every assertion still held, because a band in the wrong place is still a band that is there. Its replacement asserts that the first band starts at the top, the last ends at the bottom, every band lies inside the window horizontally, and each begins at or below the end of the band named before it. The first draft of that asserted the bands *abut* and failed honestly on a six-pixel gap — which is the assertion telling you what the layout really promises rather than what you assumed, and is worth as much as the fix. **Three more were blind in the plainer way.** `a_clue_cannot_be_written_over` wrote a 4 into a clue that already held a 4, so the write was refused as a no-op repaint and never as a clue. The paused-game test never selected an empty square, so "a paused game accepted a digit" was really the clue guard answering. And the won-game test was named after the difficulty chip but sent `SetDifficulty`, which is not what that chip sends — it sends `CycleDifficulty`. **And one was `known-issues.md` lesson 54's shape exactly**: the footer test won a single game, so "Solved 1" and "Easy 1" were the same number and a footer that read the total off the level in play passed it; it wins one Easy game and one Medium game now. **The fourth pile is one line of the program that had no test at all.** `put(grid, row, col, 0)` — the undo of a failed guess, the entire content of the word *backtracking* — was dead in the suite, because every fixture was filled by first guesses alone and a solver that never takes a guess back solves all of them. A counter-example had to be searched for: an offline replica of the solver drew three thousand random hole-sets, kept only those with exactly one answer on which the restore-less mutant *fails outright* rather than merely finishing differently, then minimised greedily. `the_solver_takes_back_a_guess_that_led_nowhere` carries the result — fourteen holes, three undos, one answer — and asserts the uniqueness before it asserts anything else, so the day the fixture stops being a fixture it says so instead of quietly weakening. **The second sweep, run against the repaired suite, caught 186, left 2 survivors and reported 9 caught by a test other than the one claiming the ground** — which is the argument for re-sweeping rather than trusting a repair: eleven of the fixes made after the first sweep were themselves worth checking, and eleven of them needed more work. **Both survivors were the first sweep's own lessons re-applied to the first sweep's own repairs.** The limit-of-none guard moved to the door of `count_solutions` had no test that could reach it, because the only grid it was ever asked of was unfinished — and on an unfinished grid the loop's `total >= limit` is already true before the first digit is tried, so nought comes back whether the door is guarded or not: lesson 58's one-witness-two-rules, found in the very suite the lesson was written from, and fixed by asking a *finished* grid. And the history cap is the paragraph above: odd was not the same as *different*. **The nine wrong-owner verdicts had four causes.** Splitting the band test in two left six expectations still naming a test that no longer makes the claim they check; they point at `the_bands_stack_down_the_window_in_the_order_they_are_named` and `the_keypad_keys_sit_in_a_row_and_do_not_overlap` now, and the *drop order* itself — reversing `BAND_DROP_ORDER` — was being scored against a bystander until it was pointed at the ladder test whose whole subject it is. `the_board_keeps_its_share_of_every_window` asserted `board.h >= h * BOARD_SHARE`, a test built out of the constant it tests, so zeroing that constant zeroed the assertion with it and the mutant passed (`known-issues.md` lesson 52); it says `0.5` in its own hand now. The band-drop ladder is only *reachable* in a narrow range of heights — above about 136 pixels the three bands fit in any budget and below about 90 they fit in none — and the layout fixture's nine window sizes all sat outside it, so a constant that only takes effect at the edge of a range was never tested at that edge; a tenth size, 600x120, sits inside it. Likewise the header-chip test only ever met a header wide enough for three chips, where the clamp holding them on at the left is unreachable, so a narrow-header leg was added that asserts containment alone — in a header that narrow the chips genuinely do pile up, and claiming otherwise would be a false test rather than a strong one. Last, `a_click_is_read_against_the_size_the_frame_was_drawn_at` called `resize` directly, which nothing outside the tests does, and so could not see `render` failing to remember the size it had just drawn at; it goes through `render` now, the way a window does. **The third sweep is clean: 197 of 197 caught, every one by the test that claims the ground, no survivors and no skips.** Seven of the 197 are caught by a hang or a crash rather than by a named assertion — an honest catch, and the weakest kind, since a mutant that loops for ever tells you only that something noticed. Window-wiring baseline 83 -> 82.
- [x] Tetris (apps/tetris: **windowed** — `app::launch`, `Frame` hit boxes, `Layout` from the live window size, a 50 ms tick that advances gravity, lock delay and the clock by the `elapsed_ms` it is handed; every CONTROLS row is the button it names and the HOLD box and the message box are clickable too, so the pointer reaches every action the keyboard does. 10x20 playfield, 7 standard tetrominoes with SRS rotation and wall kicks, 7-bag randomizer, hold piece, next preview, line clearing with scoring, ghost piece, level progression, T-spin detection, 138 tests. Four faults fixed on the way in: a multi-line clear left one cleared row behind as a permanent solid line; the layout decided the window size instead of the reverse; nothing at all was clickable; and the piece fell once per *tick* rather than once per gravity interval)
- [x] Snake (apps/snake: **windowed** — a 20x20 board fitted to the live window every frame, three difficulties, a wrap switch that turns the walls into doorways, bonus food that expires, a streak bonus for eating without dawdling, a stats panel as wide as what is written in it, and a footer of five switches; 111 tests). Wiring it found twelve faults, the missing window among them. (1) **`main` was `let _app = SnakeApp::new();`** — it built the board, placed the first food and dropped the lot, so no frame ever reached a screen and no key ever arrived. (2) **The program chose its own window and then drew in absolute pixels inside it.** `render` took no size; `window_width()` returned `PADDING * 3 + grid_width() + STATS_PANEL_WIDTH` off a `CELL_SIZE` of 24, and every draw call was placed against those constants. A window the user made smaller did not shrink the board, it cut it off. `Layout` splits the window into header, body and footer from its height, `Board` fits 20x20 square squares into whatever the body has left, and the squares are recomputed every frame. (3) **The pointer did nothing whatsoever.** `handle_event` matched `Event::Key` and `Event::Tick` and had no mouse arm at all, so pause, restart, the wrap switch and the three difficulties were keyboard-only — and on the game-over screen, which is where a new player arrives within a minute, there was no way to start again except to know that Enter does it. The footer carries those five switches as controls now, and clicking a square of the board steers the snake towards it: the axis it *can* turn on, since the axis it is travelling along is either where it is already going or a reversal. (4) **`STATS_PANEL_WIDTH = 180.0` whatever was in it, and every string carried `max_width: None`.** A small window gave 180 pixels to six short numbers and the board went without; a long number had nothing to stop it running through the label beside it. The panel is measured from its own widest row now, capped at a third of the body, and every string is told where to stop. (5) **The clock was never wound.** `App::tick_interval` defaults to `None`, so the game would have sat still even with a window; `handle_tick` returned a `bool` that the caller discarded, so a moved snake never asked for a repaint. It asks for a tick at the fastest difficulty's rate and answers `Response::Idle` for a tick that moved nothing. (6) **A stall made the snake teleport.** `handle_tick` banked `elapsed_ms` and subtracted *one* interval per call, so a window hidden for ten seconds came back with 9.85 seconds of credit and the snake then moved once per frame until it had spent them — usually into a wall, always without the player having seen it. Catch-up is bounded at two moves and the rest is dropped, because carrying it only moves the stampede to the next frame. (7) **Winning hung the game.** `random_empty_cell` drew a square at random and retried until it found one the snake was not on; with the snake covering all 400 squares — which is what winning *is* — there is no such square and the loop never ends. It is a scan of the free squares now, and a board with none of them is a win. (8) **Bonus food revived a streak that had lapsed.** `eat_normal_food` reset the streak to 1 when more than `STREAK_WINDOW_TICKS` had gone by; `eat_bonus_food` did `streak += 1` with no window check at all, so a bonus eaten after a minute of wandering counted as though it had come straight after the last meal. Both meals go through one `extend_streak` now. (9) **The wrap switch had no way to be operated.** `wrap_mode` was a field with a `match` arm nowhere: no key, no click, no menu. It is `W` and a footer switch. (10) **Choosing a difficulty threw the game away.** Num1/Num2/Num3 set the speed *and* restarted, so there was no way to say "same game, faster" and no way to say "again, same speed". They are separate now. (11) **The crate had never passed its own lint gate.** Ten blanket `#![allow(...)]` sat at the root with `dead_code` among them — which is what let a program whose `main` discarded its own app compile without a word of complaint — and underneath them were `self.snake[0]` indexing and `+=` arithmetic throughout the rules. The allows are gone and the crate builds clean under `-D warnings -D dead_code`. (12) **Nine guards in front of rules that already held** (`known-issues.md` lesson 51), each a line no test could own: `pick`'s emptiness check (`below` answers nought for a bound of nought and the nought-th of nothing is `None`), `Layout::split`'s and `Board::new`'s and `draw_board`'s and `draw_stats`'s and `draw_overlay`'s "is there any room" checks (every width below is floored at nought, and both `fill` and `Frame::hit` drop a rectangle of no size), `draw_overlay`'s second test of a state it had already matched on, `step_once`'s re-check of a reversal `queue_direction` had already refused, `Layout::new`'s two unreachable `.max(0.0)` clamps, and `init_snake`'s three assignments to fields the struct literal two lines above had just set. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/snake/mutate.py` breaks 218 pieces of the program one at a time and every one is caught by the test named for it. The first sweep left thirteen survivors and one mutant that would not compile, and the diagnoses are the interesting part. **A one-square snake is its own tail**, so "a click is measured from the head" had nothing to measure against — and a ring closed onto the *middle* of the snake passes just as well against a collision check that skips the last square, so neither the head/tail rule nor the solid-tail rule was actually held by the suite (lesson 70 twice over). **A snake travelling sideways never reaches the fallback arm** of the click-steering, because the vertical direction is tried first and taken; the "click behind the snake" test had to be run again with a snake travelling up and down before the arm it was written for was entered at all (lesson 67). **Three squares of four hundred is a coincidence, not a check**: the bonus-placement test had a three-square snake, so a `spawn_bonus_food` that had stopped excluding the snake almost always still missed it. It now runs against a snake with nine tenths of the board. **A ceiling is only a ceiling where something reaches it**: the stats panel's "never more than a third of the body" test asked at 420 pixels across, where the panel wanted less than a third anyway, and the test now walks down to 230 and asserts that the ceiling bit somewhere. **The streak window's last move** was untested from either side, and **the lifetime a spawned bonus is given** was untested because the expiry test built its bonus by hand. **A guard whose only observable job is the bank:** the state check at the top of `handle_tick` looked dead, since the catch-up loop asks the state too — but a game paused for *nearly a move* and then unpaused steps on the first millisecond of play without it, which is what the test now says. And the mutant that would not compile was a genuine fragility: `let mut moves = 0;` had its type pinned by nothing but the comparison against `MAX_CATCH_UP_MOVES`, so touching the loop condition turned it into an ambiguous integer. The thirteenth survivor is a new lesson (`known-issues.md` **lesson 71**): the board grows each square's click box by half the gap on every side, and the test for that clicked the middle of a gap and accepted *either* neighbouring square. Grow the box by the whole gap on one side and nothing on the other and it is still the right size, still leaves no gap orphaned, and the test still passes — while every click box has slid a whole gap off its own ink. A hit-box assertion has to name the square, not merely require one; the disjunction is slack, and a box displaced by exactly one gap is the fault that fits inside it (lesson 68 arriving through the hit map). Window-wiring baseline 74 -> 73.
- [x] 2048 (apps/game2048: **windowed** — a 4x4 tile-merging board laid out from whatever window it is given, slide/merge mechanics, an undo history capped at 50 moves, a win at 2048 that can be played on past, a loss declared only when nothing on the board can move, a best score that outlives a new game, arrow/WASD keys, and a direction pad and footer that make the game playable with the pointer alone; 122 tests). Wiring it found eighteen faults on top of the missing window, and the first two are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = Game2048App::new();`** — it dealt two tiles onto a board, dropped it and exited. Nothing was ever drawn and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **The layout was a picture of a window rather than a window.** The board sat at a constant `(40, 140)` with a constant 100-pixel cell, and `render(width, height)` used its two arguments for the background rectangle and nothing else, so the drawing ran to x=528, y=628 whatever window it was in. `Layout` is derived from the live window size every frame now. (3) **Nothing was clickable.** `handle_event` matched `Event::Key` and dropped the rest; there was no mouse code in the file. For a game whose whole interface is "push the pile that way", that is an absence rather than a limitation — there is a direction pad and a footer now, every control a hit box recorded by the pass that paints it. (4) **`Highest:` was a high-water mark over the session, not the highest tile on the board.** `update_highest` could only ever rise and the undo entry did not carry it, so undoing the merge that made a 512 left `Highest: 512` printed over a board whose largest tile was 256. It is derived from the grid. (5) **An undo could not undo a win.** The entry held the grid and the score; `undo` put `Lost` back to `Playing` and left `Won` alone, and `make_move` refuses to move while `Won` — so undoing the winning move left "You Win!" over a board with no 2048 on it and every direction refused. The entry carries the status and the move count now, the latter having been patched with a `saturating_sub(1)` that was right only by coincidence. (6) **Winning on a dead board left a game that could never end.** `make_move` returned the moment it saw 2048, skipping the can-anything-move check, and `continue_after_win` did not check either; continuing now re-asks. (7) **The help sheet was drawn off the window.** It began at `BOARD_Y + 480` = y620, over the last row of tiles, and ran to y820 — past the bottom of the window the rest of the file was drawn for — and its `_width`/`_height` arguments were ignored. (8) **Two pieces of text were centred by guessing**: the banner titles by a hard-coded `-80.0` (which centres exactly one string length, and "You Win!" and "Game Over" both got it), and the tile numbers by `len() * size * 0.3`. Both measure now. (9) **The crate carried `#![allow(dead_code)]` and nine more crate-wide allows** and did not pass the lane's clippy gate at all; all ten are gone, and with them `spawn_tile_at` and `is_full`, which nothing called. Writing the test suite found two more. (10) **The sheet's own closing line was written across another row.** The body was divided by the number of rows, and then "Click anywhere to close" was placed in a band measured back from the sheet's bottom edge — which *is* the last row's band. The line overprinted the last row, hung below the sheet, and in a short window below the window. Every row is sized to its own band now, in one ladder, so no row can be taller than the space it was given. (11) **A title with no room left was still painted.** `label` was handed a maximum width but nothing refused a width of *nothing*, so a header crowded out by the score boxes emitted a text command that drew an empty string. Four more came out of the pre-sweep hardening, three of them guards that could not fail. (12) **The layout shrank its type below what the renderer will draw.** `label` drew text at any positive size, but `guitk` rounds a font size to whole pixels and clamps it to at least one, so below a pixel the renderer silently overruled every caller and drew a whole-pixel line where a fractional one had been placed: in a four-pixel window the help sheet's rows were written over each other, off its foot and off the window. `label` refuses a size the renderer will not honour now — a window with no room for a legible line shows its boxes and its colours and no words. This one is `known-issues.md` **lesson 60**, because the failure mode is text drawn *larger* than asked for, which no `size > 0.0` guard can see and no test that measures the size it *asked* for can notice. (13) **`Layout::banner_button` floored its y at the banner's top** — but the button is at most 0.28 of the banner high and sits 0.08 of it up from the foot, so the floor could never bind: a guard against nothing, and a line no test could ever own (lesson 51). (14) **`apply` asked whether the game was won and then called `continue_after_win`, which asked the same question again**; neither copy could be removed on its own without the other still refusing. `continue_after_win` answers whether there was a win to take up and `apply` uses the answer — one rule, one place. (15) **`resize` floored the window size at a pixel, and so does `Layout::new`**, and every use of the stored size goes through a `Layout`; the first floor could never do anything the second had not already done. 149 deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/game2048/mutate.py`). **The first sweep caught 133, left 4 alive, reported 9 caught by a test other than the one claiming the ground, and skipped 1.** Every one of the fourteen is diagnosed below rather than papered over, and three of them were faults in the program and not in the suite. (16) **`nth_of` guarded against an empty row in front of a rule its own arithmetic already delivered**: a band that did not fit is `Rect::EMPTY`, a zero width leaves a zero gap and a zero button width, a zero height leaves a zero button height, and every offset is a multiple of those — so the guard could be deleted and nothing changed (lesson 51 again, the fourth time in this app). Its *range* check stays, because a fourth footer button in a footer that is really there would otherwise be laid out past the end of the row, and that is a different rule with a test of its own. (17) **The help sheet let the game be played behind it.** Its last line says "Click anywhere to close" — and it recorded a hit box over its own 420x300 rectangle only, so the direction pad and the footer stayed live underneath a sheet that covers the board. A click on the pad slid tiles the player could not see, and closing the sheet afterwards revealed a board that had moved on without them. The sheet claims the whole window now, recorded last so `Frame::hit_test` — which answers with the last box painted — gives it the clicks of everything under it. (18) **The keyboard had the same fault from the other side**: an arrow key played a move on a board hidden behind the help text. `apply` treats the sheet as modal now — anything the game would otherwise act on shuts the sheet instead, which is both the answer a click already got and the one the sheet's own closing line promises. The two help intents fall through, since shutting the sheet is what they were going to do anyway, and `key_intent` has already refused the window's own combinations, so this cannot swallow a ctrl-W on its way to the window manager. **The remaining eleven were the suite's faults, in five familiar shapes.** *Four fixtures were blind.* The band-drop test used one window, 600x130, at which the drop order spends its budget on the footer and the info band and stops — so the header was never dropped, and a mutation that *flattened* a band instead of dropping it went unnoticed; it is a ladder of three heights now (130, 110, 50) that asserts every one of the four bands is dropped somewhere in it. The merge test used `[2, 2, 2]`, on which a merge that reaches past its neighbour to the tile *after* it finds the same value at either distance and the leftovers pack down identically — `known-issues.md` lesson 59, a fixture with no asymmetry cannot notice a swap; `[2, 4, 2, 0]` can. The frozen-game guard was expected to be owned by the lost-board test, but a lost board has no move left in it and refuses whether the guard is there or not — only a *won* game, frozen with moves still available, can tell the guard from the board's own emptiness (lesson 58, a witness more than one rule explains). And the modal-sheet test picked the centre of the sheet, which is over the board, where there are no controls; strengthened to walk all seven buttons it stopped passing at once, which is how fault (17) was found. *Three tests were built out of the thing they test* (lesson 52): the mirror test asked `Layout::cell` for the column it then asserted the tile was in, and derives it from the board rectangle and the grid size now; the title test measured the title against wherever the score boxes turned out to be, so the boxes themselves could move; and the window-size test asserted against `Probe::SIZE` rather than `initial_size`, so the size the tests draw at and the size the window opens at could drift apart unnoticed. *One was lesson 57* — "is it drawn?" is not "is it drawn *there*?": collapsing the footer's three buttons onto the first one was scored against `control_names`, which reads the target off a hit box and never asks where the box is. *Two expectations simply named the wrong test*: the resize mutation lives in the `Resize` handler, which a test driving `render` never reaches, and the out-of-range button belongs to the row tests, which are the only ones that ask for a button past the end. *And one `[skip]` was a harness artefact rather than a program fault*: replacing `(h / 40.0).clamp(8.0, 18.0)` with `12.0` leaves the numeric type ambiguous at a later `.max`, so the mutant did not compile; `12.0_f32` mutates the same rule and does.
