Two of the four survivors were rules that are genuinely reachable and were genuinely untested, and both are asked directly now rather than hoped for: `label`'s refusal of a zero width binds only in a narrow band of window widths (`pad + bw <= w <= 3*pad + bw`, about 50 to 54 pixels with a tall window), which no size in the fixture reached, so the test searches widths 40 to 220 for one that crowds the title out and fails if none does; and `centred`'s vertical clamp binds only where a line is taller than its band, which the layout's shrink-to-fit prevents at every window in the fixture except at the rounding edge of a font size, so the rule is asked of `centred` itself with a box four pixels high. **The second sweep is clean: 149 of 149 caught, every one by the test that claims the ground, no survivors, no wrong owners and no skips — and not one of them caught by a hang or a crash, so every catch is a named assertion failing rather than something merely noticing.** Window-wiring baseline 82 -> 81.
- [x] Typing Tutor (apps/typingtutor: **windowed** -- 14 lessons across 8 categories, a lesson list that scrolls under a category filter, a typing panel laid out on the advances the font actually reports and scrolled to follow the cursor, live WPM and accuracy, a results screen with a rating, a statistics dashboard of summary cards and a recent-results table, and every control a hit box so the pointer drives the whole program; 105 tests). Wiring it found nine faults on top of the missing window. **`main` was `let _app = TypingTutorApp::new();`** -- it built the lesson list, dropped it and exited, so no lesson ever reached a screen and no keystroke was ever counted by the thing that counts keystrokes. (1) **There was no mouse of any kind.** `handle_event` matched `Key` and `Tick` and nothing else, so every control the app drew on all four views -- start, back, retry, the category chips, the lesson rows -- was a picture of a control. `Target` names them now and the drawing pass records a hit box for each, so a control is clickable exactly where its ink is. (2) **Every coordinate was a literal.** Three of the four views took a `_width` and threw it away, none was given the height at all, and the lesson list stepped 50 px per row from y = 120 whatever the window did -- a window shorter than the list drew rows below its own bottom edge, and a window taller than it left the space empty. `Layout::solve` derives every band, row, card and type size from the live window size, and drops the chrome bands whole, least important first, so the body keeps 45% of the height however small the window gets. (3) **`scroll_offset` was write-only** -- declared, reset in two places and read nowhere -- so the list could not scroll and every lesson past the window's bottom edge was unreachable by mouse *and* by key. (4) **The typing panel never followed the typist.** A fixed 200 px panel at y = 100 showed the first few lines of every lesson and the cursor walked off the bottom of it; the panel scrolls to keep the cursor's line in view now. (5) **The subtitle said "chars" and counted bytes** -- `lesson.text.len()`, which is the same number only for the ASCII the shipped list happens to be, which is why the test for it adds a lesson where the two disagree rather than trusting the list to keep disagreeing on its own. (6) **`SessionResult::category` was stored on every finished lesson and read by nothing**, so the history could not say what kind of practice a row was; the recent-results table colours the lesson name by it. (7) **`chars_remaining`, `correct_count` and `incorrect_count` were computed, tested, and drawn nowhere.** They are on the typing bar and the results screen now -- and the last two say how the text *ended up*, which is not the same number as how many keys were pressed correctly once a typist has corrected a mistake. (8) **`table_y = 240.0`** put the recent-results table on top of the summary cards whenever they wrapped to a third row; the table starts below wherever the card grid actually ended. (9) **Four column constants at x = 30, 250, 340 and 440** pushed the time column off the right edge of any window under 480 px wide; the columns are shares of the width they are given. The file also opened with **ten crate-level lint allows** -- `dead_code` among them, which is how three computed-and-unread counters and a stored-and-unread category survived -- and all ten are gone, so the crate passes the lane gate without them. Writing the mutation table found three more. (10) **A test that asserted nothing.** `a_body_too_short_for_a_row_shows_none` chose 620x40 and put its assertion behind `if l.body.h < l.row`, but at 40 px all three chrome bands are dropped, so the body gets 34 px against a 20.8 px row and the branch never held: the test passed by not running. It asserts its precondition now, at a height where the precondition is true. This is `known-issues.md` lesson 89, and auditing the rest of the suite against its own tell -- any `if`, `let ... else { return }`, `filter` or `continue` whose condition mentions the code under test -- found four more silent exits, each of which now either asserts the branch is taken or counts the cases that were checked and refuses to pass on too few. (11) **A production comment stated a falsehood** about the lesson list it sat above, claiming non-ASCII text the list does not contain. (12) **Eight rules had no owning test** -- the bands stacking without overlapping, a squeezed window giving up its chrome before its body, a row carrying its place in the *filtered* list rather than its position in the drawn one (invisible without a filter, because unfiltered the two numberings agree), a window that grows revealing the rows the scroll had hidden, a key that types nothing not asking for a repaint, an unselected row being told apart from the background, the subtitle's character count, and the window's own title and app id. Sweeping the finished program found two more, and the first is fault 4 never actually having been proved fixed. (13) **The panel-follows-the-typist test typed a lesson that fit whole in the panel.** It chose the longest *shipped* lesson -- 51 characters -- and typed it into a 300x300 panel that holds around five lines of forty, so the text never overflowed, the panel never had to scroll, and pinning it to the top of the text was genuinely *correct* on the only fixture the test ever built. Lesson 89's shape one level up: not an assertion behind an `if`, but an assertion in a regime the rule does not govern. It types a lesson of its own now and asserts, from the glyphs actually drawn rather than from arithmetic over the layout, that the panel cannot show the whole text. (14) **The rating line's placement had no owning test** -- replacing `bottom` with a constant was noticed only by the geometric catch-all, and only at the sizes where the constant itself fell off the bottom of the window. That is silence about the actual damage, which is fault 8's family in the view next door: the rating printed *over* the cards, well inside the window. A test asks the rule directly now, and asserts the card grid wraps to more than one row before believing itself, since a constant above a single row of cards is indistinguishable from the rule. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/typingtutor/mutate.py` breaks 46 pieces of the program one at a time, and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. Window-wiring baseline 63 -> 62.
- [x] Checkers (apps/checkers: **windowed** — American draughts on an 8x8 board fitted to whatever window it is given, men and kings, compulsory captures and multi-jump chains, a minimax AI with alpha-beta pruning, a dot on every square the selected piece may reach, a header counting what each side has left and how many of them are kings, a side panel carrying the capture tally, the move number, as much history as there is room for and a help block anchored to the floor, and a keyboard and a pointer that reach the same moves; 145 tests). Wiring it found ten faults on top of the missing window, and the first three are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = CheckersApp::new();`** — it built the opening position, dropped it and exited, so no board ever reached a screen and no click ever arrived. (2) **`render(&self)` took no size at all**, and eleven `const f32` pixel counts decided the layout, so the app could only ever be correct at one window size and was unreachable off the bottom of anything shorter. Two constants remain and they are the opening size and nothing else; everything else is a share of the size the window reports each frame. (3) **Clicks were answered by arithmetic rather than by the frame**: `square_at` hit-tested by hand with float-to-`i8` casts and was the inverse of `square_center`, a second copy of the board's geometry kept in step by nothing but care — so the mouse tests agreed with whatever mapping the pair happened to share, right or wrong. Both are gone; the drawing pass records a box per square and the click asks `Frame::hit_test`. (4) **The `7 - row` rank flip appeared in three places, one of them inverted by hand.** Draughts puts rank 1 at the bottom, so the board row and the screen row run opposite ways; that flip is written once now, in `Grid::square`. (5) **Six hand-tuned glyph nudges** (`-18.0`, `-7.0`, `+6.0`, a crown at `cx - 8.0, cy - 10.0`) placed text by eye at one font size; they are measured with `text::measure` now, which is what lets the fonts scale with the window. (6) **`execute_move` set `status_message = "Black thinking..."` one line before `do_ai_move` overwrote it**, so the notice was never once visible — the AI answers inside the same event that provokes it, and there is no frame in between. The stored field is gone, the status line is derived from the board, and an unreachable `GameResult::Ongoing` arm went with it. (7) **The capture counters were named for the side taken *from* and printed as if named for the side that took.** Renamed rather than swapped at the print site: the naming was the fault, and swapping the print would have left the next reader to fall into it again. (8) **The history drew eighteen rows eighteen pixels apart whatever the window's height**, running straight through the help line below it; it fills the space above a floor-anchored help block now. (9) **The panel stacked its rows downwards from a fixed top with no regard for its own bottom**, so at a small window every row after the first painted over the board and the status line. `panel_row` declines a row that will not fit and comes back empty — which `union` and `Frame::hit` already ignore, so it needed no special case at either call site. (10) **Ten blanket `#![allow(...)]` hid about thirty-seven real lint findings and three dead functions** (`dark_index`, `generate_jumps_for`, `has_jumps`); the allows, the dead functions and the findings are all gone and the crate passes the lane gate. `has_jumps`'s two tests became a statement of the rule that actually matters — a capture on offer is compulsory, so a board with one on it offers nothing else. 40 deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/checkers/mutate.py`); the first sweep left thirteen unaccounted for, and unlike reversi's not one of them was a fault in the program — all thirteen were the suite's, which is what a suite written after that campaign rather than before it should look like. **Six were behaviour no test asserted at all.** The board's centring was invisible to every geometry test there was: with the board jammed into the corner of its area and the empty space in one stripe down the side, the squares still tile edge to edge, still stay inside the window and still miss the panel. Stating it absolutely is awkward because neither edge of the room the board is given is recorded in the frame, so the new test states it as a difference, where the padding cancels — two windows of the same height, and the board moves right by half the extra room. `a_click_off_the_board_moves_no_cursor` compared the cursor against its opening value, which is a1, and a1 is also where any "resolve a miss to *something*" mistake lands (`hit_test(..).unwrap_or(Target::Square(0, 0))`), so it could not tell a click that was ignored from a click that was misread; it walks the cursor to d4 first now. `App::render` had no test whatsoever — every drawing test went through `Probe::draw`, which calls `frame` directly, while `render` is the path the real window uses and the only one that can be wrong on its own: remember the new size, hand `frame` the old one, and the app resizes its hit boxes while painting the previous size's picture. The opening board is twelve against twelve, so a header reporting each side the *other* side's count reads correctly there and is wrong for every position after it; the test plays three black pieces off the board now. Nothing asserted *where* the header's text landed, only what it said, so the piece chips could go back to the hand-counted offset that slid them under the title at a large font and left them floating at a small one. And `on_event` was untested: `handle_event` says whether the app used an event, `on_event` turns that into what the window should do about it, and answering `Redraw` to everything is invisible in a test of `handle_event` alone and on screen is the whole board repainted per key release. **Seven were wrong expectations in the mutation table itself**, which is a verdict worth as much as the others. Three of the seven are one discovery: a label placed against the square it names cannot disagree with that square, so the tests reading the rank number and the file letter against `g.square(..)` are invariant under *every* mutation of the grid — upside down, mirrored, transposed. That invariance is exactly what those two tests are for, since a second copy of the flip arithmetic written for the labels is fault (4) again; a board drawn the wrong way round is caught instead by `the_ranks_read_one_to_eight_upwards_and_the_files_a_to_h_rightwards`, which reads the labels against the *screen*. Of the remaining four: the corner-click test runs at the opening size alone — a rounding check wants one size read closely rather than eight read loosely — so "resolve the click against the opening size" is what it already does; the row that makes `Probe::draw` ignore its size argument mutates the suite's own window onto the app rather than the app, and is worth keeping because it checks that the suite really does vary the size it draws at instead of asking for eight and always getting one; and the selection ring moved onto the cursor's square is not caught by the cursor's own test because the two rings are different colours and the mutation adds a second ring rather than moving the first. **The seventh was a genuine weakness and became a stronger test.** `panel_row` returns its rect whether or not it drew anything in it — that is deliberate, since the caller hands the rect to `f.hit` and an empty one is ignored — but it means a panel that recorded all five of its boxes and painted no text in any of them satisfied a test that only asked whether the boxes were there. That is a panel which answers clicks and shows nothing, and it is what the sweep produced; each box must now contain the origin of a line of text. Window-wiring baseline 67 -> 66.
- [x] Reversi (apps/reversi: **windowed** — an 8x8 Othello board fitted to whatever window it is given, flipping in all eight directions, a dot on every square the mover may play, passes detected and announced, a minimax AI four plies deep with alpha-beta pruning and corner/edge weights, a history in algebraic notation, a score bar split in proportion to the two counts, and a keyboard and a pointer that reach the same moves; 78 tests). Wiring it found eight faults on top of the missing window, and the mutation sweep found three more in the code it had just written. (1) **`main` was `let _app = ReversiApp::new();`** — it built the opening position and dropped it, so no board ever reached a screen and no key ever arrived. (2) **Every measurement was a `const f32`**: `CELL_SIZE: f32 = 60.0`, `BOARD_OFFSET_X`, `BOARD_OFFSET_Y`, `PANEL_X` and four font sizes, which is a board that is the wrong size at every window size but one and unreachable off the bottom of anything shorter. Every one of them is a share of the size the window reports each frame now. (3) **Clicks were answered by arithmetic rather than by the frame**: `cell_at` recomputed the square from the same constants the drawing used, a second copy kept in step by nothing but care, so the square it answered was only accidentally the square on screen. The drawing pass records a box per square and the click asks the frame. (4) **No player was ever told a pass had happened.** `advance_turn` wrote the pass notice and then called `update_message`, which overwrote it — and the same for “AI thinking (White)…”, written on a turn the AI answers inside the same event. The stored `message` is gone; the standing line is derived from the board and only the exception is kept. (5) **`pass_count` was a guard for a case it could not reach**: incremented and tested against 2, when every path into the function had just reset it to 0, and read nowhere else. What ends a game is that neither player can move, which is what is asked now. (6) **A search that found no move left White to move forever.** `do_ai_move` returned silently, with no key and no click that could unstick it; it hands the turn back and says so. It also recursed once per forced pass, which near the end of a game is a stack made deeper by a board rather than by a bug — it loops. (7) **The four arrow keys carried their bound in the match guard**, so a press at the edge of the board fell through to the catch-all and was read as a key with no meaning. (8) **`empty_count` was `64 - total_pieces()`** — a third place that knew the board size, with a helper that existed only to serve it. Twelve blanket `#![allow(...)]`, `dead_code` and `unused_imports` among them, are gone and the crate passes the lane gate. 103 deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/reversi/mutate.py`); the first sweep left fifteen unaccounted for and the three that were the *program's* fault are the ones worth recording. **A comment claimed the thing that stops the recursion is the thing that does not.** `minimax`'s pass branch said that spending a ply on a pass is what keeps two passes from bouncing forever; it is not — a board where both sides pass is a board `is_game_over` has already returned on, one line above. Leaving the ply unspent changes the value the search returns and cannot make it run forever, which is exactly why the test aimed at the branch, built on `Board::empty()`, never reached it. **A guard stood behind a duplicate of itself** (`known-issues.md` lesson 51 again): `draw_board` computed the legal-move list behind `if self.phase == Phase::Playing`, and the only place that list is read makes the same check four lines down — so the sweep could delete the outer one and no test could notice, because deleting it changed nothing. It is gone. **A defence sat outside the guard that made it unreachable**: the score bar divided by `(black + white).max(1.0)` while `if black > 0` a line above already guaranteed the total was at least one, so the floor could never fire and the sweep could remove it silently. The division lives inside the guard now, and a defence that cannot fire is not written at all. The other twelve were the suite's, and they sort into the same three piles this campaign keeps producing. **Five were fixtures in which the right answer and the wrong one coincide** (lesson 54): the occupancy check in `is_legal_move` was tested at d4, a square that flanks nothing on the opening board and so is refused twice over; the corner-versus-b2 comparison was satisfied by the separate corner bonus rather than by the weight table it claimed to test, so the table could be deleted wholesale; the column letters and row numbers were pinned to a1, which is on the diagonal, the one square a board whose rows and columns have been swapped still puts in the right place; the pass branch was tested on a board `is_game_over` returns on first; and the search's sign convention was tested on a free corner, which is the best move whether the reply is read as the opponent's or as a second move of its own. That last one needed a position where the two spellings actually disagree, found by playing games out and comparing — it is the board one ply into a game, after Black plays c4. **Four were behaviour nothing asserted at all**: the status band's share of what the header left rather than of the whole window (both spellings still sum to the height, the body absorbing the difference, so the band-ordering test cannot see it); the panel's turn line while the game is being played, only ever read after it was over; the result line's white and tie arms, the only finished game any test reached being one Black wins; and a refusal left standing over the move that answers it. **Two were the assertion being weaker than it reads**, and both are `known-issues.md` lesson 80, written from them: `nothing_is_drawn_outside_the_window` walked the frame's hit boxes, and `Frame::hit` intersects every box with the active clip before recording it — so a hit box outside the window is not something the frame declines to produce but something it cannot produce, and the test passed with the panel's entire band shifted twenty pixels off the bottom of a 60x60 window. The obvious rewrite, checking every painted rectangle against the window, is too strong rather than too weak and failed at once: a panel too short for its rows runs the score bar off the bottom and the app draws it anyway for the whole-window clip to crop, which is the design. The boundary that works is neither — it is the layout's own panel, which the clip has no opinion about. The floor under `inset` had the same problem from the other side: it only takes effect where a band is thinner than twice the padding, which no window in the size list is, so its test calls `inset` directly rather than waiting for a window small enough. **And two were wrong expectations in the mutation table itself**, which is a verdict worth as much as the others: deleting the board's own hit box cannot swallow a click, because it is recorded *before* the squares and the frame answers with whatever was recorded last — the fault that shape models is the box recorded *after* the loop, which is now a second row, and deleting it does something different and smaller, which is leave the board unnameable. And turning `if cell.is_piece()` into `if true` draws no piece that was not there, `draw_piece` returning on an empty cell; what it does is take the branch away from the dot, which is the `else` of the same `if`. The tautology that opened all this — a loop over `Frame::hits()` asserting every box lies inside the window, in an app that clips the whole frame to the window — was then hunted through the rest of `apps/**`: twenty-one apps carry an `outside_the_window`-shaped test, only three of them touch `f.hits()`, and two of those three (`apps/battleship`, `apps/freecell`) had it. Both keep only the half that can fail, as `the_whole_frame_is_clipped_to_the_window`, and both carried a comment claiming the reverse of `Frame::hit`'s own documented behaviour, which is how it survived three readings. Whether the deleted half had left a real hole was then asked of each app separately rather than assumed, with a mutation drifting a drawn band off the rect the layout gave it: battleship's is caught by four tests it already had, so nothing was added; freecell's was caught by nothing, so it gained `at_a_size_that_fits_the_clip_crops_nothing`, which measures every free cell, foundation and column bottom against `Table`. `apps/pdfviewer` pushes no window-sized clip at all — only per-band ones — so its assertion can fail and was left alone. The other fifteen apps that iterate `Frame::hits()` do so for centring or finiteness, not containment, and are unaffected. Written up as known-issues Lesson 80. Window-wiring baseline 68 -> 67.
- [x] Ebook Reader (apps/ebook: library with 5 sample books, paginated reading view, 3 font sizes, bookmarks, TOC from chapter separators, text search with next/prev, dark/sepia themes, progress tracking, 80+ tests) — *as built (2026-09-27): opens real plain-text books (Ctrl+O or the Open button; UTF-8, UTF-16 with a byte-order mark, anything else as Windows-1252 and says so; title and author from a Gutenberg-style header) and keeps the library, with each book's place, bookmarks and type size, in `<config>/ebook/library.txt`. It opened on five invented books and could open no other until then; they are test fixtures now. The reading theme (System or Sepia) is kept in `ebook.yaml` (C-Q26), and a value there it does not know is said.*
- [x] Compass (apps/compass: circular compass rose with degree markings, 16-point cardinal directions, waypoint system with Haversine distance, magnetic declination, coordinate entry, km/miles toggle, 128 tests)
  - [x] **Windowed** (apps/compass: `app::launch`, a `Layout` solved from the live window size every frame, and hit boxes recorded by the drawing pass). This is the forty-seventh application in the wiring campaign. `main` was one line — `let _app = CompassApp::new();` — which built a rose, a needle, a declination model, ten waypoints, three views and a coordinate parser, dropped all of it without drawing a pixel or reading a key, and exited zero. Wiring it found eight faults. (1) **Nothing was clickable but the waypoint list**, and that only by accident: the list re-derived its rows arithmetically from constants, so a press was answered at coordinates a scrolled-away or clipped-away row no longer occupied. The rose, the tabs, the unit toggle, the declination steppers, the delete button, the entry fields and the add button did not exist as controls at all — the views were reachable with `W`, `C` and `Esc`, the heading only with the arrow keys, and declination only with `D` and `Ctrl+D`, where **`D` alone *decreased* it** and its sole mention on screen was a help line. (2) **The name field was painted, labelled, and impossible to type into**: `CoordField::next` cycled two of the three fields, so `Tab` never reached the third and no pointer route existed to any of them. (3) **`key_to_char` mapped `Key::A` to `.` and `Key::B` to `-`**, ignoring shift and the keyboard layout both, so on any non-US layout a coordinate could not be typed and a `+` could not be produced at all; text comes from `event.text` now, which is what the keyboard actually produced. (4) **The waypoint list was one padded `format!` per row** — `{:<14}`, `{:<12}` — which is a claim that the font is monospaced. It is not; the list has real columns. (5) **All but three runs of text were drawn with `max_width: None`**, so a long waypoint name walked over the column beside it; every run goes through one `bounded` funnel now. (6) **Ten crate-level `allow`s** covered the file, one of them hiding a dead palette entry. (7) Found by the size sweep before a mutation was written: **text was vertically centred in its box at whatever size the caller asked for**, so a run taller than its box stuck out of both ends — which is how the status line came to be drawn *below the bottom edge* of a 30-pixel window. The fix is at the funnel, not the callers: `bounded` shrinks type to its box, and `centred` measures at the shrunken size, because centring a 15-point string against a 15-point measurement in a 4-point strip puts it in the wrong place. (8) Found by the mutation sweep: **the panel's second guard could never be false.** `w - wanted_panel >= wanted_panel` asked whether the readouts column takes more than it leaves, but the column is 32% of the window, so it leaves 68%, and above the 300-point clamp the window is already wider than 600. It was removed; the gate that does the work is legibility — a column too narrow for its own numbers is a column of ellipses, and the rose is the better use of that width. The suite is 128 tests, of which 12 sweep 12 window sizes across 9 states. The mutation table is 30 rows and **all four of the first sweep's non-`[ok]` verdicts were faults in the tests, not in the table.** Two were mis-aimed expectations: hanging the status strip off the bottom of the window does not make anything *overlap* it, and taking a too-narrow panel is a legibility fault rather than a rose-starving one. The other two were blind spots worth the name. `rows_in`'s `floor` → `ceil` survived because a half-fitting row is both drawn *and* hit-boxed, so a test comparing "what is drawn" with "what is clickable" watches both sets grow together; what gives it away is the *shape* of the last hit box, since `Frame::hit` trims to the clip and a part row therefore answers over part of a row. And swapping the rose's `atan2` axes survived because the test pressed at exactly 45°, the one bearing at which measuring from east and measuring from north agree. Fixing the second of those exposed a third: the new `body_h` mutation then survived `nothing_is_drawn_over_the_status_line`, whose assertion was `rect.y < status.y` — the *top* edge only, so a control that starts above the line and ends under it passed. That is lesson 85's one-sided comparison in a hit box; it asks for `rect.bottom()` now.
- [x] Stopwatch (apps/stopwatch: **windowed** — `app::launch`, `Frame` hit boxes, `Layout` from the live window size, and a 16 ms tick asked for *only while the clock runs*. The timekeeping was already right here (it advances by `elapsed_ms`, fixed 2026-08-25 per known-issues lesson 45), so the four things it could not do were the others: (1) **it drew a frame** — `main` built the struct and dropped it, so the stopwatch had no face and no buttons. (2) **it took a click** — Start/Pause, Lap, Reset, Mode, History, Set Time, the three countdown digit cards and their six ▲/▼ steppers were all drawn and inert, reachable only from the keyboard; the card's hit box is recorded *before* its steppers so `hit_test`'s reverse walk makes a stepper click a step rather than a selection. (3) **it fits its window** — the lap table's length was the field `max_visible_laps: 8`, and the history list carried a *second*, unrelated `8` split between its scroll clamp and its renderer, so the two lists could drift apart and neither had anything to do with how tall the window was; both now come from `Layout`, which is derived per frame and never remembered, and the toolbar slides before it scales rather than running off the edge. (4) **it scrolled** — no wheel handler at all, so past the eighth lap the only way down was the arrow keys. One logic bug fell out of the reading: resuming a countdown that had already reached zero put it in RUNNING at 00:00 with the alert still up and the tick advancing nothing, escapable only by Reset — a state a Start button makes reachable in one click where the space bar made it deliberate; it is refused now, with a regression test. Precision stopwatch with lap timing, split times, best/worst/average stats, countdown timer mode, session history, 79 tests)
- [x] Metronome (apps/metronome: BPM control 20-300, 9 time signatures, 4 subdivisions, tap tempo, accent patterns, practice mode with auto BPM increment, visual beat indicators, 80+ tests) — *visual only: it makes no sound, since no application can play one (`known-issues.md` → `[E] Applications can neither record nor play sound`), and since 2026-09-25 says so*
- [x] Connect4 (apps/connect4: **windowed** — a 7x6 board laid out from whatever window it is given, gravity drops, an alpha-beta search at depth 6 playing Yellow, the winning four ringed and joined by a line so its direction reads at a glance, a running You/AI/Draw tally, undo of the whole round, a modal help sheet, and every column a hit box so the pointer plays the game on its own; 245 tests). Wiring it found sixteen faults on top of the missing window, and the first three are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = Connect4App::new();`** — it built a board, dropped it and exited; no window opened, nothing was drawn, and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **`render` was not given the window at all.** It took `&self` and no size and drew from constants — the board at a fixed `(40, 90)` with a 72-pixel cell, and a background rectangle whose size was computed *from the board*, the drawing pass telling the window how big to be. `Layout` is derived from the live window size every frame now. (3) **Nothing was clickable.** There was no mouse code in the file — for a game whose whole interface is “put one in that column”. Every column is a hit box now, recorded by the pass that paints it, plus a footer with the three verbs that are not a move. (4) **“AI thinking…” could never be seen:** the string was in `render`, but the search ran synchronously inside the key handler that started it, before the frame that would have shown the message. The AI's move is owed on the next tick now, so the frame that says it is thinking is painted first. (5) **`Escape to quit` was in the module documentation and in no handler** — Escape fell into `_ => false`. (6) **The game's state was kept twice:** `Connect4App::status` was maintained by hand after each drop while `Board::status` computed the same answer from the grid, and `find_winner` ran once for the status and again for the highlight. One `Board::outcome` answers both in a single scan now. (7) **A move could not be taken back.** The `undo_drop` the search leans on was there and nothing offered it to the player, so a misclick — the exact accident a pointer interface introduces — ended the game. (8) **The column numbers were centred by guessing** (`cx - 4.0`, half of one digit at one font size); they are measured now. (9) **The crate did not pass the lane's clippy gate at all**, carrying `#![allow(dead_code)]` and nine more crate-wide allows; all ten are gone, and with them `Board::check_line`, which only the tests called. (10) **`has_won(Cell::Empty)` was true on an empty board** — the scan asked whether four cells in a line all held the colour handed to it, and on a board of nothing they all hold `Empty`. One caller away from crowning a winner before the first move; absence is not a colour, and the scan refuses `Empty` up front now. (11) **`minimax`'s emptiness guard was a duplicate of the test above it** — `is_terminal` had already returned on exactly those positions — and what it stood in for, “the loop improved on nothing”, was papered over by a `fallback` column claiming a choice had been made when none had. (12) **…and the `Option` that replaced the fallback was then seeded with a column anyway**, described as the answer for a case alpha-beta cannot produce. Found by mutation: replacing the seed with `None` changed no test's verdict. (13) **The help sheet excused the two keys that are about the help sheet from its own modal rule.** Both arms ended where the rule ends, so no key could tell the paths apart, and the exemption's only effect was to make `ToggleHelp`'s toggle and `CloseHelp`'s open-sheet branch unreachable. (14) **The undo snapshot carried two fields that could only hold one value** — every snapshot is taken of a live game, so `status` was always `Playing` and `win_line` always `None`; a restore that read them and one that ignored them were indistinguishable. `restore` sets the game back in play rather than pretending to restore it. (15) **All five drawing passes opened with a “did this band fit?” guard that nothing needed** — `fill`, `centred`, `label` and `Frame::hit` each refuse an empty box already. Four came out under `known-issues.md` lesson 51; the fifth was kept, with a paragraph explaining why the board was different, and the paragraph was wrong: the padding is capped at a quarter of the smaller side and the drop ladder empties every other band before it touches `BOARD_SHARE`, so there is no window down to 1x1 in which the board is empty. That is `known-issues.md` lesson 61 — an exception argued for in a comment is an assertion nobody checks — and the guard is replaced by `the_board_is_drawn_in_every_window_however_small`, which sweeps a grid of sizes rather than the `WINDOWS` list, because a claim about every size has to be asked about sizes nobody picked deliberately. (16) **The AI's move was witnessed only by the AI's own report of it.** The test checked that the column `ai_turn` returned was the column that appeared in the move list — but both come out of the same two lines, so shifting the played column one to the right between choosing it and dropping into it failed nothing: the piece landed in the wrong place, the report said the wrong place, and the two agreed. That is `known-issues.md` lesson 62 — a witness the code under test produced moves with the fault — and the test now asks `ai_best_move` what it would choose, on a clone of the board, before letting `ai_turn` play at all. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/connect4/mutate.py` breaks 210 pieces of the program one at a time, and after two sweeps every one of them is caught by the test that claims to cover it.
- [x] Hangman (apps/hangman: **windowed** -- a header, a gallows, the word and a three-row keyboard laid out from whatever window they are given, a statistics column that is dropped whole rather than squeezed when the window cannot pay for it, keys that grow with the window up to a size past which they would be billboards, a hint that is a button, category rows and difficulty chips that are buttons, a result card offering both ways out, and every letter a hit box so the pointer plays the game on its own; 152 tests). Wiring it found twelve faults, and the first two are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = HangmanApp::new();`** -- it picked a word, revealed whatever the difficulty owed, dropped the whole thing and exited. No window opened and nothing was ever displayed. (2) **There was no mouse handler at all** -- not a broken one: none. `handle_event` matched `Event::Key` and nothing else, so the three rows of letters the program drew across the bottom of its window were a *picture* of a keyboard: a player could look at the A key and could not press it. The hint was in the same state -- a line of text reading "Hint: H key", a label describing a keystroke where a button would do -- and so were the category rows and the difficulty chips, which is the entire menu. Every letter, chip, row and verb is a hit box recorded by the pass that paints it now, and a test asserts that typing a letter and clicking the key that bears it do the same thing. (3) **`render` took no width and no height** and painted the same 740x560 picture into every window it was given, from ten constants (`PADDING`, `HEADER_HEIGHT`, `GALLOWS_SIZE`, `WORD_AREA_HEIGHT`, `KEYBOARD_HEIGHT`, `STATS_PANEL_WIDTH` and four font sizes). `Layout::solve` is derived from the live window size every frame now, and the key size is the smaller of what the width allows and what the height allows -- taking the width alone survives every containment test and eats the game in a wide, short window, which is what the mutation sweep had to be told to look for. (4) **The win rate was drawn at `x: header_w - 60.0`** -- `header_w` is a *width* being used as a coordinate, so in a narrow window the rate left the header entirely. It is measured and set against the header's right edge now, and a window too narrow to hold it drops it rather than drawing it somewhere wrong. (5) **The menu title was centred by subtracting a literal 80.0**, half of one string at one font size; it is measured now. (6) **The header items sat at `PADDING + 100.0` and `PADDING + 220.0`** -- offsets that were right for one set of words, so a long category name put the difficulty on top of it. Each item starts where the one before it ends. (7) **The chip row started `5.0 * (btn_h + btn_gap)` down the menu** -- the number of categories written a second time, forty lines from the `Category::ALL` that holds it, so a sixth category would have been drawn under the chips. (8) **The figure was six `if wrong_count >= N` blocks**, one per part, each repeating a number `MAX_WRONG` already holds; it is a table of parts walked to `wrong_count` now, and `MAX_WRONG` is its length. (9) **Fifteen crate-level `#![allow]`s** hid every arithmetic and indexing finding the lane gate would have named -- the crate had never been built with it on. All fifteen are gone and every finding is fixed structurally: the win-rate percentage goes through `checked_div`, the letter index through `checked_sub`, and the alphabet through `slice::get`. Writing the tests and mutating the finished program found two more. (10) **The rule for "which letters are still hidden" was written twice** -- `apply_free_reveals` and `use_hint` each walked the word building the same list -- and so was the word's display form, once in `draw_word` and once in a `display_word` that only the tests called. One `unrevealed_letters`/`reveal_one_at_random` pair answers the first now, and the second is a test-module query built from the frame's ink rather than a second copy of the rule (lesson 92). (11) **`draw_keyboard` opened with a second copy of the `key <= 0.0` guard `Layout::key_rect` already holds.** No test could reach one of the two, and the sweep found it by deleting the copy in `key_rect` and watching nothing fail -- a window with no room for a keyboard yields no rectangles, and a loop over no rectangles draws nothing without being told to. The copy in the drawing pass is gone and `a_window_too_small_for_a_keyboard_draws_no_keys_and_offers_none` reaches the one that is left. (12) **The band between the header and the keyboard could start below where it ends.** `keyboard.y` is floored at the header's bottom rather than at the header's bottom plus a pad, so in a 900x55 window the free band began at 38.6 and finished at 37.3. `free_h` clamped to zero and hid it, but the word row is *placed* at the band's top, so the word was drawn over the top row of keys -- and the test written to catch the mutation of the word row's clamp found the unmutated program failing it first. A band cannot begin after it finishes, and `free_y` is now floored at the keyboard's top. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/hangman/mutate.py` breaks 20 pieces of the program one at a time, and every one of them is caught by the test that claims to cover it. Window-wiring baseline 60 -> 59. **Revisited 2026-09-01 for C-CENTRING-IS-NOT-A-BOUND** (lesson 109), which is why the counts above are now 160 tests and 48 mutation rows. The grep predicted nine unbounded-centring sites; the app had nine faults, and only four of them were the shape the grep can see. (a) **`centred_text` did the horizontal half of the job and nothing at all about the vertical**, so every heading it drew was placed by an unclamped `(band.h - size) / 2.0`; it is deleted, and `centre_line`/`span`/`run_in`/`column_line` are the four helpers everything now goes through. (b) **The gallows square overran its band by one padding**, and the figure's strokes overran the square: a stroke straddles the line it is drawn on and its thickness has a floor of one point that does not scale, so a 6-point band drew the beam above the band's own top edge. The square is inset by a whole stroke width now. (c) **The header's height was `.min(h)`** -- a clamp to the whole window is not a bound on a band that starts a padding down, and a 740x12 window put a 12-point bar at y = 4. (d) **An empty keyboard still claimed the gap between the keys it did not have**: `(key * 0.14).clamp(1.0, 5.0)` has a floor that does not care whether the key exists, so a window with no room for a keyboard took two points out of everything above it. (e) **`draw_stats`' divider was drawn at `stats.x + pad` with a `.max(0.0)` width** -- a stroke of no length is still a mark one line width across, so a column narrower than two paddings put a 1-point square a padding past its own right edge. (f) **`draw_header` filled its band before looking at it**, the one pass that did not refuse an empty band outright. Two bands were *named* rather than merely bounded, because a region no test can name is a region no test checks a pass against: the difficulty chips' strip became an argument instead of `x`, `y` and `w`, and `draw_result`'s dimmed band became `Layout::overlay` instead of four lines in the middle of the drawing code -- which is what made its card-height clamp and its button-fit check reachable at all. Two clamps a stronger guard already dominated were deleted rather than left standing as claims (`chip_h`'s `.min(band.h)`, which `intersect` subsumes on all four sides; the chips' `is_empty` early-out, which `intersect` answers by returning `None`). And `draw_word`'s comment claimed "one `centre_line` for the row rather than one per letter" while the loop called `run_in`, which asks again per cell -- the row's guard placed nothing but the rule and could be replaced by the bare centring it exists to prevent with the suite green; the letters sit on the row's single baseline now. The test side grew three things the campaign will reuse: `no_pass_paints_outside_the_region_it_owns` sweeps `squeezes()` of the five bands that are *inputs* to their pass and says in the test why the keyboard's derived band is excluded; `squeezes()` yields sixteenths of the band as well as absolute slivers, because the interesting failures live between "the type fits" and "the type and what hangs below it fits", which is a fraction and not a constant; and the frame reader measures a stroke's thickness on both axes, without which a pass made only of strokes -- the gallows -- measures as painting nothing and can be drawn anywhere. Full write-up in `known-issues.md` under C-CENTRING-IS-NOT-A-BOUND.)
- [x] Simon (apps/simon: **windowed** — four pads in a two-by-two grid laid out from whatever window it is given, a sequence that grows by one colour a round and is played back at one of three speeds, the lit pad derived rather than stored, a haloed glow behind the pad rather than over it, a status line that counts the player through the sequence, a game-over panel carrying the score, the session best and the losses, a modal help sheet, and every pad a hit box so the pointer plays the game on its own; 142 tests). Wiring it found twenty-six faults on top of the missing window, and the first three are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = SimonApp::new();`** — it built the game, dealt the first colour and exited; no window opened, nothing was painted, and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **`render` was not given the window at all.** It took `&self` and no size, and every rectangle in it was a compile-time constant — a 160-pixel pad at a fixed `(GRID_X, 160.0)`, an info panel at `GRID_Y + BUTTON_SIZE * 2.0 + BUTTON_GAP + 30.0` — so a window the user resized showed the same picture in the wrong place with unpainted canvas around it. `Layout` is derived from the live window size every frame now. (3) **Nothing was clickable:** `Event` was matched for `Key` and `Tick` and everything else fell into `_ => {}`, so a pointer could do nothing at all in a game whose entire interface is four big buttons. Every pad is a hit box now, plus a footer with the three verbs that are not a pad. (4) **The module documentation promised persistence that did not exist** — “high score tracking persists across restarts”, with no load and no save anywhere in the crate, and nothing in the toolkit to save *to*; the claim is now what is true, and the real gap is logged in `known-issues.md` rather than papered over. (5) **The best score was kept twice under two names,** `high_score` and `longest_streak`, raised by the same two comparisons in the same two places and shown as two readouts that could not possibly disagree. (6) **A window that had just opened said “Games: 1”** — `games_played` was incremented by `start_new_game`, which the constructor calls to deal the first colour. It counts games *lost* now, which is the only way a Simon game ends. (7) **Both animations ran at frame rate rather than on the clock:** `pulse_counter` was `wrapping_add(1)` once per tick and the pulse and blink were `% 8` and `% 6` of it, so they sped up and slowed down with the compositor's load. (8) **`MAX_SEQUENCE_LEN = 999` was declared and never read** — deleted rather than enforced, because enforcing it needs a behaviour at the boundary the constant never had. (9) **`round` and the sequence could disagree:** the round number was raised and the colour pushed *conditionally*, so the counter and the thing it counts were two facts; the round is read off the sequence's length now. (10) **The two-by-two grid was written out four times** — `ALL`'s order, `to_index`, `grid_pos` and the arrow keys' literal `2`s — so a fifth colour would have compiled after being added in three of the four places. (11) **Pad labels were centred by guessing** (`- 20.0`, `- 10.0`); they are measured now. (12) **The glow was painted over the thing it was meant to be behind,** covering the pad's face and label with a 60-alpha wash instead of haloing it. (13) **The window said the same word twice,** one of them blinking. (14) **The pause before playback threw away its overshoot,** so the first colour of every round was shown one frame longer than the rest — more on a slow machine, since the overshoot is the frame interval. (15) **Changing speed mid-playback could skip a colour:** elapsed time was measured against the *new* duration, so Slow→Fast with 700ms on a flash ran 400ms straight through the 150ms gap and advanced two steps, in the middle of the sequence the player is being asked to memorise. (16) **The pad the player lost on stayed lit for ever,** because the timer cleared it only `if state == PlayerInput` and a wrong press leaves that state immediately. (17) **Four code paths wrote `lit_button`,** and 16 is what that costs; which pad is lit is not state but a question about the state machine and the timers, and `Simon::lit` computes it and nothing stores it. (18) **The restart hint listed three ways to restart out of five;** the keys and the sheet that documents them are one table now, walked in both directions by a test. (19) **Ten crate-wide `allow`s,** including the `dead_code` and `unused_imports` that would have said the quiet part out loud — the whole render half of the file was unreachable. (20) **Modifiers were ignored, so `Ctrl+S` cycled the speed.** Mutating the finished program found six more, five of them lines that could be deleted with nothing failing. (21) **A `max(1)` in `flash_pad` was defended by a comment that was false** — it claimed a zero-millisecond flash would never be cleared, but `age_flash` maps `Some(0)` to `None` on the very next tick, so the case the floor prevented could not arise. That is `known-issues.md` lesson 61, an exception argued for in a comment being an assertion nobody checks. (22) **Four assignments restated what already held** — `new_game`'s `flash = None`, the handover's `player_index = 0` and `flash = None`, and a second `Playback::new()` one line after the first — each deleted under lesson 51 and replaced by a test holding the property it was aiming at. (23) **The pad halo was clipped against a window it cannot leave:** below a 200-pixel step the halo grows by at most `step * 0.0368` against a half-gap of `step * 0.04`, and above it the growth caps at 6 against a half-gap of 8, so the `intersect` was dead in every window. (24) **A test proved `PAD_SHARE` by computing `PAD_SHARE`,** rebuilding the expected geometry from the same constant the code uses, so it agreed with any value that constant took; it asserts against measured pixels now. (25) **The harness itself exited 0 on a sweep it had not passed** — the second sweep returned 182 `[ok]`, one `[skip]` and two wrong-test verdicts and reported success, the three found by reading 185 lines of log. It fails the run now, and a `[skip]` counts: a survivor is loud, but a stale anchor silently stops applying its mutation inside a run that still ends “0 survived”. (26) **And one fault that was not simon's at all.** Deleting `draw_readout`'s empty-box guard failed a text-inside-the-window test at 24x24, naming the *pad numbers*, which no readout draws: at that size every band is dropped, so a readout box is empty and its caption asks for a 0-pixel line height before the early return that would have skipped it; `gui/font`'s `FontCache::get` rounded 0.0 to cache key 1 but *built* the entry at the raw 0.0, which no face can scale to, installing the coarse 8x16 bitmap fallback under key 1 — and the pad numbers, legitimately 1.472 pixels, round to that same key and measured 8x16. A cache keyed coarsely but built from the raw argument hands the wrong answer to whoever asks second, and hands it three subsystems away from the mistake. Entries are built at `key_px(key)` now, with two regression tests verified to fail against the old code, and it is written up as `known-issues.md` lesson 64. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/simon/mutate.py` breaks 185 pieces of the program one at a time, and after two sweeps every one of them is caught by the test that claims to cover it.)
- [x] Crossword (apps/crossword: **windowed** -- three interlocking 5x5 puzzles, a grid sized from the live window every frame with every cell a hit box on its own ink, a banner naming the word under the cursor, a scrolling clue panel whose every row is clickable, a footer of six buttons that each duplicate a key, a running clock, a help card sized to the window it is drawn in and an end card that counts what was given away; 70 tests). Wiring it found seventeen faults, and the first four mean the program was not a crossword and could not be played. (1) **`main` was `let _app = CrosswordApp::new();`** -- it built three puzzles, numbered every cell, hung the clues and then dropped the lot and exited. No window opened, nothing was ever drawn, and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **The three grids were not crosswords.** They were laid out from their across answers and nobody read down the columns, which spelled `SAPETRE`, `HALLILA` and `FSIVRNA`. All three grids are replaced with fully interlocking 5x5s -- `SPA/TAR/ELITE/SEE/END` over `STEAK/PAL/ARISE/SPEED/TEN`, and two more like it -- and `every_run_of_two_or_more_cells_is_a_word_with_a_clue` asserts that every maximal run of two or more cells in *both* directions is a word with a clue of the right length, which is the property the old grids failed. `ANSWERS` pins all thirty words so a future edit to a grid string is a failing test rather than a puzzle nobody can solve. (3) **The clue tables carried hand-written numbers, and the program derived its own.** Every cell that starts a word is numbered in reading order at load; the tables also stated a number per clue, and in all three puzzles the two disagreed -- a clue whose number named a *down* start was hung on whatever cell happened to carry that number, and the eight words per puzzle the tables never mentioned got no clue at all. The tables carry no numbers now, only text in grid order; the number on a clue is read off the cell it starts at, once, and `every_clue_number_is_the_number_on_the_cell_it_starts_at` and `a_clue_carries_the_text_written_for_the_word_it_is_on` are the two halves of that. `MISSING_CLUE` makes a word with no clue visible in the panel instead of silent. (4) **A click was resolved against a grid twelve pixels above the one on the screen.** `handle_grid_click` re-derived the geometry from its own copies of `cell_size`, `grid_x` and `grid_y`, and its `grid_y` was `60.0` while the drawing pass used `72.0`. The pass that paints a cell records its hit box now, so the picture and the hit test are one fact; `a_click_selects_the_cell_it_was_painted_in` walks every cell of every puzzle at twelve window sizes. (5) **The clock was a field nothing incremented.** `elapsed_secs` was set to zero on load, displayed in the header, and never added to by anything in the file. The app asks for a 200ms tick now, the clock runs while a puzzle is open and stops in the menu and on the end card, and three tests say so. (6) **The end test was `all_filled && all_correct`.** No board can fail the first half while passing the second -- a cell whose entry equals its solution has an entry -- so deleting it changed nothing observable and no test could reach the difference (lesson 92). It is `all(Cell::is_correct)` over a non-empty board now; the non-empty half is real, because `all(..)` over nothing is true and a program with no puzzle loaded would otherwise open on its own congratulations card. (7) **`flagged_wrong` was a field on every cell**, set by the check and cleared at four separate call sites, so the mark could disagree with the letter it was marking -- type the right letter over a marked cell and the mark stayed. It is derived from `check_mode` and the cell now, and `a_mark_follows_the_letter_it_marks` is the test that could not have passed before. (8) **The panel drew `.take(8)` of each direction from a scroll offset no event ever changed**, so a puzzle's ninth clue was simply not on the screen and there was no way to ask for it. The wheel scrolls the panel now, through the shared `Accumulator` so a fraction of a notch is kept rather than rounded away, and moving the cursor brings the clue it lands on into view. (9) **Two functions took an argument and ignored it** -- `move_to_next_clue(_reverse: bool)` walked forwards whichever way you asked, so Shift+Tab was Tab; `word_length(.., _dir)` measured across whatever direction it was handed. Both are one code path per rule now: `cycle_clue(forward)` and `word_cells(row, col, dir)`, the latter replacing four hand-written word walks (`word_length`, `reveal_word`, `cells_in_current_word` and the render's highlight) that each had their own loop and their own bounds test. (10) **`key_to_letter` answered `'\0'` for "not a letter"** and the caller asked `is_ascii_alphabetic` of the answer -- a sentinel character standing in for an absence the type system can carry. It is `Option<char>`. (11) **A clue was cut at `(w / 7.0) - 3` *bytes*** -- a guessed advance, and a byte offset into a `&str`, which aborts the process the first time a clue holds an accented letter. The renderer is the only thing that knows how wide the text it draws will be, so it is the only thing that may cut it: every clue goes to it whole with a `max_width` and `TextOverflow::Ellipsis`, and `a_clue_with_an_accent_in_it_is_drawn_rather_than_aborting` puts `Café frappé -- a naïve piñata` in a clue at twelve window sizes. (12) **Every heading was centred by subtracting half of one particular string at one particular size** -- `width / 2.0 - 100.0`, `cx - 60.0`, `bx + bw / 2.0 - 30.0`, and a letter in a cell placed by subtracting six pixels from the middle of it. Everything is measured now, and `a_heading_is_centred_by_measuring_it_rather_than_by_a_literal` asserts that 300 more pixels of window moves a centred heading exactly 150 right, whatever the string is. (13) **The help card was a fixed 360x280 box** at `width / 2.0 - 180.0` with its rows at literal offsets inside it, so in any window narrower than 360 the help was drawn outside the window that needed it. It is sized from the lines it holds and clamped to the window. (14) **The footer was one line of text naming eight keystrokes**, drawn in the one strip of the window that exists to be clicked. All six verbs are buttons now, recorded as hit boxes by the pass that paints them, and `the_buttons_and_the_keys_do_the_same_thing` asserts each button and its key reach the same state; a footer too narrow for a button leaves it out rather than drawing it off the edge. (15) **The four arrow keys were four hand-written loops**, each with its own bounds test and its own `if cursor == 0 { return }` guard in front of a subtraction -- and `Key::Up`'s guard was in the match arm while `Key::Down`'s was in the loop, so the two directions were not the same code in any sense a reader could check. One `arrow(step, face)` answers all four, and the arrows cross black squares where typing stops at the end of a word, which the old `advance_cursor` did not: it scanned to the next playable cell in the row and carried on typing into a different word. Writing the tests found one more, which no amount of reading would have, and mutating the finished program found the last. (16) **The clue panel's scroll limit was counted in clues and its rows were drawn in rows.** `max_scroll` was `clues.len() - rows_that_fit`; the drawing pass emitted two direction headings among the clues out of a budget that had never counted them, so the list was two rows longer than anything scrolled for and the last clue of every puzzle was on no screen the panel could reach. It was invisible for the length of the wiring turn because the default 860x580 window shows all ten clues at once -- `max_scroll()` is zero there, so four scrolling tests passed against a program whose wheel did nothing, having nothing to do (lesson 90, four times over). A `SHORT` fixture with a guard test put them in the regime, and the repair makes the drawn row the unit of the arithmetic: a `PanelRow` enum, one `panel_rows()` that produces the whole list in draw order including the headings, a `max_scroll` that counts it and a `show_clue` that scrolls in row space. The heading-drawing code also existed twice -- once before the loop for the sticky heading and once inside it for the transition (lesson 92) -- and there is one copy now. Because a heading can scroll off, a clue row carries its direction: `7A.`, not `7.`, since 7 Across and 7 Down are otherwise the same row. Recorded as lesson 95. (17) **Three rules were written twice, and the mutation sweep found which copy nothing was holding.** Eighty rewrites of the production file, each required to make a named test fail, turned up three: `scroll_clues` clamped to `max_scroll()` inside its forward branch *and* again at the store; `enter_letter` upper-cased a letter `letter_of` had already upper-cased; and `select` bounded an index `Key::Down` had already bounded. In each case one copy could be deleted with no test failing anywhere, which is what a copy nothing holds means (lesson 92) -- and the second copy is the one that turns a real rule into a rule that is only accidentally true. Each is one copy now, at the place that owns it. The same sweep found three tests whose names claimed more than their bodies: `every_button_is_drawn_where_a_click_can_reach_it` asserted the button *intersected* the footer, which half a button hanging over the grid satisfies, so a button drawn a whole height too high passed; `a_clue_is_handed_to_the_renderer_whole_and_bounded_by_width` looked for each clue's whole text and skipped the clues it could not find, so a cut clue passed by being absent from the frame; and `a_clue_with_an_accent_in_it_is_drawn_rather_than_aborting` searched the whole frame for `piñata`, which the banner draws by a second route, so the panel could cut the same clue in half unseen. It also found six claims with no test at all -- that a panel too narrow to read is left out rather than drawn (reachable only in a window whose grid is bounded by its *height*, so that dropping the panel does not widen the grid and the useless gap stays: no fixture was in that band, lesson 90 again), that `MISSING_CLUE` reaches the panel, that `index` rejects a row past the bottom as well as a column past the right edge, that an arrow turns the cursor the way it moved, that an arrow which cannot move does not turn either, and that nothing with no area is painted. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/crossword/mutate.py` breaks 80 pieces of the program one at a time, and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. Recorded as lesson 96. Window-wiring baseline 58 -> 57. The eighth app through the **C-CENTRING-IS-NOT-A-BOUND** campaign (`known-issues.md`), which took the suite to 77 tests and 107 mutation rows and closed all seven centring sites. Two faults were predicted from a read-ahead and both were there: `text_at` pushed `max_width: None`, so no run it drew was ever cut to anything (sokoban's shape 9), and **every centring in the file was computed against the font size rather than the line height** -- the compositor's `draw_text` computes `baseline = y + ascent`, so a run occupies `[y, y + line_height)` and a box centred on the font size is short by the face's leading, 33% here. Every centred run in the app sat *low* by half of that, and the direction is worth stating because it says which edge the fault reaches first: the slack above the run grows and the slack below it shrinks, so it is always the band's bottom. The second is the one worth recording, because **the campaign's own containment test cannot see it, and not for want of sizes**: a clue row is 1.7x the font and a line is 1.33x, so a run centred on the font size is off by a sixth of a line and never leaves its row -- no window size reaches it, no squeeze reaches it, and none ever will. It produced three separate mutation survivors before the cause was understood. The counterweight is cheap and general and is now shape 29: **where a pass fills a box and then writes in it, the fill is the band**, so the run and the fill must share a centre line. Getting that test right added three more shapes. It has to pair a run to a band by the run's **centre point**, not by containment -- a cell's fill is the cell inset by a point while its letter is bounded to the whole cell, so the letter is never inside the fill it belongs to, and pairing by containment silently drops exactly the boxes drawn tight to their band (shape 30). Its coverage counter has to be **per pass**: a single `checked > 40` was met by the panel and footer alone and hid the fact that the grid pass contributed zero, which turned out to be a fixture fault rather than a code fault -- `playing(0)` is a fresh puzzle with no letters in it at all, so the pass had no runs to give (shape 31). And a `WRONG TESTS` verdict on the footer's button strip was the fix working rather than a hole in the suite: with `centre_line` in front of it an oversized strip is not drawn at all, so the failure moves from the containment tests to the *existence* tests (shape 32). Separately, the height grid gained `(900, 26)`, `(900, 18)` and `(900, 14)` -- added only to make two row-fence mutations reachable -- and at 900x18 they found an unmutated fault the twelve-size grid never had: the **menu drew its 17.29-point heading at a fixed `l.pad`**, four points down an eighteen-point window, the heading never having been in a named band at all. A size grid extended for one reason routinely pays for itself in another. Two survivors were genuine equivalent mutants and both were *proved* rather than assumed -- this font's Bold and Regular faces have identical vertical metrics, measured with a throwaway probe rather than reasoned about (which also showed the cache quantises size, so 12.6pt gives 17.29 and not the 16.76 that multiplying would predict: never compute an expected line height, probe it), and `head.bottom()` differs from the literal offset it replaced only in windows shorter than the heading band, where both produce a first row already past the fence. Both rows carry the proof, and the second was replaced by a mutation that does bind. One last addition, shape 22 in a new dress: `inked` gives a run with a zero width limit a zero-width box and an empty box is inside anything, so **a run the layout has left no room for passes containment because it has nowhere to go** -- `check_containment` now scans the command stream directly for a non-positive `max_width`.)
- [x] Word Search (apps/wordsearch: **windowed** — a board of 10, 15 or 20 cells a side laid out from whatever window it is given, words hidden in all eight directions from five categories, a running clock, five hints, and a mark you can either walk with the arrow keys or drag across with the pointer; 97 tests). Wiring it found ten faults, and the tenth is the one worth leading with because it is not a wiring fault at all: **the game could not be played.** (1) `main` built a `WordSearchApp`, bound it to `_app` and returned — no window opened, so no board reached a screen and no event ever arrived. (2) **There was no `Event::Mouse` arm anywhere in the file**, in a game whose natural gesture is to drag from a word's first letter to its last. (3) **There was no `Event::Tick` arm either**, so the clock read `00:00` for the length of every game. (4) **`format_time` was dead code with four tests and no callers**: the header formatted `MM:SS` inline with its own `format!`, so the tested function and the drawn string were two implementations of one rule and only the untested one shipped. (5) **`HintHighlight::ticks` counted down from ten and nothing decremented it**, so the renderer's `ticks > 0` could never be false and a hint stayed lit for the rest of the game. (6) **`render()` painted a background sized from the grid rather than from the window**, which is the picture-of-a-board fault this campaign keeps finding. (7) **`check_selection` checked the spelling *and* the cells** — two checks of one invariant, since the cells determine the letters. (8) **Every hint revealed the first unfound word in placement order**, so a second hint said exactly what the first had; naming the word is the pointer's gesture now, by clicking it in the list. (9) **`#![allow(dead_code)]` at the top of the file** is what let (4) and (5) ship in silence, and it is gone. (10) **`walk` sent the still axis of a straight line backwards.** It chose between counting up and counting down with `if end > start { .. } else { .. }`, and a horizontal line does not move on the row axis at all — so `walk(2, 2, i)` took the `else`, and the rows of the line from `(2, 3)` to `(2, 7)` came out `2, 1, 0, 0, 0`. Every horizontal mark below row 0 previewed as a staircase running off the top of the board, the marked cells then matched no placed word, and **no word lying along any row or column but the first could be marked at all.** `saturating_sub` is what made it look plausible instead of crashing: the wrong answer was clamped into a legal coordinate. It is three `Ordering` arms now, and it is `known-issues.md` lesson 53 — *an `else` is a two-way choice, and some choices have three ways*. Two further deletions were reasoned out before the sweep by asking what state each guard exists to catch and whether the public API can reach it: `word_between` skipped words already found (two distinct words cannot occupy the same cells, and `mark_found` is idempotent, so the skip could suppress nothing), and a parallel `found_cells: Vec<(usize, usize)>` was a second copy of a fact the words already carry — `is_found_cell` is derived from them now, which is the same reasoning that makes the clock milliseconds and the seconds computed. 103 deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/wordsearch/mutate.py`), fault 10 restored literally. The first sweep ran 105 and caught 100, and the five survivors sorted into two piles, neither of them "the sweep was wrong". **Two were unreachable guards and both are deleted** (`known-issues.md` lesson 51): `generate_puzzle` skipped a word longer than the board, which `try_place_word` already declines identically — down to the random stream, because `rng.below(0)` returns without drawing — and `hint_for` refused on `status == Won`, which the `placed.found` test two lines below already covers, since `Won` is set in one place and means every word is found. **Three were blind tests**, two of them the shapes `known-issues.md` lesson 52 names. `assert_ne!(a.words(), b.words())` treats inequality as distinctness, so a colour list with fifteen animals pasted onto the front of it passed as loudly as ever; the claim is a fact from outside now — all five lists are thirty words long. The greying test looked only at the *after* of finding a word, so `is_found_cell` losing its `w.found &&` — which paints every hidden word green on the first frame, the whole puzzle solved on sight — changed nothing it watched; it asserts the before as well. And the seeding test was built entirely out of `with_seed`, so `seed ^ 1` kept equal seeds equal and unequal seeds unequal and walked straight through; it is pinned by one literal board now, deliberately brittle, because "the same seed gives the same puzzle across builds" is the entire content of the word *seeded*. One incidental fix: `apps/wordsearch/src/main.rs` was the only file under `apps/` with CRLF line endings, which is why 45 of the sweep's anchors matched nothing on the first run. Window-wiring baseline 85 -> 84.
- [x] Wordle (apps/wordle: **windowed** — guess a hidden word in six tries with every guess answered letter by letter, four-, five- and six-letter puzzles, an on-screen keyboard that remembers what each letter has been shown to be, a hard mode that refuses a guess contradicting what you have already been told, keyboard and pointer, a running played/won/streak/best counter, and a translucent panel over a finished game offering the way on; 94 tests). Wiring it found nine faults on top of the missing window, and the first two are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = Wordle::new();`** — it seeded a generator from the system clock, drew a target word, built a grid and a keyboard and dropped the lot, so no puzzle ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **The layout was a constant, and the click map was a second copy of the constant.** `render` laid the on-screen keyboard out from `kb_y_start = 420.0`, `key_w = 36.0`, `key_h = 40.0`, `gap = 4.0`, a left edge of `80.0` and row offsets of `18.0`/`36.0`, put Enter at `x: 80.0, width: 32.0` and Backspace at `80.0 + 36.0 + 7.0 * (key_w + gap) + 36.0`; `handle_keyboard_click` wrote every one of those literals out again and compared the click against its copy rather than against the ink — a rule kept by copying (`known-issues.md` lesson 63), agreeing only for as long as nobody touched one of them. Neither copy was a rule about the window: `render(width, height)` used `width` to centre the title and the grid and used `height` for nothing at all, so the board began at a fixed `y = 70` and the keyboard at a fixed `y = 420`, and on any window shorter than about 545 pixels the keyboard was drawn past the bottom edge and there was no other way to reach it. The layout is solved from the live window size every frame now and the hit boxes are recorded by the drawing pass, so a key is clickable exactly where its ink is. (3) **Every key would have fired twice.** The handler destructured `Event::Key(KeyEvent { key, modifiers, .. })` and never read `pressed`, so the release ran it again: every letter typed itself twice per press, `Enter` submitted the guess and then submitted the empty row behind it, `N` dealt two words and threw the first away, and `1`/`2`/`3` set the length twice. (4) **The pointer could type and nothing else.** `handle_keyboard_click` is the whole mouse route, and it tests the three letter rows, Enter and Backspace — so changing the word length, turning hard mode and dealing a new word were keyboard-only, and the difficulty was not a control at all but an *indicator*, the string `"Normal (5)"` drawn under the title. Hard mode was an indicator too, and only when it was on: a switch whose off state is drawn as nothing. There was no footer, no help and no hint, so the only record that `1`, `2`, `3`, `H`, `N` and `Esc` existed was the source. (5) **`Difficulty::label` spelled the word length a second time** — `"Easy (4)"`, `"Normal (5)"`, `"Hard (6)"` — in a place no test would ever compare against `word_len`, which is the same lesson 63 as fault 2 in four characters instead of forty lines. `name()` gives the name and the button composes it with the length it deals. (6) **`Difficulty::max_guesses(self)` took a receiver and returned `6` without looking at it** — a signature advertising a rule that varies per difficulty when it does not, so every caller had to read the body to learn that the four-letter puzzle is not more generous than the six-letter one. It is a `const` now, and a test asserts every difficulty allows the same six. (7) **Text was positioned by guessing its own width, and nothing was ever told where to stop.** The title sat at `width / 2.0 - 40.0` — a claim that "WORDLE" at 28px is exactly 80 wide — the difficulty at `width / 2.0 - 30.0`, the "HARD" flag at `width - 100.0`, each key's letter at `kx + key_w / 2.0 - 6.0`, "ENT" at `+2.0` and "DEL" at `+6.0`; and every `RenderCommand::Text` in the file carried `max_width: None`, so the counter drawn at `x = 10` and the "HARD" flag drawn from the right edge ran through one another on a narrow window with nothing to stop either. Every string is placed from `text::measure` now and limited to the box that decided its centre, so the width that picks the position is the width the renderer stops at. (8) **Twelve blanket `#![allow(...)]` at the crate root**, `dead_code` and `unused_imports` among them — which is what let a program whose `main` discarded its own app compile without a word of complaint. (9) **Letters have to be read from the key, not from the text.** The compositor's `handle_text_input` is `fn handle_text_input(&mut self, _text: &str) {}` (`gui/compositor/src/lib.rs`), so `KeyEvent::text` arrives empty from a real window: a Wordle that read the text field would have accepted no letters at all while every test that built its own event passed. The mutation sweep then found six more, and five of them are one mistake — a test whose title claims a placement while its body asserts only an interval (lesson 68). (10) **"The grid is centred in what is left over" was in a test's name and not in its body.** It proved the tiles fill the board and that the board is square; nothing said where the board *is*, so pushing the grid to the top-left corner of the area the bands leave moved all thirty tiles and the suite stayed green. It asserts the two centres now — across the window, and midway between the header and the message, which is exact because the padding above and below that strip is the same. (11) **"No two tiles overlap" is satisfied by tiles that touch.** Stepping by the tile instead of the tile plus its gap butts every tile against the next — one solid block with no letter legible in it — and the test that owns tile placement said nothing, because not-overlapping is an inequality with the whole gap as slack. It asserts the separation *is* the gap now, horizontally and vertically. (12) **"Nothing is drawn outside the window" inspected rectangles only.** A string centred by a width measured without regard to the box it is centred in starts at a negative x and is drawn where nobody can read it, which is outside the window in the one way the test named for that rule could not see. It checks text origins too — origins rather than origins-plus-limits, because a limit is measured from the box and not from where the string begins, so the sum legitimately reaches past the box. (13) **The keyboard's clamp binds only below about 26 pixels of window height,** which no fixture reached, so the one band that shrinks rather than vanishing could be made to take what it asked for — growing up through the message, through the header and out of the top of the window — with every test still passing. The shrink test sweeps every height from 10 to 400 now and asserts the keyboard never starts above the top edge.
