(14) **No window shape was narrow enough for the footer to be crowded.** The counter is right-aligned from a width clamped to the room the band has; with no shape where the counter wants more than there is, the clamp could be deleted and the two footer strings still did not collide. `140x900` — tall enough to keep every band, narrow enough that the counter wants twice the room it has — is in the list of shapes now. (15) **A mutation was named against a test that cannot see it:** dividing the keyboard band by three rather than by three-rows-and-two-gaps was expected to be caught by the overlap test, but each row is drawn from its own step, so the rows do not overlap — they overrun the foot of the band, which is the band test's rule and not the overlap test's. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/wordle/mutate.py` breaks 75 pieces of the program one at a time, and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. Window-wiring baseline 76 -> 75.
- [x] Pipes (apps/pipes: **windowed** — pipe-rotation puzzle at three sizes, keyboard and pointer, a help sheet, a flow overlay, and a floor on the turns still to make; 54 tests). Wiring it found eleven faults on top of the missing window, and the first of them is the whole game. (1) `main` built a `PipesApp`, dropped it and exited: no pipe ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **The generator had never once dealt a solvable board.** `generate` walks a path from the source to the drain and lays pipe along it; the openings it asked each cell for were wrong. `prev_dir` was `dir_from(prev, here)` — the direction the walk *travelled to arrive*, which points **away** from the previous cell — where the opening the cell needs points back *toward* it. A straight run therefore asked for "Right and Right", which the two-opening branch read as non-opposite and fitted a **corner**. Measured over 2000 seeds at each of the three sizes before the fix: the walk reached the drain 2000/2000 times and the board laid from it was connected **0** times. Every puzzle the program ever dealt was solvable only by whatever accident the random fill left behind — and nothing in the app or its 80-odd tests could tell, because no test ever asked whether the laid solution joined up, and no player could report it because no player could reach it. Two tests now do ask, across 400 seeds × 3 levels (the laid solution connects) and 200 × 3 (the dealt board is solvable at all). (3) **There was no pointer input.** `handle_event` matched `Event::Key` and nothing else, so a mouse could not turn a pipe; every cell and every control is clickable now, left clockwise and right anticlockwise, from hit boxes the drawing pass records so the hit test cannot drift from the ink. (4) **The layout was a constant.** `render(width, height)` used its two arguments for the background rectangle and nothing else: cells were `50.0.min(400.0 / cols)` at a fixed (20, 95) — capped at 400 pixels wide however large the window — and the victory message drew *below* the board at `95 + rows * cell + 15`, which on Hard is past the bottom of any window shorter than about 570 pixels. Winning is exactly when you most want to be told you won. (5) **The controls line named five of the seven keys it answered**: `F` (the flow overlay) and `Enter` (a synonym for Space) were answered and unmentioned. A test walks the sheet both ways — every key it names answers, every key that answers is named. (6) **`PipeKind::Cross` could not occur.** The fill drew `rng.below(5)` and mapped `0..=3` to Straight, Corner, Tee and End with `_ => Straight`, so the fifth outcome — the one arm that would have made a cross — produced a second straight. The kind existed, drew correctly and was tested; it was simply unreachable. (7) **`pipe_for_openings` ignored *which* three openings it was given**: the three-opening arm was `_ => (PipeKind::Tee, 0)` whatever sides were asked for. Nothing on the laid path has three openings today, so it never bit — a latent wrong answer sitting behind the one that was actually wrong. It now searches every kind × rotation for the set that matches exactly, one rule in place of four hand-written arms. (8) **A board could be dealt already finished**: the scramble turned each cell a random number of quarter-turns and checked nothing, so a board that came out solved was presented as a puzzle and the first turn you made broke it. It reshuffles now, a *bounded* number of times, because a board that cannot be unsolved must not turn the guard into an unbounded loop — and each attempt scrambles the laid solution afresh rather than scrambling the previous attempt again, since turning a board twice more is not another draw from the same distribution but a walk away from one. (9) **A turn that changed nothing counted a move.** A cross turns onto itself and a straight repeats every second turn; both incremented the counter the win message reports. Turning a shape that cannot change is now refused, and says which shape and why rather than going silent. (10) **Ten blanket `#![allow]`s sat on lines 6-15**, `dead_code` among them, which is what kept 6 and 11 invisible. (11) **`let _half = (cell_size - 2.0) / 2.0;`** was computed once per cell per frame and read by nothing. One thing was added rather than fixed: nothing said how close you were — the turn counter only goes up — so there is now a floor on the turns remaining, computed as a shortest path over `(cell, the side the water entered by)` states that charges each cell, as the search leaves it, the fewest rotations opening it on both the side it was entered by and the side it is left by. Any real solution contains some source-to-drain run of joined cells and each cell of that run has been turned at least that many times, so no sequence of turns can beat it; when it is `u32::MAX` no rotation could ever join the two, which is what `is_solvable` now asks. Twenty-five deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/pipes/mutate.py`), the original generator bug among them, restored verbatim by swapping the arguments of `dir_between` — and worth recording is which test does *not* catch it: `every_puzzle_dealt_can_be_solved` passes against the restored bug, because a board whose laid solution is nonsense is still usually *solvable*, the random fill leaving enough pipe about that some rotation joins the two ends by accident. That is exactly how the bug survived eighty-odd tests: solvability is the property everyone thinks to check and it is the one the bug does not break. Five mutants survived the first sweep and every one was a weak test rather than a spare guard. (a) `water_reaches_a_cell_only_by_a_run_of_joins_from_the_source` asked `Board::joined` where two cells met and then checked the flood had gone exactly where `joined` said — self-consistent, and green however wrong `joined` is; it now spells the condition out from `openings()` and floods independently, comparing cell for cell. (b) `the_bands_go_in_the_stated_order` built its expectation by reversing `BAND_DROP_ORDER`, the constant under test, so it agreed with whatever that constant happened to say and passed on every order; the order is written out literally now, with the constant checked against it. (c) the modal test clicked the middle of the board, which is *under* the sheet, so a hit box covering only the sheet's own pixels passed it while every control round the edges went on answering invisible clicks; it now walks every control the frame records and clicks each one's centre. (d) the scramble's retry and its bound were both untestable through `generate`, because a 6x6 scramble lands solved approximately never — the suite passed with the retry deleted and never ran the bound at all; `deal` is split out so a two-cell board (which reassembles about one time in sixteen) exercises the retry and a 1x1 board (its own source and drain, so no rotation can unsolve it) exercises the bound, the latter caught by the suite hanging — and the mutation for the guard has to be "take the first scramble whatever it is", because *deleting* the condition leaves the loop running all eight rounds and returning the last, a fresh scramble that is almost never solved, which is an almost-equivalent mutant surviving on its own merits rather than through a weak test. (e) `distinct_rotations` is only ever read through `> 1`, so raising a straight's 2 to a 4 is an equivalent mutant; it was replaced by a cross claiming four faces, which reaches behaviour. A sixth candidate was dropped as provably equivalent rather than papered over: reading the board's top from `info.bottom()` instead of the accumulated band heights is only safe because `BAND_DROP_ORDER` drops the header before the info line, so no test can tell the two apart today; the safe form stays in the source with that reasoning at the line, and `mutate.py` records why it is not a mutation. Window-wiring baseline 90 -> 89.
- [x] Breakout (apps/breakout: **windowed** — brick-breaking game with paddle, ball physics, angle-based reflection, power-ups, level progression, 3 lives, 134 tests). Wiring it found five faults. Four needed the game to be playable to see: `main` built a `BreakoutApp` and dropped it, so the ball never moved and no key ever arrived; `render_game` ignored both of its size arguments and drew a constant 600x500 play area at a fixed offset, with the header and menu text at absolute coordinates, so the game was the same size in every window and fell off the small ones; `handle_tick` turned `elapsed_ms` straight into distance with no cap and no sub-stepping, so a window that had been frozen played out its whole freeze in one frame and a 500 unit/s ball passed clean through an 18-unit brick row; and nothing was clickable in the archetypal mouse game. The fifth was latent: `update_powerups` collected "caught" and "fell off" indices against the vector as it was, then deleted from both lists, so a power-up caught above one that had fallen removed the wrong entry. `Layout` now fits the play area into the live window at a single aspect-preserving scale and drops the button row rather than the game when the window is cramped, the pointer steers the paddle, and the message panel and footer buttons do what their text says.
- [x] Sokoban (apps/sokoban: **windowed** — fifteen warehouses of crates and targets, keyboard and pointer, undo with a visible depth, move and push counters, a level menu that remembers what you have finished, a translucent victory panel offering the three ways on, and every cell and every menu row a hit box so the pointer plays the game on its own; 107 tests). Wiring it found twelve faults on top of the missing window, and the first two are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = SokobanApp::new();`** — it parsed fifteen levels, loaded the first, dropped the lot and exited, so no warehouse ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **The layout was a constant, and the window was told to match it.** `render(&self)` took no size at all; `window_width`/`window_height` *computed* the window from `CELL_SIZE = 48.0` and the level's dimensions, so the program drew whatever picture it liked and expected the window to be that shape. Every offset below — `grid_origin_x`, `cell_screen_x`, the header bar, the 400x-something menu panel — was a constant plus a constant. The layout is computed from the live window size every frame now and the hit boxes are recorded by the drawing pass, so a square is clickable exactly where its ink is. (3) **The pointer did nothing at all.** `MouseEvent`, `MouseButton` and `MouseEventKind` were imported and never used, and `handle_event` matched `Event::Key` and dropped everything else — leaving a fifteen-row level menu, the one screen in the program that is a list of things to click, keyboard-only. (4) **`#![allow(dead_code)]` and `#![allow(unused_imports)]` sat at the top of the file**; with `main` discarding the app most of the program was dead and three imports were unused, and the two allows are what let it compile without ever saying so. (5) **The module doc described a program that did not exist** — it advertised "level completion celebration, and auto-advance to the next level", but nothing advanced and the celebration was `celebration_ticks = 120`, a countdown written twice, decremented by nothing and read by nothing. A doc comment is an assertion nobody checks; this one was false in three places at once. (6) **"Outside the warehouse" was walkable.** `is_wall` asked only whether a tile was `Tile::Wall`, and `Tile::Empty` — the tile the parser produced for every unrecognised character *and* for every cell padding a short row out to the full width — is not `Tile::Wall`, so the padding around an irregular level was floor the player could step onto and push crates into. It never bit only because every built-in level happens to be fenced where its rows are ragged, which is a property of the table and not of the program. `Empty` is impassable now, like off-grid. (7) **`parse_level` had no failure case.** Any character it did not know became `Tile::Empty` and was forgotten; a level with no `@` silently started the player at (0, 0); a second `@` silently won over the first; and nothing compared crates to targets, so a level with three crates and two targets parsed, loaded, played and could not be solved. Parsing returns a `Result` now, the table is validated at startup, and a test asserts all fifteen pass — a typo in the table is a failing test rather than an unwinnable puzzle. (8) **`MAX_LEVEL_WIDTH` and `MAX_LEVEL_HEIGHT` were declared and never read** — a bound nothing enforces is a bound the program does not have; they are what `parse_level` now rejects oversized levels against. (9) **The win was a latch, and undo could not reach it.** Solving pushed the program to `Screen::Won`, whose key handler has no undo, so the winning move was the one move you could not take back — in a game whose entire failure mode is pushing a crate one square too far. Winning is derived from where the crates are now, the victory panel is an overlay on the board rather than a separate screen, and undoing the winning move un-wins because there is no second fact left to disagree with the board. (10) **The victory panel was placed at fixed offsets from a fixed box.** The scrim's alpha was real, but the 320x150 box, its stats and eight decorative dots were laid out in literal pixels from it, so in a window smaller than that the celebration was drawn off the edge of the thing it was celebrating. The panel is sized from the window now and every string in it placed from its own measured width. (11) **Text was positioned by guessing its own width** — the menu's completion marks at `PADDING + 8.0` and its level names at `PADDING + 60.0`, two guesses at how wide `[done]` renders, and the victory lines at `+20`, `+58` and `+90` — in a program that already linked `guitk::text`. Every string is placed from `text::measure` now, and every centred string is limited to the box it is centred in, so the width that decides the centre is the width the renderer stops at. (12) **The undo cap dropped the oldest move by shuffling the vector** — `undo_stack.remove(0)` per move past `MAX_UNDO`. It is a `VecDeque` now, and the header shows the depth so the loss is visible rather than silent. (13) **`Escape` on the menu was a match arm that did nothing,** under a comment saying so; an arm that exists to be empty is a line no test can own (lesson 51), and the key simply is not ours on that screen now. Writing the tests found two more in the new code. (14) **A right-aligned string was bounded on one side only**: the first `label_right` placed text at `right - measure(text)` with no width limit — the mirror image of fault 11, guessing nothing but assuming unlimited room — and in a 170-pixel window the header's `Pushes: … Crates: … Undo: …` counter started at x = -42, its right end exactly where it was asked to be and the rest of it off the screen. It is clamped to the band's left edge and elided there now. (15) **A validity check the notation cannot fail**: `parse_level` ended by rejecting a level whose player or crates stood on an unwalkable tile, but `@`, `+`, `$` and `*` each *write* the tile beneath them, and the `Empty` padding of a ragged row only lands past the characters that row has — so no level could enter the arm and no test could own it. The check is gone and the invariant is asserted over the whole table instead. The mutation sweep then found four more, and all four are the same mistake twice over — a guard standing in front of a rule that already holds (lesson 51), or one rule kept in two places (lesson 63). (16) **Every band was special-cased to `Rect::EMPTY` when it was dropped**, behind five separate `if h > 0.0` arms: one each for the header, the footer and the controls, plus a `lower` and a `bottom` that read a fallback when the band below them might not be there. Not one of the five is observable. `Rect::is_empty` is `w <= 0.0 || h <= 0.0`, so a full-width strip nought pixels tall already answers "no" to the only question any drawing code asks, and every read of a band's `x`/`w`/`right()` happens after that bail. The strip form is also what makes the two edges fall out for free — a dropped footer sits at `y = h` and a dropped controls band at `y = lower`, which is exactly where the body should stop, whereas `Rect::EMPTY` sits at the origin and had to be guarded against putting the body's bottom edge at zero. Three unconditional `Rect::new` calls replace all five arms. (17) **`button_rects` bailed three times and needed none of them** — on `controls.is_empty()`, on `n == 0`, and on a zero `bw`/`bh`. An empty band gives a zero-height button, which is already empty; with `n == 0` the loop runs no times, so the `inf` that `inner / 0.0` produces is never read. What the sweep did find under those guards was a real hole: nothing asserted the buttons are *centred* in their band, only that they stay inside it, so pinning them to its top edge moved every button and no test noticed. (18) **The level bound was checked twice.** `start_level` repeated the bounds test `load_level` already did, so `load_level`'s own bail could be replaced by a silent wrap back to the last level and no test could see the difference — the second guard was standing in front of the first. `load_level` answers `bool` now and owns the rule alone; `start_level` asks it rather than repeating it. (19) **A centred string was clamped twice** — `.min(r.w)` on the measured width *and* `.max(r.x)` on the resulting x. Either alone is the whole fix, so each masked the other from the sweep: with `w` never wider than `r.w`, `(r.w - w) / 2.0` is never negative and the `.max` was an arm nothing could enter. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/sokoban/mutate.py` breaks 120 pieces of the program one at a time, and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. **Revisited 2026-09-01 for C-CENTRING-IS-NOT-A-BOUND** (lesson 109), which is why the counts above are now 112 tests and 120 mutation rows. The grep predicted eight unbounded-centring sites; the app had eleven faults, and three of them were found by reading rather than by the sweep. (a) **The mat under the grid was a bare offset, and it overran the body by a gap.** `cell` is solved so that the *grid* fills the body exactly, and then the mat was drawn a gap wider than the thing that already filled it — on whichever axis the cell size came from, the crust ran a gap past the body's edge. The solve reserves `cols + 1` and `rows + 1` gaps now, and the mat is a named `Layout::board_frame` rather than four arithmetic terms inside `draw_board`, because a region no test can name is a region no test checks a pass against. (b) **The header's one-line fallback was the unbounded branch.** `two_lines` is itself a fit check, so the two-line stack was bounded by the thing that chose it — but falling back to one line does not make one line fit, and the header wants `(h * 0.11).clamp(26.0, 58.0)` while `big` clamps at 28, whose bold line height is taller than 26. A 260-point-tall window drew "Sokoban" above the bar it is supposed to sit in. (c) **Two guards stood in front of rules that already held** and are gone: the footer's `lh > footer.h` bail, which is exactly what `centre_line` answers for `shown == 1`, and `label_centred`'s `r.is_empty()`, which `centre_line` refuses outright. A third, `label_right`'s `(right - w).max(left)`, is dominated by the `.min(room)` one line above it — `w <= room` implies `right - w >= left`, so the `max` can never fire — and a clamp no test can reach reads as coverage and is not. Then the containment sweep found six more, and five of the six are one mistake: **a bound measured from the box rather than from where the run starts.** (d) **`label_centred` handed the renderer `r.w`**: a "Play" 27 points wide, centred in a 136-point button, was told it could fill 136 points starting 54 past the button's left edge, and ran 54 points off the end of the band the button sits in. `label_right` (e) had the same fault against the very edge it aligns to. Both pass `right - x` now. (f) **`label` had no width limit at all** — "Sokoban" at 28 points ran 110 points wide out of a band 1 point wide. It is deleted; `push_text`'s limit is an `f32` rather than an `Option<f32>`, so there is no way left in the file to spell "unbounded", and the header is split into a named title column and a named counter column so the title takes what the counters leave. (g) **A stroke straddles the line it is drawn on**, so the victory card's 2-point border painted at y = -0.71 in a card flush with the panel's top. `stroke` insets by half the line width now and has `fill`'s contract: `r` is the rectangle the ink stays inside. (h) **The confetti clamped its origin, not its dot** — `dy.clamp(0.0, (window.h - d).max(0.0))` pins the top-left corner to zero in a window shorter than a dot and then draws `d` points of it anyway, so a 1-point-tall window got a full 2x2 dot. The pass refuses a window too small for one dot outright and clamps window-relative. (i) **The menu cursor's stripe has a one-point floor that does not scale**: `(pad * 0.5).max(1.0)` is 1.7 points wide in a row 1 point wide. It is `.intersect(r)` now, which bounds all four sides and answers `None` for a row with no room — the same `Option` shape as `centre_line`. (j) **The "done" mark was drawn unlimited** on the grounds that it is the string the gutter was measured from; in a row narrower than the gutter it ran past the row. It is bounded by the gutter. The test side is hangman's: `painted`/`inked`/`inside`/`check_containment` over a `PASSES` table, `squeezes()` sweeping sixteenths as well as absolute slivers, `no_pass_paints_outside_the_region_it_owns` restricted to the five bands that are *inputs* to their pass (the board's is derived from the body and the solved cell, and the test says so), and `a_pass_with_room_paints_and_a_pass_with_none_paints_nothing` as the converse — because containment is satisfied by a pass that draws nothing at all. The sweep over the finished suite then faulted six rows, and none of the six was a missing bound: four were guards standing in front of rules that already held, and two were places containment is structurally blind. The guards went — `draw_header`, `draw_controls` and `draw_footer` each opened with an `if l.<band>.is_empty() { return; }` that `fill`'s own refusal, `button_rects`' empty rectangles and `centre_line` had all already made unreachable, and `draw_list`'s `centre_line` is dominated by the floor on `Layout::row` (`2.1 * font` beats `max(7, 0.44 * r.h)` everywhere the clamps can reach; the tightest case is a 14.4-point row against a 9.5-point line). `draw_list` **keeps** its band guard, and the comment now says why: `f.clip` pushes a command whether or not the rectangle has area, so a pass that clips before it has refused the band cannot be silent about a band it was never given. Each of the four rows is re-pointed at the bound that does the work — `fill`'s refusal, `Layout::row`'s floor, `centre_line` returning `None`, and the order of the footer's refusal and its clip. The two blind spots got tests of their own: `the_headers_title_stops_where_its_counters_begin`, because a title handed the whole band does not leave the band, it runs *under* the counters, and `no_run_is_pushed_into_a_box_with_no_room`, because a run told it may fill nought points measures as an empty rectangle and an empty rectangle is inside everything. And the footer's own bounding test was asserting `max.is_some()` — a real check while "unbounded" was spelled `None`, and vacuous the moment `push_text` started taking an `f32`; it asserts the limit's value against the band now.
- [x] Gomoku (apps/gomoku: **windowed** -- a 15x15 board sized from whatever window it is given, five in a row in any of four directions, an opponent whose search runs on a tick so that "White is thinking" is a state a frame can be drawn in, a coordinate margin and an information panel that are each dropped whole when the window cannot pay for them, and a cursor you can walk with the arrows or put anywhere with one click; 78 tests). Wiring it found nine faults. (1) **`main` was `let _app = GomokuApp::new();`** -- it built the opening board, dropped it and exited, so no stone ever reached a screen and no event ever arrived. (2) **`handle_key` never read `pressed`**, so every keystroke did its work twice: the arrows stepped two intersections, which on a 15x15 board leaves every other row and every other column unreachable from the keyboard entirely; `N` dealt two games; `Z` took back two pairs. (3) **`render(&self) -> Vec<RenderCommand>` took no width and no height at all** and painted a background of `PANEL_X + 220.0` by a constant height into every window there was. (4) **The board was drawn from `CELL_SIZE = 36.0` and two offset constants, and the click was resolved by `intersection_near`, a free function of the same three** -- so the arithmetic agreed with itself in every window and with the picture in exactly one. This is the fault the whole rewrite turns on, and it is why `every_intersection_is_clickable_where_it_is_drawn` measures all 225 boxes against `Layout::intersection` at six window sizes rather than clicking by name. (5) **Nothing recorded a hit box** -- there was no `Frame` -- so the click handler answered from arithmetic the picture might not have been drawn from. (6) **`handle_mouse` returned early unless it was Black's turn in a live game**, which made New game and Undo keyboard-only at exactly the moment a player most wants them: after a win, the pointer could not start the next game. (7) **`ai_move` ran synchronously inside `place_stone`**, so `GamePhase::Thinking` was a variant no frame was ever drawn in and "White is thinking" was a string the player could never see. The search runs on `Event::Tick` now, and `the_frame_after_blacks_move_says_white_is_thinking` plays a move and looks at the frame that follows it, rather than setting the phase by hand. (8) **The White-win score was credited twice over** -- `place_stone` matched on the stone and `ai_move` had its own copy that hard-coded `scores.1`, which is the same answer only for as long as nothing else calls it. Both colours settle through one `place_stone` now. (9) **Twelve crate-level `#![allow]`s**, including `dead_code` and `unused_imports`, and beneath them eleven array indexings and forty-three unchecked integer operations -- the crate had simply never been built with the lane gate on. Every read of the board goes through a bounds-checked `get(row: i32, col: i32) -> Option<Cell>` now, which is the accessor rather than a guard bolted onto one: the win search walks lines until they leave the board on purpose, so "off the board" is an answer it needs. One test was deleted rather than written: `every_frame_is_balanced` asserts that every clip pushed is popped, and gomoku pushes none, so it was a claim the program could not fail. The mutation sweep found one more fault, in the tests rather than in the program: the `says` helper searched the whole frame for a string, and the header and the status band both say "White is thinking", so a mutant that broke the band left both tests naming it green -- assertions about a band now name that band's `Rect` (lesson 91). It found a tenth fault in the program: **the phase was tested twice**, once by the `Event::Tick` arm to pick `Consumed` over `Ignored` and once at the top of the `think` it calls, and since `think` is private with one call site the inner copy could not be reached -- deleting it changed nothing the suite could see. `think` returns whether a search ran and the tick arm is answered by that, which also stops the arm claiming `Consumed` for a tick in which nothing happened (lesson 92). And it found a hole in the undo tests: `take_back` only lifts a stone of the colour asked for, but every fixture's history either held one move or ended in White, and on those "lift the top stone" and "lift this colour's stone" agree -- they part only on a history of two or more ending in Black, which is a win Black finished, so `undoing_blacks_win_does_not_also_take_back_whites_last_reply` was written to enter that regime (lesson 90). Window-wiring baseline 62 -> 61.
- [x] Battleship (apps/battleship: **windowed** — the 10x10 naval game with a hunt/target AI, a ship-placement phase, hit/miss/sink marks, a six-figure stats band and both grids clickable, all fitted to the live window size every frame; 126 tests). Wiring it found fifteen faults, the missing window among them. (1) **`main` was `let _app = BattleshipApp::new();`** — it placed the AI's five ships, drew a board into a buffer nobody displayed and dropped the lot, so no grid ever reached a screen and no key ever arrived. (2) **`render(&self)` took no size**: every coordinate was a `const f32` off a `CELL_SIZE` of 36, so the two boards were the size they were whatever window they were in, and a window shorter than the one the constants were written for ran its grids off the bottom. A `Layout` solved from `(w, h)` every frame now carries five bands and a `Grids` that fits two labelled boards into the body from *both* axes. (3) **The module doc said the seed came from the system "so that two players do not get the same game"; `new` wrote `SeededRng::new(0xDEAD_BEEF_CAFE_1234)`** — one constant, so the AI's five ships stood in the same five places on every machine and in every session for ever. The doc was right about the intent and wrong about the code, which is the worse of the two ways for them to disagree. (4) **The grid captions were centred by hand-tuned half-widths** — 40 for "Your Fleet", 55 for "Opponent's Ocean" — so the two were centred to different standards and neither survived a change of font size; they are measured now. (5) **The row and column labels were nudged by `- 4.0`, `- 7.0` and `+ 2.0`**, three guesses at the middle of a glyph. (6) **There was no pointer handling at all**, on a 10x10 grid — the most click-natural thing a program can put on a screen — so every cell was reachable only by walking a cursor to it with the arrow keys. Every cell of both boards, both mats, both captions and every band of chrome is a hit box recorded by the drawing pass now. (7) **`Key::Escape` carried "Could quit, but we just reset for now"**: the key a player presses to back out of something threw away the game in progress, from any phase, silently. Escape closes the window; `N` alone deals a new board. (8) **`clamp_placement()` was called on Down and Right and not on Up and Left**, harmless only because those two happen to move away from the edge the clamp guards — a coincidence, not a reason, and one a click placing the cursor anywhere at all destroys. (9) **`ai_turn` had an `if hit { "Hit!" } else { … }` inside a branch reached only when `!hit`** — an arm that could not be taken. (10) **`place_ai_ships` checked bounds and overlap, called `place_ship` which re-checked both, and discarded the `bool` saying whether the placement it had just made actually happened** — two copies of the rule with the authoritative one thrown away. (11) **`SHIP_DEFS` was a second copy of `ShipKind::size()`**, and the opening prompt spelled the Carrier and its size as literals in two more places, so a change to the fleet would have left the game naming a ship it no longer starts with. (12) **Pervasive `[r][c]` indexing** on grids reached with cursor arithmetic, including a neighbour scan that subtracted 1 from row 0. (13) **Twelve blanket `#![allow(...)]`**, `unused_imports` and `dead_code` among them. They are gone. (14) An earlier session's finding, guarded by a test here: **the local LCG's `state % max` made `row + col` always odd**, anchoring the AI's entire fleet to one colour of the checkerboard. (15) **`randrange`'s `seed_from_system` fallback was the very constant fault (3) used**, so on any machine with no kernel randomness to open the ships still stood exactly where the bug put them — the fault moved rather than fixed, and invisible to every test run off Slate OS. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/battleship/mutate.py` breaks 63 pieces of the program one at a time and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. Fourteen were not, on the first sweep, and they sorted into five kinds worth keeping apart. **Three were unreachable guards in the production code** — equivalent mutants whose value is that deleting them changed nothing, which is the definition of dead code. `pad` was capped at half the shorter side after being set to two percent of it, and two percent of a length is always under half of it; the cap has a real `clamp(2.0, 24.0)` under it now, so a 60-pixel window keeps a margin and a 4K one is not margined by thirty, and the cap can bind. The other two were the band arithmetic's `max` guards: each band took a share of *what remained*, so the five heights could never sum past the window, come out negative or fall out of order — at 150 pixels tall the four fixed bands total 44. `Layout::solve` says that outright now, a running remainder with the body taking what is left, and needs no guards at all. **Four were tests asking the wrong thing.** The cell-geometry test took its expected rectangle from `cell_rect`, the function under test, so swapping the row and the column inside it — which on a square board transposes every cell — agreed with itself and passed; the expectation is worked out in the test now. The Escape test asked `key_at`, which reaches `handle_key`, and `handle_key` has had no Escape arm since the reset came out of it — so the reset could come back in `on_event` and the test aimed at it would not see it. The label test asserted the 1-10 row sits at or below the caption, which a layout reserving *no* caption line satisfies at exact equality, the caption and the label being the same height. And the board test never checked that the mat reaches its last cell: measured in drawn squares rather than whole steps it stops six percent short and the far corner of a grid answers as nothing at all. **Three were simply uncovered**: that the band heights are shares of the *height* (giving the header `w * 0.075` passed everything, because the four bands below it derive from it and still tile the window); that an unused key is left alone in the *placement* arm, the three phases routing a key through three arms and the test having pressed one; and the clamp after a **rotation**, the one move that carries a ship's far end off the board without the cursor going anywhere. **One was a redundant guard**: the phase check on a click on one's own grid is unreachable while `phase` and `placement_index` agree, and nothing has ever set them apart — so the test sets them apart, which is exactly the case the guard defends. **And one was a fault that had only moved**, fault (15) above: the mutation putting the hardcoded seed back survived because the fallback *was* the hardcoded seed. Window-wiring baseline 69 -> 68.
- [x] Game of Life (apps/life: **windowed** — an 80x60 toroidal board fitted to whatever window it is given, ten preset patterns behind a modal sheet, play/pause/step, nine speeds, grid lines, a cursor, pointer and keyboard editing, and a help sheet; 81 tests). Wiring it found eleven faults on top of the missing window, and the first of them is the plainest statement this campaign has produced of what an unwired `main` costs: **the program could not run one generation of Conway's Game of Life.** (1) **Every key ran twice, on the press and again on the release.** Both key handlers destructured `KeyEvent { key, .. }` and the field the `..` swallowed was `pressed`; the compositor really does send a key-up for every key-down. Space is `self.running = !self.running`, so it started the simulation and stopped it again between one frame and the next — the whole point of the program, unreachable. The rest of the keyboard is the same fault in seven more shapes: `Enter` toggled a cell twice, which is not at all; `F1` and `G` had no state in which their feature was on; `S` advanced two generations under the name of one; each arrow moved two cells; `R` drew a soup, threw it away and drew another; and **placing a pattern flipped one of the pattern's own cells back off** — the menu's `Enter` placed the figure *and* set `view = Board`, so the release reached the board handler's `Enter` and toggled the cell under the cursor, which is the origin every pattern is stamped from. Blinker and Beacon, the two patterns containing `(0, 0)`, arrived one cell short and were therefore not the figures they were named for. (2) **The clock was correct and never arrived.** `tick_accum`, `speed_ms` and the catch-up loop were all present, all right, and all under test; there was no `impl App`, so no `tick_interval`, so no `Event::Tick`. This is `known-issues.md` lesson 47's eighth application and the first in which the timekeeping itself was already sound — the version of that fault that survives a code review, since nothing in the function being read is wrong. (3) **The catch-up loop was unbounded.** `while self.tick_accum >= interval` at speed 9 (15 ms) turns a ten-second stall into 666 sweeps of 4800 cells in the frame the window comes back, which stalls it again. It takes at most `MAX_CATCHUP` and *drops* the rest rather than banking it, because banking it means the backlog is paid out on the next tick and the stall repeats. (4) **The click-to-cell mapping was a pair of constants, and it disagreed with the drawing.** `grid_y_start = 44.0` was written out three times and `cell_size` was set to `8.0` once; worse, the two mappings did not even agree with each other — `render` wrapped the row with `% height` where the click added `view_row` and *rejected* the overflow. There is one `Layout::cell_rect` now, the drawing pass records a hit box from it for every cell, and the pointer is read from those boxes, so "where a cell is drawn" and "where clicking hits it" are one fact rather than two that agree by maintenance. (5) **The viewport did not exist.** `view_row` and `view_col` were read by the drawing and by the click handler and written by nothing in the program; the doc comment's "Zoom in/out with +/-" described the same absent machinery. Deleted rather than built: the whole world is fitted to the window at every size, and `-`/`=` nudge the speed. (6) **Nothing was clickable but a cell** — `render` returned a bare `Vec<RenderCommand>`, so the pattern menu could not be picked from with a pointer at all. (7) **Neither overlay blocked what it covered**, so a click meant for the sheet edited the board underneath it. (8) **`Ctrl` was filtered in one handler and not the other**, so the pattern menu answered shortcuts the board refused. (9) **The header was six strings at fixed offsets** of 12, 160, 260, 400, 530 and 640 with `max_width: None`, so the last two left any window under about 700 pixels and, being unbounded, could not even be ellipsised into view. (10) **Twelve blanket `#![allow]`s on lines 15-26**, `dead_code` and `unused_imports` among them, which is what kept 5 quiet — six unused palette entries and a hand-written `impl Clone for Grid` reproducing the derive field for field went with them. (11) **The Pulsar was not a pulsar.** Found by a test written for this rewrite: the pattern is defined by mirroring a twelve-cell quadrant into four, and six of the twelve sat *on* the mirror lines. A cell on a mirror line is its own reflection, so it contributes one cell to the figure rather than four — the "48-cell" Pulsar was 36 cells, and was not an oscillator of any period: it decayed to two blocks and a blinker in three generations and stopped. Two faults more were found by the tests during the rewrite rather than by reading the old program, both in code written for it. **`centred_in` promised a clip it did not deliver**: it moved the string's start left to centre it but passed the box's *whole* width as `max_width`, so the ellipsis point sat half a box past the box's right edge — the last control's label was free to run to 922 in a 900-wide window — and a string too long to centre started left of its box, where the ellipsis trims the end of a string whose beginning has already fallen off the other side. And **the margin could be larger than the window it was a margin within**: `pad` had a floor of two pixels, so at 1x1 the board's own origin was at (2, 2), outside the window. A margin may never exceed a quarter of the side it is taken from. Both were caught by invariants asserted across ten window sizes rather than by a test aimed at either. Seventy-nine deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/life/mutate.py`), faults 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7 and 11 restored literally. Window-wiring baseline 87 -> 86.
- [x] Maze (apps/maze: **windowed** — recursive-backtracker mazes at three sizes, keyboard and pointer, a help sheet, a clock that runs, a solution overlay, and a live count of the steps you have left; 56 tests). Wiring it found nine faults on top of the missing window. (1) `main` built a `MazeApp`, dropped it and exited: no maze ever reached a screen and no key ever arrived. (2) **Every key ran twice.** Both key handlers destructured `Event::Key(KeyEvent { key, .. })` and the field the `..` swallowed was `pressed`, so the press ran the action and the release ran it again — and the compositor really does send releases (`gui/compositor/src/lib.rs` builds a `KeyEvent` with `pressed: false` for every key-up), so this was what the program would do the first second anybody used it. Three of the four keys are worse than "twice". **The solution overlay could never be seen at all**: `S` flipped `show_solution` on at the press and back off at the release with no frame drawn between them, so the feature named on the help bar had no reachable state in which it showed anything. **The difficulty cycled backwards**: `D` ran `Difficulty::next` twice, so the sizes appeared in the order Small, Large, Medium — the reverse of the one `next` spells out — and each press also dealt two mazes and threw the first away. **The move counter was always double**, which is exactly the number the win box compared against `Optimal`: a player who walked a 10x10 perfectly was shown "Moves: 36 (Optimal: 18)" and told, in effect, that they had taken twice the best route while standing on it. And `N` dealt two mazes and showed the second. (3) **The advertised "Timer tracking" never ticked.** `elapsed_secs` was set to zero in `new` and in `new_maze`, read only by `format_time`, and incremented nowhere; `format_time` was in turn never called by `render`, so even a working clock would not have reached the screen. Both halves were invisible because `#![allow(dead_code)]` sat on line 13. This is `known-issues.md` lesson 47's sixth application, and the clock is real now: `tick_interval` asks for one only while the maze is unsolved, `Event::Tick` advances it by the `elapsed_ms` it carries rather than by the interval asked for, and it is drawn. (4) **The layout was a constant.** `render(width, height)` used its two arguments for the background rectangle, the help bar's `y` and the win overlay, and nothing else: the maze drew at a fixed `(20, 50)` with a cell size read from the *difficulty* — 15 pixels on Large, whatever the window — and the top bar's five strings drew at fixed `x` of 12, 80, 260, 380 and 530, so "Won: N" fell off the right edge of any window narrower than about 600 pixels and the maze fell off the bottom of any window shorter than 500. (5) **There was no pointer input at all**, though `MouseButton`, `MouseEvent` and `MouseEventKind` were all imported — hidden by `#![allow(unused_imports)]` on line 24. Clicking a cell now walks the player there along the maze's own shortest route, one counted move per step, which is exactly what the arrow keys would have charged for the same journey; and the walk stops if the route passes through the exit, because walking on past the way out because the click asked for somewhere else would be a win the player never got to see. (6) **`handle_won` did not check modifiers while `handle_playing` did**, so Ctrl-N dealt a fresh maze on the win screen and did nothing during play. There is one key handler now, with one modifier check in it. (7) **`Maze::solve` stepped out of bounds unchecked.** Its BFS computed `let nr = (r as i32 + dr) as usize;` with no `in_bounds` test — unlike `generate`, three functions above, which had one — and relied entirely on `can_move` never being true at a border. That holds only because `generate` happens never to remove a border wall; nothing in the type says so. A single removed border wall turns `-1` into `usize::MAX`, and `idx` then computes `row * cols + col`, which for that row is not an out-of-range index that panics but a *plausible* one that silently reads another cell. Every neighbour is bounds-checked now, in the one place that does the arithmetic. (8) **The "Optimal" figure was frozen at the opening** — the length of the route from `(0, 0)`, computed once in `new_maze`, still describing that journey after twenty moves down a journey you were no longer on. The opening optimum is still shown as the thing to read your score against, but the live number beside it is the steps remaining **from where you actually stand**, which in a perfect maze is not an estimate but the answer. (9) **One best-move count was shared by all three sizes**: a 10x10 solved in 18 moves set a best a 30x30 could never beat, and the win box presented the two as comparable. Best is kept per size now. (10) **Nine blanket `#![allow]`s sat on lines 13-24**, `dead_code` and `unused_imports` among them, which is what kept 3 and 5 quiet, beside a hand-written `impl Clone for Maze` reproducing the derive field for field. Forty-four deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/maze/mutate.py`), fault 2 restored by deleting one line. Four mutants survived the first sweep and none of the four was a spare guard. (a) **A mutation runner that reads only the harness's `tests::<name>` failure lines cannot tell a crash from a clean run.** Deleting the "already numbered" check from the search makes it re-queue cells forever; the binary dies on a two-gigabyte allocation before any test can report, and the runner scored that as SURVIVED — the exact opposite of what happened. It now treats a non-zero exit with no named failure as a catch. (b) **A perfect maze is a tree, and a tree cannot tell breadth-first from depth-first**: there is exactly one route between any two cells, so every traversal order gives the same numbers and reading the queue from the back changed nothing on any maze the generator can deal. Caught only by a hand-carved ring — eight cells joined in a circle with the middle sealed off — on which the two answers are 2 and 6. The same ring is the only shape that can separate the two halves of "perfect": connectedness and the edge count each pass a maze that is wrong in the other direction. (c) `passages` and `is_perfect` had **no test at all** — the suite checked the generator with an independent flood, which is right, and then never asked whether the program's own checker agreed. (d) The best-score test walked two *different* mazes out by the shortest route and asserted the best was the lower of the two, which is true whichever way the two mazes happen to fall, so it passed against a program that overwrote the best on every win; the worse run is now worse by construction — padded past 150 moves, where the longest possible route on a 10x10 is 99 — rather than by luck. That padding then produced a fifth finding about the tests themselves: written as the obvious `while g.moves() < moves`, it turned the "a step is not counted" mutant from six named failures into a timeout, because a program that stops counting moves makes the loop spin forever. **An unbounded loop in a test helper converts every fault it depends on into the same undiagnosable hang**, so it is bounded now and names the fault when the bound is reached — the same shape as the `walk_out` helper beside it. Window-wiring baseline 89 -> 88.
- [x] Tic-Tac-Toe (apps/tictactoe) **windowed** — `App` + `Probe`, layout derived
  from the live window size, hit boxes recorded by the pass that draws them, and
  the computer's reply on an `Event::Tick` clock. Wiring it up found five faults
  the simulation `main` had kept out of sight:
  1. **Every key fired twice** — the handler never read `pressed`, so an arrow
     moved the cursor *two* squares per press (the middle row was unreachable
     from either edge), and `Enter` on a finished game reset the board on the
     press and then played a move on the release: "new game" started you one
     move down, in a square you never chose.
  2. **A click outside the grid placed a mark inside it** — `((x - grid_x) /
     cell) as i32` truncates towards zero, so a click a fraction of a cell left
     of the board gave column 0 and `(0..3).contains(&0)` accepted it. The same
     held above the board, where the title and status line live: clicking the
     word "Tic-Tac-Toe" played the top-left square.
  3. **The layout was a constant** — 100px cells at a fixed y=100 and a score
     line pinned at y=430, so a window shorter than 460px drew its score below
     the bottom edge and one narrower than 300px hung the grid off both sides.
  4. **The "AI thinking..." status was unreachable** — the search ran inside the
     human's own event handler, so the turn flipped back before any frame was
     drawn. The reply is now a state the window renders, held for `THINK_MS`.
  5. **`render` took `&mut self` only to hit-test**, caching `win_width` for a
     hand-written click test (plus a `win_height` nothing read).
  60 tests replace the old 30, including an exhaustive check of the
  unbeatability claim: every one of the ~4.7k reachable positions, both marks,
  compared against a three-valued negamax solver written from the rules rather
  than from the code under test. Window-wiring baseline 94 -> 93.
- [x] Mandelbrot (apps/mandelbrot: **windowed** — fractal explorer with zoom/pan, 5 color schemes, preset locations, adjustable iterations/resolution, 63 tests). Wiring it found six faults, three of which made the program wrong rather than merely unwired. `main` computed one fractal, dropped it and exited, so nothing reached a screen. Every key fired twice: `pressed` was never read, so the key ran again on release — which meant `I` and `F1` toggled back off the instant they were let go and the help sheet could not be seen at all. The renderer indexed cells (`px / cols`) while the click handler mapped pixels (`x / width`), against a `cols` computed by a truncating divide: the image was stretched by up to a cell, the right and bottom edges were never painted, and a click landed on a different point than the one drawn under it. The per-frame arithmetic was unbounded — at 4K with `pixel_size` 1 and 2000 iterations a single frame asked for four billion iterations — and every frame recomputed from scratch, so merely recolouring cost a full recompute. And the mouse wheel did nothing despite the help text saying it zoomed. One `complex_in` mapping now serves both the renderer and the hit test, cells are sampled at their centres and sized with `ceil` so the edges are painted, a `TileKey`-keyed cache holds *iteration counts* (not colours, so `C` is free), an iteration budget enlarges the cell rather than silently ignoring the resolution setting and the readout says when it has done so, the scale is clamped to where `f64` stops resolving, and the wheel zooms about the pointer.
- [x] Pong (apps/pong: **windowed** — classic two-paddle ball game with AI opponent, speed increases, first to 11 wins, 63 tests). Wiring it up found four faults, each of which needed the game to actually be playable to see: `main` built a `PongApp` and dropped it; the key handler never read `KeyEvent::pressed` — with a comment claiming the framework has no key releases, which it always has — so one Up press welded the paddle to the wall and *releasing* P paused again; `update()` moved the ball a fixed distance per tick, making its speed the compositor's frame rate (speeds are now per second, `advance` scales by `elapsed_ms` and sub-steps at 4 field units so a fast ball cannot jump a 12-unit paddle); and `FIELD_X`/`FIELD_Y` were absolute screen coordinates with a constant 700x500 field, so `render(width, height)` used its arguments for one line of help text. `Layout` now fits the field into the live window at a single aspect-preserving scale, the pointer steers the paddle, and the message box and footer buttons do what they say.
- [x] Sliding Puzzle (apps/sliding: **windowed** — 8/15/24-puzzle at 3x3/4x4/5x5, keyboard and pointer, a help sheet, a per-size record of the fewest moves, and a floor on the moves remaining; 70 tests). Wiring it found eleven faults on top of the missing window. (1) `main` built a `SlidingPuzzle`, dropped it and exited: no tile ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **The key handler never read `KeyEvent::pressed`** — the pattern was `Event::Key(KeyEvent { key, modifiers, .. })` and the `..` swallowed exactly the field saying whether the key went down or came back up, so **every key ran twice**. That is not "twice as fast" on this program: an arrow slid *two* tiles and counted two moves, so the board went somewhere you had not asked for; `H` opened the help sheet on the press and closed it on the release, so **the only written record of the controls could never be seen**; `T` turned the numbers off and straight back on, so **they could not be turned off**; and `N`/`3`/`4`/`5` each built and shuffled two boards and showed you the second. (3) **The hit test was a second copy of the layout** — `handle_mouse_click` declared its own `grid_offset_x = 50.0`, `grid_offset_y = 80.0` and `cell_size = 80.0` beside `render`'s `grid_x`/`grid_y`/`cell_size`, two sets of constants that had to agree with nothing to make them; hit boxes are now recorded by the drawing pass, so a tile is clickable exactly where its ink is. (4) **The layout was a constant**: `render(width, height)` used its arguments for the background rectangle and nothing else — the grid was 80px cells at (50, 80), the best-scores panel was pinned at `50 + 80 * size + 40` and 180 wide (off the right edge of any window under 670px at 4x4 and 750px at 5x5), and the help sheet ran to `y = 440` inside that panel rather than being a sheet at all. (5) **A click had to land on a tile touching the gap** — `slide_pos` returned false unless the square was orthogonally adjacent, so clicking the far end of the gap's own row, the one gesture every physical sliding puzzle supports and the reason a row of tiles is a row, did nothing; a click now pushes the whole run and counts every tile it moved. (6) **The only pointer control was the board.** Size, new game, numbers and help were keyboard-only while the help sheet listed "Click — Slide tile" as though the pointer were first-class; every control is clickable now, including the best-scores rows, which switch to the size they name. (7) `timer_ticks` was written once and read by nothing, its comment calling it a "rough frame counter for animation" of which there was none. (8) **Twelve blanket `#![allow]`s sat on lines 1-12**, `dead_code` and `unused_imports` among them, which is what had kept 7 and 9 invisible. (9) `SURFACE1` and `SURFACE2` were declared and never used. (10) **A shuffle could hand back a solved board** — `new_game` walked at random and never checked the result, so a walk that came home left a finished puzzle presented as an unstarted one, and the first move you made broke it; it reshuffles now, but a *bounded* number of times, because a board that cannot be unsolved at all (a 1x1 has no legal move) would otherwise turn the guard into the same unbounded loop the shuffle itself had to be rescued from. (11) **Every launch dealt the same board**: the only seed in the program was `SeededRng::new(42)`, so a "best moves" record was a record for one fixed scramble rather than for a size; it seeds from the system now, with `with_seed` kept so the tests stay deterministic without the program being. Two things were added rather than fixed: nothing said how close you were — the move counter only goes up, so the program could report 300 moves and not that you were two tiles from home — so there is now a floor on the moves remaining, the sum over tiles of the distance each still has to travel, which no sequence of moves can beat because one move moves one tile one square; and the help sheet's row for `H` used to be untrue in both directions (Escape never opened the sheet, and Enter and Space closed it unmentioned), so the sheet now states what the program actually answers and a test walks the list in both directions. Twenty deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/sliding/mutate.py`), including one that removes the scramble's retry bound and is caught by the suite hanging. Two survived the first sweep and both were real faults rather than weak tests: the help sheet's hit box was unreachable, because `handle_mouse` short-circuited on `show_help` before the hit test ever ran — a control wired to nothing, and worse, the frame went on recording boxes for controls buried under an opaque sheet, so it was describing a screen nobody was looking at. The sheet now records one hit box over the *whole window*, last, which makes the modal work through the ordinary hit test with no special case anywhere and keeps the frame's account of what is clickable true; and the shuffle's anti-undo guard had no test, which a two-move walk now covers by asserting the distance floor is exactly 2 (each slide moves it by exactly one, so 2 catches an undo even where the undo does not happen to land back on a solved board). Window-wiring baseline 91 -> 90.
- [x] Solitaire (apps/solitaire: **windowed** — Klondike dealt into whatever window it is given, a stock that recycles from the waste, four foundations, seven tableau columns fanned so a face-up card shows its rank and a face-down one shows only that it is there, alternating-colour descending builds, kings-only on an empty column, unlimited undo of every kind of move including the flip a move exposed, a move counter, `a` to send everything that will go to the foundations, `n` to deal again, and a cursor that reaches every pile with Tab and the arrow keys or with the mouse; 119 tests). Wiring it found ten faults on top of the missing window, and the first four are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = SolitaireApp::new();`** — it dealt a hand into memory, dropped it and exited, so no card ever reached a screen and no click ever arrived. (2) **`render` took no size and painted a hard-coded 700x700 background**, so a wider window kept a 700-wide picture with a black margin around it and a taller one kept a 700-tall one. (3) **Fifteen fixed-geometry constants** — card width, card height, the fan offsets, every pile's origin — meant the deal was right at exactly one window size, clipped at anything smaller and adrift in anything larger. `Layout::solve` takes the live size each frame and `Table::fit` derives one card size from it. (4) **There was no mouse handling at all**: `handle_event` matched `Event::Key` and nothing else, so a card game that is played with a pointer could not be played with one. The drawing pass records a box per card, per pile and per header field, and the click asks `Frame::hit_test`. (5) **`handle_key` returned nothing**, so a key the game ignored looked to the caller exactly like a move — every key release repainted the whole table. (6) **`render_empty_pile` stroked before it filled**, and the fill then overpainted the inner half of the focus ring, so the cursor on an empty foundation was a hairline instead of a ring. (7) **The move counter was drawn at x = 400 and the help line at x = 500**, which collided with the title in a narrow window and floated away from it in a wide one; the header is a band divided into three now. (8) **A crate-wide `#![allow(dead_code)]` / `#![allow(unused_imports)]` kept seven unused palette constants and two unused methods alive** — the allow was not hiding a lint, it was hiding the fact that the code was not there to be used. (9) **53 production sites indexed or added without a check**, with the guards (`if col >= TABLEAU_COLS`) sitting a line away from the panic they were meant to prevent, which is a guard that is correct only as long as nobody inserts a line between the two. Asking the column for its cards *is* the guard now (`col`, `col_mut`, `found` fold the bounds check into the lookup), the deal runs off an iterator so a short deck ends it rather than panicking one card past the end, and `step_index` replaced the `i as i32 + delta` cast round-trip that lost any index past `i32::MAX` on the way in and could overflow before the cast back out. (10) **Every move popped the source before it knew the destination existed.** `self.waste.pop()` then `self.foundations[fidx].push(card)` is fine while the index is in range and deletes the card outright when it is not — the card is off the waste and the push never happens. Each move takes its destination by name first and pops second. 44 deliberate mutations check that the test claiming to cover each piece is the one that fails (`apps/solitaire/mutate.py`); the first sweep left eight unaccounted for, and as with checkers not one of them was a fault in the program — all eight were the suite's. **Two were one discovery: the card size is fitted to two rows at once and only one of them was ever measured.** `Table::fit` sizes the card to the top row *and* to the deepest fan a column can reach — six face-down cards under twelve face-up — and takes the smaller. The opening deal fans column 6 seven cards deep, so every geometry test read a table whose deep-fan reserve was slack, and dropping that half of the fit entirely changed nothing any test could see. `the_deepest_fan_a_deal_can_reach_still_fits_the_window` builds the worst case directly rather than hoping the opening deal contains it. **One was a tautology** — Lesson 80 again. `every_pile_is_drawn_inside_the_window` asserted that every recorded box lies within the window, which `Frame::hit` guarantees by construction: it trims a box to the clip in force and the clip *is* the window. The claim was true of any layout at all, including one that fits the card to the tableau alone and runs the top row off the side of a narrow window, which is the mutation that walked past it. Restated with teeth: every card in the game is one size, so a card narrower than its neighbours is a card the window cut. **One was a missing claim rather than a wrong test.** Columns spaced by the card width alone overlap — but they are still evenly spaced, still the same width and still under the top row, so `the_seven_columns_are_evenly_spaced_and_the_same_width` was right not to fire. It was given the sentence it was missing (the step exceeds the width) rather than being blamed for the one it never made. **One was a wrong mutation, which is a verdict worth as much as the others**: the row named "the column strip is recorded over the cards" only *deleted* the strip's hit box, so it tested something other than its own name. Split into a delete row and a genuine record-it-after-the-cards row, which are caught by different tests. **The remaining three each survived because the opening deal is a special case the suite mistook for the general one.** A click on a face-up card picks up from that card down, but the opening deal turns exactly one card up per column, so "the card clicked" and "the deepest face-up card" are the same card in every column of it and the mutation is a no-op; the test builds a three-card run and clicks the middle card's visible sliver. A click on the title was untested because the only header test played a won game, and the won-game branch returns before the header's arm is ever reached. And `test_horizontal_clamp_right` walked off the right of the tableau but never off the right of the foundations, which is a separate match arm with its own edge. Window-wiring baseline 66 -> 65.
- [x] Nonogram (apps/nonogram: **windowed** — picture logic puzzle, 10 built-in puzzles across 5x5/10x10/15x15 grids, clue bands solved from the puzzle's own clue depth, check mode, a running clock, keyboard and pointer, and a select list whose three columns are laid out rather than offset; 83 tests). Wiring it found ten faults on top of the missing window. (1) **`main` was `let _app = NonogramApp::new();`** — it built the puzzle catalogue, computed every clue and dropped the lot, so no picture ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **`render` took no size, so the program chose its own window.** The select screen declared itself `520.0` wide and the playing screen measured its own width from the grid, both in absolute pixels off a `CELL_SIZE` the window had no say in; a window narrower than the program's idea of itself simply lost the right-hand end of the board. Everything is solved from the live window size every frame now — `Layout` for the bands, `Grid` for the cells and their clue bands, `List` for the select screen. (3) **The select screen's click map was a second copy of its geometry, and the copy had already drifted.** The list was drawn from `PADDING` to `520 - PADDING` and hit-tested from `PADDING` to `500.0`, so the last four pixels of every entry were drawn as part of it and did nothing — a rule kept by copying (`known-issues.md` lesson 63), which is exactly the failure mode that copying produces. The hit boxes come from the drawing pass now. (4) **`CLUE_HALF_WIDTH = 4.0` was eyeballed, under a comment claiming no text metric was available** — which is false, `guitk::text::measure` is what the rest of the tree uses — so every two-digit clue sat off the column it described. A clue slot is exactly as wide as the cell it belongs to now, and a test asserts that for every column of every puzzle at every window shape. (5) **Every string carried `max_width: None`, and three columns of the select list were hand-guessed** at `+12`, `+160` and `+260` from the left margin: a puzzle name over 144 pixels wide ran straight through the size label beside it, with nothing to stop it. The thumbnail is taken from the right, the size label from the column left of it measured at the size it is drawn at, and the name gets what is left — and each is told where to stop. (6) **The pointer could fill a cell and nothing else.** `X` (mark a cell blank), `C` (check) and the way back to the list were keyboard-only, so a player using the mouse had no way to record "this one is blank" at all — the single most-used move in a nonogram. Right-click marks, and both switches are controls in the footer. (7) **The clock counted ticks nothing ever sent.** `App::tick_interval` defaults to `None`, so the header read `0:00` for the whole game. It asks for a tick every 200ms, and a tick off the board is `Response::Idle` rather than five wasted repaints a second on a screen at rest. (8) **`GridSize::label` spelled the side length a second time** — `Small` was `5` in `side()` and `"5x5"` here — so a grid size could be changed in one place and go on being described by the other. The label is derived from `side()` now and `label` is deleted. (9) **The crate had never passed its own lint gate.** Twelve blanket `#![allow(...)]` at the root covered `dead_code` and `unused_imports` — which is what let a program whose `main` discarded its own app compile without a word of complaint — and underneath them were 36 real warnings, 25 `arithmetic_side_effects` and 9 `indexing_slicing`, straight through the picture parser and the win check. The allows are gone and the crate builds clean under `-D warnings -D dead_code`. (10) **A guard in front of a rule that already held.** `parse_picture` bounded its *line* iterator by the grid side, but a line past the bottom indexes past the end of the grid, which the `get_mut` below already answers for — a line no test could own (lesson 51). It is gone; the `take` on *characters* is real, because without it a character past the right edge lands on the next row rather than off the end, and it now has a test that owns it. Notably absent from this program: the double-fire fault every other game in this campaign had — `handle_event` already gated on `key_event.pressed` — and the grid geometry had already been single-sourced (design-decisions.md §486). The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/nonogram/mutate.py` breaks 100 pieces of the program one at a time, and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. Getting there cost five test rewrites and one new lesson (`known-issues.md` **lesson 70**): every arrow-key test pressed its arrow *at the edge that arrow stops at*, where a missing bound check and a movement that goes the wrong way both leave the cursor exactly where the test expects it — so four separate faults survived the first sweep. The rule that came out of it is a pair: to test a direction, press the key from somewhere it can move; to test a bound, assert the `EventResult` and not just the position. The same shape turned up twice more — the centring test measured vertical slack in a fixture that was height-constrained, where "centred" and "flush against the top" are both `0 == 0`, and the clock was ticked once, which cannot tell a sum from an assignment. Window-wiring baseline 75 -> 74.
- [x] Asteroids (apps/asteroids: **windowed** — a header of five readings over a playfield fitted to the live window size each frame, ship rotation/thrust with drag and a speed cap, three asteroid sizes that split into two of the next size down, waves, explosion and thrust particles, lives with a blinking invulnerable respawn, a high score, and pause and game-over sheets that can be clicked as well as typed at; 179 tests). Wiring it found twelve faults, the missing window among them. (1) **`main` was `let _app = AsteroidsApp::new();`** — it built the ship, spawned the first wave and dropped the lot, so no frame ever reached a screen and no key ever arrived. (2) **`handle_event` was written and had no caller.** The whole input path — key down, key up, the pause toggle, the fire button — existed and was reachable only from the tests; nothing in the program could call it, because there was no window to call it from. (3) **The drawing pass took no size.** `render()` placed everything against `WINDOW_WIDTH` and `WINDOW_HEIGHT`, so the picture was the same 824×674 whatever window it went into: a larger one left a band of nothing down two sides, a smaller one cut the field off. The world is now fitted to the window rather than drawn at a fixed size — and fitted, not stretched, because `FIELD_WIDTH`/`FIELD_HEIGHT` are *rules of the game*, not a drawing size (they are how far a shot travels before it wraps, and how much room there is to dodge in), so a wide window letterboxes rather than changing how the game plays. (4) **Twelve blanket `#![allow(...)]` at the top of the file** — `dead_code` among them, which is what let a `main` that discards its own app compile without a word. Removing them *was* the audit: five methods nothing called (`length_sq`, `sub`, `distance_to`, `asteroid_count`, `bullet_count`), and every one of the arithmetic faults below, surfaced the moment the compiler was allowed to speak. (5) **The five header readings were drawn at 10, 120, 280, 420 and 560 pixels from the left edge** — offsets that line up only under an 824-pixel window and a score below five digits. A six-digit score ran into the high score beside it. They are laid out by their own measured widths now, and one that will not fit is dropped rather than drawn over its neighbour; the controls line is what a squeezed header gives up first, since a reminder of which key turns left is worth less than the score. (6) **A wave spawned one more asteroid than the last, with no ceiling.** Wave 40 would have opened with 43 asteroids in a field with room to dodge in about a dozen — not a crash, just a game that stops being playable and gives no sign of why. Capped at twelve. (7) **The score was a wrapping `u32`.** Four billion points is a hundred thousand hours, so no player reaches it — but a champion put back to nothing is exactly the kind of thing that only ever shows up in a bug report. Saturating now, along with the wave counter. (8) **`new_game` reset the window size**, because it rebuilt the whole app from a seed and copied only the high score back. Starting a new game in a resized window snapped the layout back to 824×674 until the next resize event, which for a maximised window never comes. (9) **The game-over box was a fixed 300×200** on a field that now scales, so in a half-size window it covered twice the field it was drawn to cover. It takes its share of the field now. (10) **The overlay sheets had a dead zone in the shape of their own box.** `handle_mouse` had one arm for `Target::Overlay` and one for `Target::NewGame`, which reads as "a click anywhere on the sheet" — it was not: the game-over box is drawn over the middle of the overlay and its title and final-score lines record hit boxes *after* the overlay's, so they, not the overlay, won the hit test. A click on "GAME OVER", or on the score the player had just earned, did nothing, while a click on the dim margin around them started a new game. The dead zone was exactly the part of the sheet a person looks at. The pause sheet escaped only by the accident that its middle line is `Resume`, which had an arm of its own. (11) **The asteroid outline joined its last vertex to its first with `%`**, and read the asteroid it had just found the index of back out with `[ai]` — both fine as written and both one edit away from not being, which is what the blanket allows were suppressing. The outline pairs each vertex with the next through `zip` against the finite list now, so the cycle cannot run away even for an asteroid with no vertices at all. (12) **The body band could start past the bottom of the window.** The header's height is a share of `h` clamped up to a floor of 20, so in a window shorter than 20 pixels the clamp is trying to make the band *taller* than the window it is in; `header.bottom() + pad` then lands outside — at 824×6 it is 8, two pixels past the edge. The band was empty there, so nothing was drawn wrong and nothing looked wrong, but an empty rectangle at an impossible origin is a number waiting to be used by whatever is added next, and it would then be wrong in a way that traced back to the layout rather than to the code that trusted it. Every size the test tried was 300 or more, so the case was unreachable until the list was widened to reach it. And the tests themselves were wrong in six places rather than the code, all of them found by the mutation sweep and all the same shape — a test that passes for a reason other than the one in its name. One set `score` without `high_score`, a pair the game never produces, so it asserted about a state `new_game` cannot be handed. One asked `to_screen` for both sides of its own assertion, so a `to_screen` that forgot to move the point onto the field agreed with itself to the last decimal. One resized *down* and clicked the pause sheet, where every target resumes, so reading the click against the wrong size still gave the same answer — and resized through the probe helper, which calls `resize` directly and so routed around the `Resize` event entirely. One asserted a text box was wide enough for its text, a threshold that a hardcoded 130px cleared by luck. One clicked a reading rather than the header strip behind it, which the readings cover. One asserted a zero-size window's frame was *balanced*, which says nothing about the zero-size rectangles it was filling. Window-wiring baseline 72 -> 71.
- [x] Pacman (apps/pacman: **windowed** — a maze chase with four AI ghosts (Blinky/Pinky/Inky/Clyde), power pellets, scatter/chase/frightened/eaten modes, tunnel wrap, lives, levels, and menu/pause/game-over sheets that can be clicked as well as typed at, all fitted to the live window size every frame; 147 tests). Wiring it found fifteen faults, the missing window among them. (1) **`main` was `let _app = PacmanApp::new();`** — it parsed the maze, placed the four ghosts and dropped the lot, so no frame ever reached a screen and no arrow key ever arrived. (2) **`render(&self)` took no size**, so the picture was a fixed 528×702 whatever window it went into. Every frame is built from `(w, h)` now — a `Layout` for the bands, a `Board` that fits a square-celled grid into the body. (3) **The overlay lines were centred by hand-tuned half-widths** — 80, 90, 100, 70 and 50 pixels, one per line, each of them a measurement of a string taken once by eye and then frozen. They are measured with `text::measure` and the stack with `text::line_height`. (4) **The header readings sat at fixed offsets, including a bare `- 120.0`**, which is a right-aligned reading written as a left-aligned one that happens to land in the right place at one width. (5) **The footer drew life tokens from a flat 50 px at a 22 px step**, so in a narrow window the third life was drawn off the end of the window; the tokens are shares of the measured word "LIVES" now, and the footer draws no more than it has room for. (6) **The eyes and pupils were nudged by `+1.0` / `-3.0` / `±1.0` pixels** — corrections to a drawing at one scale, which stop being corrections at any other. All are shares of the cell. (7) **Twelve blanket `#![allow(...)]` sat at the top of the file.** Removing them uncovered ~56 lint sites, and 30 of those were `as usize` indexing on a `Pos` that is *deliberately* allowed off the board (tunnel wrap puts the player at column −1, and a ghost's aim point is routinely outside the maze) — so a lost bounds check would not have read the wrong cell, it would have indexed at four billion and panicked. `Pos::index() -> Option<(usize, usize)>` with `cell_at`/`set_cell` replaced the lot. (8) **`start_new_game` rebuilt the app wholesale**, and so discarded everything that is not a fact about the game: starting a new game snapped the layout back to the built-in size until the next resize event, which for a maximised window never comes. The window size rides across with the high score now. (9) `Ghost::home` was written and never read — the blanket `dead_code` allow is what let it sit there, along with `Direction::ALL`, `GhostId::name`, `released_ghost_count`, `ghost_at` and `count_cell_type`, none of which had a caller outside the tests.
