(10) **Three walkability predicates were the same set.** `Cell::is_ghost_walkable`, `PacmanApp::is_ghost_walkable` and `PacmanApp::is_ghost_passable` all reduced to "not a wall" — `Cell` has six variants, and `is_walkable() || cell == GhostDoor || cell == GhostHouse` covers five of them. So the `GhostMode` argument threaded through the movement code decided nothing, the house door stood open in both directions, and **a live ghost could sit inside the ghost house for the length of a power pellet**, somewhere the player cannot follow. A live ghost is held to the cells the player walks now; only a pair of eyes may cross the door. (11) **Reviving an eaten ghost left it standing on the door**, whose other three neighbours are house and wall. A ghost may not reverse, so a ghost that arrived heading down was stranded on that square for the rest of the game — one of the four ghosts simply stopped playing. It revives at the house exit facing out now, the same place and pose a ghost released from the house starts in. (12) **`INITIAL_WINDOW_W`/`_H` spelled the natural size a second time**, next to `WINDOW_WIDTH`/`HEIGHT`, under a comment promising they could not drift — which is a promise a comment cannot keep. `initial_size` converts from the float pair. (13) **The margin had a two-pixel floor and no ceiling relative to the window**, so in a window one pixel wide the bands' left edge was at x = 2: a band that begins outside the window it is in. Held to half the shorter side as well. (14) **Nothing was clipped.** A reading too wide for a narrow window was written straight across the maze below it, and a *zero-sized* window still recorded a clickable `Press N to start` at its centre — a control on a window with no pixels to show it. The frame is clipped to the window, the header to the header and the footer to the footer. (15) And the fault the clipping work turned up was **in the toolkit, not the app**: `Frame::is_balanced` existed to catch a clip pushed and never popped, and missed the mirror image entirely. `Vec::pop` on an empty stack is a silent no-op, so a helper that popped a clip it had not pushed released *its caller's* clip and still left both stacks empty — which is all `is_balanced` looked at. Everything after the stray pop escaped the clip it was meant to be held inside, and the frame reported itself fine. Stray pops are counted now, with tests in `gui/toolkit` for the over-pop and for the damage it does. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/pacman/mutate.py` breaks 44 pieces of the program one at a time and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. Two survived the first sweep, both real gaps rather than weak assertions, and both of the same shape — a wrong answer that the geometry happens to keep in bounds. Giving the header a share of the *width* instead of the height is caught by no in-bounds check, because `.min(h)` and the clamp keep the band inside the window however it was measured; the test that owns it now holds the *pad-free* readings — where the header ends and where the footer begins — fixed while only the width changes. And using a cell's column for its row lands inside a grid that is 28 columns by 31 rows, so every box stays in bounds and the maze is merely a transposed smear; it is caught by walking a few cells down and across from the origin instead. Window-wiring baseline 71 -> 70.
- [x] FreeCell (apps/freecell: **windowed** — the card game with 4 free cells, 4 foundations, an auto-move that cascades, full undo, keyboard navigation and a clickable button strip, all fitted to the live window size every frame; 188 tests). Wiring it found thirteen faults, the missing window among them. (1) **`main` was `let _app = FreeCell::new();`** — it dealt fifty-two cards across eight columns, drew them into a buffer nobody displayed and dropped the lot, so no card ever reached a screen and no key ever arrived. (2) **`render(&self)` took no size** and filled a hardcoded 900×800. The header's three readings sat at bare `x` of 200, 340 and 520, which is where they do not overlap only at one width and one font size; they are measured and right-aligned now, against a `Layout` solved from `(w, h)` every frame. (3) **The win overlay's three lines were placed at x 280/300/270 and y 300/350/390** — three separate guesses at the middle of that one board, no two of them agreeing on where its middle was. (4) **A column deeper than about twenty-six cards ran off the bottom**, because the cascade step was a pixel count rather than a share of the room left; the step tightens as a column deepens now, and the deepest column on the board is what the table is fitted to. (5) **There was no pointer handling at all**, under a help line advertising six controls. Every card, cell, foundation and column is a hit box recorded by the drawing pass, and the strip along the bottom is four buttons — each of which runs *its own key* rather than calling the method that key calls, so a button cannot come to do something its caption does not say. (6) **`try_tableau_to_freecell` was written, tested, and reachable by nothing**: the one move freecell is named after could only be made the long way, select-then-place. `F` and the Free button run it now. (7) **The auto-run wrote its move out twice**, and in each copy the card left its pile *before* the foundation was reached — an index the arrays did not have would have taken a card off the board and put it nowhere. Both loops share `send_home`, which confirms the destination first. (8) **Eight copies of `move_count += 1`** became one `count_move`. (9) **The three zones each wrote out "wrap round at the start" for themselves** — three copies of one rule, and the `len - 1` in each of them underflows on a zone of length zero. `wrap_back`/`wrap_forward`. (10) **The top row's split was the literal `4`, in two places**, which is `FREE_CELL_COUNT` written out: changing the count would have sent the cursor to the wrong half of a row it still drew correctly. (11) **Six of the seven card colours were second copies of a palette entry written out again in hex**, free to drift from the palette they named — retheme the palette and the cards keep the old scheme, with nothing to say they had ever agreed. (12) **`can_place_on_foundation` said `== top + 1` on a `u8`**, which wraps 255 to 0 and calls an Ace placeable on a foundation whose top has run past a king. (13) **Twelve blanket `#![allow(...)]` hid 125 lint sites.** They are gone. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/freecell/mutate.py` breaks 53 pieces of the program one at a time and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. Fifteen were not, on the first sweep, and they sorted into five kinds worth keeping apart. **Six were simply uncovered** — the fan's tightening measured against the wrong card count, the table pinned left instead of centred, the column gap closed to nothing, the tableau drawn on top of the row above it, a column's reachable box stopping at its last card rather than the table floor, and the auto-run's *free-cell* loop marking no step as the player's while the tableau loop's copy was the only one under test. **Three were fixtures chosen so that the right answer and the wrong one coincide**: a room reading with two of four cells occupied, where the count of free cells and the count of full ones are both 2; an "is not sent home" test whose column was *empty*, so lifting the card before checking the destination and checking the destination first both return false with nothing there to lose; and a "won board refuses `F`" test whose columns were all empty, so a refused press and a press with no work to do look identical. **Three were assertions too weak to see a wrong answer**: a cursor tour that walks a ring and checks it came back where it started, which a cursor that never moves at all also satisfies — it collects the slots visited and requires them all distinct now; a button-and-key agreement test, where a button and a key that both do nothing agree perfectly; and a no-clipping check written over the recorded hit boxes, for which the outermost `PushClip` is asserted to be the window itself. (That last one was diagnosed backwards at the time and put right on 2026-08-30, when reversi's sweep turned up the same shape — known-issues Lesson 80. `Frame::hit` trims a box to the clip in force and drops it outright if nothing survives, so the boxes are the *only* thing that can see the clip vanish and are blind to everything else: a band the layout walked off the edge comes back cropped to the window and the loop waves it through. The test keeps only what can fail, as `the_whole_frame_is_clipped_to_the_window`, and a new `at_a_size_that_fits_the_clip_crops_nothing` measures every free cell, foundation and column bottom against the rect `Table` laid it out at — the boundary the clip does not enforce.) **One was an equivalent mutant**: `checked_add(1) == Some(v)` and `v == top.wrapping_add(1)` differ only at a `u8` top of 255, which wraps to 0, and no rank has value 0 — nothing on any board can tell them apart. It was replaced with an observable one, a foundation that accepts any card *above* its top rather than the one card above it. **And one was a redundancy a single-mutation sweep cannot see**: the win sheet is guarded twice over — a hit box across the whole window, so a click at a card's own position finds the sheet rather than the card, *plus* a `won` branch in `click` that catches anything reaching past it — so deleting either guard alone changes nothing a click does. (The test had also been asserting that no card was *picked up*, which the card underneath could not have been in any case: it was a King with its Queen already home, so a click that did reach the board would have sent it straight to the foundation without ever becoming a selection.) The two guards are named and owned separately now, one for what a click on the sheet finds and one for the single target the branch routes differently. Window-wiring baseline 70 -> 69.
- [x] Yahtzee (apps/yahtzee: **windowed** -- five dice fitted to whatever window they are given, thirteen scoring categories with the upper bonus and the joker rule, a Yahtzee-bonus row that appears only once a bonus is earned, a roll button that says what the click will do and is sized to say it, a high score that survives a new game, and a cursor that reaches every die and every box with Tab and the arrow keys or with the mouse; 229 tests). Wiring it found eleven faults on top of the missing window, and the compiler had been told to keep quiet about the first three. (1) **`main` was `let _app = Yahtzee::new();`** -- it rolled an opening hand into a struct, dropped it and exited, so no die and no scorecard ever reached a screen and no click ever arrived. (2) **`render`, `handle_event`, `handle_key` and `handle_mouse_click` were called from the test module and from nowhere else.** In a non-test build every one of them was unreachable, and the crate-wide `#![allow(dead_code)]` at the top of the file is precisely what stopped the compiler from saying so: the allow was not silencing a false positive, it was silencing an accurate report that the program's entire interactive half was not connected to anything. It is gone, along with `#![allow(unused_imports)]`. (3) **`render(width, height)` spent both arguments on the background rectangle and nothing else** -- `height` appears exactly once in the whole of the old drawing pass. Every other coordinate came from one of **sixteen fixed constants** (`DICE_SIZE`, `SCORECARD_WIDTH`, `HEADER_HEIGHT`, six font sizes...), so the game was laid out correctly at exactly one window size, clipped at anything smaller and adrift in a sea of background at anything larger. `Layout::solve` takes the live size each frame and `Dice::fit` derives one die size from what is left after the button and the help lines are reserved off the bottom. (4) **`handle_mouse_click` recomputed the dice row, the button and the scorecard from those same constants** -- a second copy of the geometry that had to be kept in step by hand -- and a third for the row order: `category_display_row` returned `cat_index + 3` for the lower section while the painting walked its own sequence, so the click and the picture agreed only for as long as nobody inserted a row between them. The drawing pass records a box per die, per scorecard row and per header field now, and the click asks `Frame::hit_test`; one `rows()` list is walked by both. That is also what finally makes the conditional Yahtzee-bonus row clickable, which under the `+ 3` it could never have been. (5) **`GamePhase` was stored beside `turn_number` and `roll_number` and set but never read back**, so the button's legend and the field that decided what the button did could disagree; the phase is derived from the counters. (6) **`handle_key` and the event entry point returned nothing**, so a key the game ignored looked to the caller exactly like a move -- every keystroke and every key *release* repainted the whole window. (7) **The roll button was a constant 140 wide, cut for "Roll (R)"**; "No Rolls Left" and "New Game (N)" are both longer and spilled out of it. (8) **`new_game` was `*self = Self::with_seed(seed)` with the high score lifted out and put back by hand** -- a reset that clears every field, including the ones that are not game state, and needs a new line of rescue for each one anybody adds. The window's live size is a field now, and it would have been thrown away on every new game. (9) **The scorecard's row height had no floor**, so a window too short for eighteen readable rows squeezed them all into a strip nobody could read rather than dropping the ones that did not fit. (10) **The roll button was centred on its band rather than on the dice row**, so it sat beside what it rolled rather than under it. (11) **The scorecard's width floor was an inert magic number.** `(w * 0.44).clamp(170.0, 380.0).min(w / 2.0)` looks like three constraints and was two: at every window shape the app was ever tried at, either the share or the half-window cap decided the width, so the 170 never applied and deleting it changed nothing anywhere. The floor is measured now -- the longest category name at the font *this* window uses, widened back through the row's own name/score split, which is a single `SCORE_SHARE` constant rather than `0.32` written out in two places that could drift apart. 68 deliberate mutations check that the test named for each break is the one that fails (`apps/yahtzee/mutate.py`); the first sweep left fourteen unaccounted for and the confirming sweep two more, and they sorted into five kinds. **One was a fault in the program** -- the dead 170 above, which is fault (11) and was found by the sweep rather than by reading. The first remedy for it was a sixth test window, 350x1000, chosen as the one shape where the floor is the binding constraint -- and the confirming sweep said that was not enough, which is the whole reason to re-sweep. At 17pt the measured floor comes out at 171, so the old 170 was within a pixel of right *at that font*, and a card one pixel narrow still shows every name. What a constant cannot do is *shrink*: between the old 170 and the old 380 the share `w * 0.44` decided the width at every font the app uses, so a card whose rows are 8.8pt was the same 361 pixels as one whose rows are 17pt, taking 44% of the window to hold text that needed a quarter of it -- and the dice paid for the difference. A card that is too *wide* still shows its names, so no legibility check can ever fail on one; `the_scorecard_is_sized_to_the_font_it_is_drawn_at` compares the card at two heights of a single width, and that is the test that fails. **Two were tests that agreed with the bug.** `something_is_painted_inside_every_die_box` asked whether some fill covered the die's centre -- but the window paints its own background across the whole screen first, so that was true of every point in the app and a die that drew nothing at all passed the test whose entire purpose was to notice that it drew nothing. It now wants a fill that fits *inside* the die. And `every_category_box_carries_that_category_s_name` looked its expected name up through `Category::at`, the same function the drawing pass uses, so an `at` that returned the next category relabelled every row and shifted the test by one along with it; the expectation is indexed straight out of `Category::ALL`. **Six were claims the suite had simply never made.** The dice were checked for not overlapping but not for being *spaced* (edge-to-edge dice satisfy a zero gap and read as one long block), for staying inside their band but not for being centred in it, and not at all for their number and "HELD" labels staying inside it. The header was checked for its three strings not overlapping but not for the turn counter *following* the title -- a counter at a fixed x from the left edge overlaps nothing at any size tried, it just leaves a hole. The button was checked against its widest legend, which a constant 140 satisfies everywhere by luck rather than by measurement -- the font is capped at 17pt and the longest legend measures about 121 there, so "wide enough for its widest legend" is a claim a 140-pixel button can never fail; it is now also checked against itself at two font sizes, and the mutation that pins the width is named for *that* test, since the legend check is not the one that can catch it. And `every_category_has_a_box_of_its_own` ran at one window size, so a fixed row height that drops the last four categories at 400x300 went unseen -- silently, because the card's own "do not draw past the bottom" guard means nothing overlaps, there are simply fewer categories to play. **Three were mis-named mutations**, and renaming them honestly is the whole of the fix: width-only padding kills the *header* at 1200x260 (the inset is 24 and the header is 23.4 tall), not the left column, whose bands collapse through `.max()` guards rather than overlapping; a left-pinned dice row does not desync the button, because the button centres itself on the row and travels with it; and a fixed row height does not draw past the card's bottom, it drops rows. **Two were mutations that did not compile**, which is a mutation that tests nothing: deleting a variant from a `match` arm leaves the match non-exhaustive. They split the variant into its own arm that does the wrong thing instead, which is what a mutation has to do to be a test at all. And **one was a test aimed at a different guard than the one it named**: scoring a box ends the turn, which zeroes the roll counter, so the second click at a spent box was refused by "you have not rolled yet" and the "already spent" check it meant to exercise was never reached -- deleting that check outright left the test passing. It rolls again first. Window-wiring baseline 65 -> 64.
- [x] Hearts (apps/hearts: **windowed** -- four seats round a felt table, a hand sized and stepped from the live window every frame with every card a hit box on the rectangle that was actually painted, a trick that stays on the table long enough to be seen and names who took it, machine players that answer on a clock rather than inside the human's click, a scoreboard that follows the felt or is left out, seat labels carrying what each seat holds and what it has taken, a footer of four buttons that each duplicate a key, a help card sized to the window it is drawn in and a bounded status line; 86 tests). Wiring it found fifteen faults, and the first two mean the program was never on a screen and could not have been played on one. (1) **`main` was `let _app = Hearts::new();`** -- it shuffled a deck, dealt four hands of thirteen, worked out who held the two of clubs and wrote a status line saying so, then dropped the whole thing on the next line and exited. No window opened, nothing was ever drawn, and no key or click ever arrived. It is `app::launch("hearts", &mut app)` now, behind a real `impl App`. (2) **The picture was drawn at one size in a window of any other.** `render` took a width and a height and passed neither to `render_trick_area`, `render_hand` or `render_opponent_labels`; `render_scores` took the width and named it `_width`. Only `render_status` used what it was handed. Every rectangle now comes from `Layout::solve(w, h)`, recomputed each frame, and `a_frame_is_drawn_at_the_size_it_is_given_not_the_size_it_remembers` plus `render_draws_at_the_window_it_was_given_and_records_it` hold both halves: draw at the size you are given, and remember it, because the click handler resolves a pointer against the frame the renderer last drew. (3) **The hit test and the picture were two facts that could disagree, and did.** `handle_mouse` re-derived the hand from its own copies of `HAND_Y`, `HAND_X_START`, `CARD_OVERLAP` and `CARD_WIDTH`, and searched the strip `HAND_Y ..= HAND_Y + CARD_HEIGHT` -- while `render_hand` drew a card chosen for the pass at `HAND_Y - 16.0`. The top sixteen pixels of every chosen card were unclickable and the strip the click searched was the row the card had left. The pass that paints a card records its hit box, lift and all, and `a_chosen_card_lifts_and_its_hit_box_lifts_with_it` is the test that could not have passed before. Because the boxes are recorded in painting order, `a_click_where_two_cards_overlap_reaches_the_one_on_top` also holds: the card the player is pointing at is the one drawn on top of the overlap, not the one underneath. (4) **The hand was 516 pixels wide in every window there has ever been** -- thirteen cards at a fixed 38-pixel step from a fixed `HAND_X_START = 60.0`, off the right edge of a narrow window and adrift in a wide one. `hand_step` closes the cards up only as far as it must, `hand_card` centres the fan in its strip, and both are the one place the hand's geometry exists. (5) **The scoreboard was pinned at `SCORE_X = 700.0`** by the method that took the window width and ignored it: off the right-hand edge of any window narrower than 860, floating mid-felt in a wider one. It sits in the top-right of the table now and is left out entirely rather than squeezed when what is left would not hold a name. (6) **The status line was painted through by its own controls hint.** The status was drawn at `height - 18.0` and the hint at `height - 14.0` -- four pixels apart, in the same font -- and both were `max_width: None`, so a status naming a seat and a card ran straight off the edge of a narrow window. The status has a bar of its own, bounded and elided, and the hint is gone because its eight keystrokes are buttons. (7) **A finished trick vanished in the event that finished it.** `finish_trick` did `mem::replace(&mut self.current_trick, Trick::new())` the instant the fourth card landed, and what the table showed in its place was four `FillRect`s of `rgba(49, 50, 68, 180)`: blank grey rectangles with no rank and no suit on them. The player never saw the trick they had just played into. A settled trick now stays for `SWEEP_MS` with its cards face up and a green ring round the taker's, which is the whole reason the `taker` field exists. (8) **There was no clock.** `handle_event` matched `Key` and `Mouse` and nothing else; the machine players were moved along inside the human's click handler by `play_ai_turns()`, so three seats answered instantly and simultaneously in the middle of the human's own click. The app asks for a 40ms tick, a machine player thinks for `THINK_MS` before it plays, and nobody plays at all while a settled trick is still on the table. (9) **`completed_tricks: Vec<Trick>`** was pushed to on every trick and cleared on every round and read by nothing. It is gone. (10) **The seat labels were three literal offsets from a literal centre** -- `TRICK_CENTER_X - 200.0`, `- 30.0`, `+ 140.0` -- and said how many cards a seat held but not what it had taken, which is the number that decides the round. They are placed from the felt now, clamped to its edges, and carry the name, the count and the round score; the seat to play is filled differently as well as coloured differently. The three machine hands are drawn face down under their labels, fanned from a step cut for a full thirteen so the fan visibly shrinks as the round is played, and left out rather than drawn over the trick when the felt is too short. (11) **The window had no buttons.** The only clickable thing in it was the hand; every other verb was a keystroke, listed once in the four-pixel hint that was painted through the status line, and there was no help at all. All four verbs are buttons now, recorded as hit boxes by the pass that paints them, and `every_button_does_what_its_key_does` asserts each button and its key reach the same state. Enter means four different things across the four phases, so the button asks `confirm_label` -- the same function the help card reads -- and a button too wide for what is left of the footer is left out rather than drawn off the edge. (12) **`Key::Left` carried its bound in the match arm's guard and `Key::Right` carried its own in the arm's body**, so the two directions were not the same code in any sense a reader could check. One `move_selection(step)` answers both. (13) **No modifier was ever examined, and `Key::N` dealt a new game**, so Ctrl+N -- the compositor's new window -- threw the player's game away as they opened a terminal. A key with Ctrl, Alt or Super on it is left to the window. (14) **`Trick::lead_suit` was a field written by `play`** -- the same fact in two places. A trick constructed any other way carried cards and no lead suit, and `winner` answered `None` for it, which is a trick nobody takes. It is derived from `cards.first()`. (15) **Every launch dealt the same game**: `SeededRng::new(42)`, a fixed seed shuffling a fixed deck into a fixed order, so the identical thirteen cards arrived every time the program was opened. `new()` seeds from the system and `with_seed` is what the tests use, which is freecell's split. One thing that reads like a sixteenth fault and is not, recorded so nobody repairs it twice: the no-points-on-the-first-trick rule used to be written only in the cannot-follow branch of `valid_plays`, which is the wrong shape -- it is a rule about the first trick, not about one path through it -- and it is written once after the branch now. But it is not a behaviour change. The first trick is always led with the two of clubs, so the follow branch on trick 0 can only offer clubs, which hold no points, and the leading branch is pre-empted by the forced two of clubs; the void branch was the only one that could ever offer a point card. The restructuring is for the reader, and the mutation sweep still holds it, through the void-branch test. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/hearts/mutate.py` breaks 77 pieces of the program one at a time, and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. The first sweep found eight holes. Four were one mistake made four times: a claim about the **layout** was checked at six sampled window sizes, and a layout rule is a property of `Layout::solve` at every size, so the sizes that break one are exactly the ones nobody would think to sample -- 1200x900, where an uncapped card grows faster than the strip it sits in until every card but the last is partly behind its neighbour; 400x57, where a card is taller than what is left of the felt once the buttons have been paid for; sixty wide, where the fourth button does not fit; twenty wide, where a seat's label cannot be both on the felt and clear of its own card. Those three claims sweep a grid of forty-two sizes now, and `a_wide_window_lays_the_hand_out_without_hiding_any_card` is new. Two more were the suite pointing at the wrong thing: nothing said the four seats play on four different squares of felt -- stacking all four was caught only incidentally, by the ring round the taker -- and `the_selection_stays_on_a_card_that_exists` played index 0 of a one-card hand, where `0.min(len - 1)` is zero whether the clamp is there or not. The last two were faults in the **new** program, and both are faults already on this list wearing a new coat. `tick` carried its own copies of the phase and seat tests that `arm_think` had already made, which is (14): the same fact in two places, and the copy in `tick` could be deleted with no effect at all because the original had already cleared the clock. `tick` asks only whether a pause is owed now, and `arm_think` is the one place that decides whether one is. And a seat label clamped onto the felt could be clamped onto the very card it names, which is (5) and (11): a thing squeezed where it does not fit rather than left out. A label that cannot be both on the felt and clear of its card is left out now, which is the answer the scoreboard and the footer already gave. Window-wiring baseline 57 -> 56.)
- [x] Spades (apps/spades: **windowed** -- four seats round a felt table, thirteen tricks a round played to 500 with nil, blind nil and bag penalties, a bid pad and a hand both sized from the live window every frame, machine seats that think on a clock, a footer of clickable buttons whose labels say what the keys do, a help card, and a contract panel that is left out rather than squeezed; 132 tests). Wiring it found seventeen faults on top of the missing window. (1) `main` was `let _app = SpadesGame::new();` -- it built the game, dropped it and exited, so no card ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **`render` took no size**: `fn render(&self) -> Vec<RenderCommand>` painted a literal 900x700 whatever window it was given, which is why every number in the faults below is a constant rather than a measurement. (3) **The hand's hit test was a second copy of the layout, and the two copies disagreed**: `handle_mouse_playing` re-derived the card row from `CARD_SPACING` and `TRICK_CENTER_X` and then searched `HAND_Y ..= HAND_Y + CARD_H`, while the drawing pass lifted the chosen card to `HAND_Y - 10.0` -- so **the one card in the hand you could not click was the card you had just chosen**, and the ten pixels it had vacated went on answering for it. Hit boxes are now recorded by the drawing pass, so a card is clickable exactly where its ink is, lift included. (4) **The bid pad was drawn in one place and clicked in another**: the grid was painted from `TRICK_CENTER_X - 140.0`, `TRICK_CENTER_Y - 120.0` at `+10, +75`, and hit-tested from `TRICK_CENTER_X - 120.0`, `TRICK_CENTER_Y - 100.0` at `+10, +50` -- twenty pixels right and five pixels up of its own ink, on a 32-pixel button with a 4-pixel gap. Only the **right twelve pixels** of each button bid the number printed on it; the left sixteen bid the number to its left, and the four in between bid nothing. (5) **The hand was 540 pixels wide, centred on `TRICK_CENTER_X = 390.0`** in a window it never measured, so in anything narrower than 900 the cards ran off both edges and in anything wider they huddled to the left of centre. (6) The rest of the screen was pinned the same way: the sidebar at `SIDEBAR_X = 720.0`, the footer at `FOOTER_Y = 675.0`, the four seat labels and the four played cards at literal offsets from a fixed table centre, the header's score boxes at a literal x 230 and 470. The layout is now solved from the live size every frame, the seats are placed by one `seat_card_slot` that offsets from the table's real centre, and the panel is dropped when the window is too narrow to hold it rather than drawn over the felt. (7) **Every string was drawn with `max_width: None`** -- the status line, the seat labels, the contract rows, the card corners. On a narrow window they simply kept going past the edge; they are all bounded and elided now. (8) **There was no clock.** `run_ai_bids_before_human` and `run_ai_plays` ran *inside the human's own key and mouse events*, so the three machine seats bid and played instantaneously at the moment you acted and never at any other time: the game only moved when you touched it, and a window left alone was a dead picture. They now think on `tick_interval`, one decision per `THINK_MS`. (9) **A settled trick parked at `Phase::TrickDone` for ever** waiting for Enter, and Enter was also the only thing that would unblock the machines -- so a trick the human did not win sat face-up until the human pressed a key to watch a hand that was not theirs. It is swept on the clock after `SWEEP_MS` now, which is also what makes "a game left alone reaches the human and stops there" a thing a test can assert. (10) **`Key::Left`'s bound was in the match guard and `Key::Right`'s was in the arm body** -- `Key::Left if self.selected_card > 0` versus a `Key::Right` arm that fetched `max` and compared inside. Two spellings of one rule, which is one spelling too many for a rule that has to hold at both ends. (11) **`Key::N` never examined the modifiers**, so Ctrl-N -- and any other chord the window manager might want -- threw the round away and dealt a new one. Modified keys are left to the window now. (12) There were **no buttons and no help**: every control existed only as a key, and the only record of which keys those were was a single line of footer text that changed per phase. (13) **Thirteen crate-level `#![allow]`s** -- `dead_code`, `unused_imports`, `too_many_lines`, four `cast_*`, `needless_range_loop`, `match_same_arms` and the rest -- were hiding 62 real lints. All thirteen are gone. (14) The grid sweep found the hand strip could be **shorter than the card in it**: at 60x57 the card was sized against the whole free height while the strip was only what the footer left over, so `card_h` came out 6.15 in a 5.4-tall strip. One named `HAND_STRIP_SLACK` now feeds both the cap on the card and the lift, so a card cannot be taller than the strip that holds it. (15) The same sweep found the header title, the help card's two columns and the card faces' three glyphs were still unbounded after (7) was fixed; `centred` now bounds to the width it centres in, so a run too wide to centre is elided at the right edge rather than centred by a negative offset that hangs off both. (16) Sweeping the *states* as well as the sizes -- a bid pad up, the help card open, a round played out -- found the fix for (7) had a hole in the one helper every string funnels through: `centred` started a run at the centred offset but bounded it by the **whole** box, so the bid pad's heading claimed 300 pixels of a 360-pixel window and 85 of them were past the pad's own edge. It hands on the room left from where the run starts now, and the helper has its own test rather than being inferred from the pictures its callers happen to draw. (17) `bounded` drew a run even when it had been squeezed to no width at all: the help card in a nought-wide window wrote seven invisible rows at x = 4, outside the frame. A run with no room is left out now -- the rule `fill` already kept for an empty rectangle. Smaller things the rewrite cleaned up on the way past: `Trick::leader` was written on every trick and read by nothing but a test, `standard_deck` rebuilt the ranks as a bare `2..=14` beside the named `Rank` constants, the partnership pairing was spelled out longhand in four separate places, and `Suit::color()` returned palette greens and blues that are illegible on a near-white card face. The suite sweeps a 42-point grid of window sizes for every layout invariant (lesson 97), draws four game states rather than one for every whole-frame claim (lesson 99), and a 59-row `apps/spades/mutate.py` proves each test is the one that catches its own fault. The sweep earned its keep twice over here: besides faults (14)-(17) it found a `.min(strip_h)` on the hand strip that no window could reach, an unreachable clamp taking the credit for the cap that was actually holding the invariant (lesson 94), and it caught three tests that looked strong and were not -- a hit box checked against a second reading of `Layout` rather than against the ink, a button test that proved the button and the key agreed without proving either did anything, and a whole-frame text sweep that drew a state in which the strings it was looking for are never drawn.
- [x] Mahjong Solitaire (apps/mahjong: **windowed** -- the 144-tile turtle fitted to whatever window it is given, matching pairs under the free-tile rule with the season and flower wildcards, hint, shuffle, undo, a legend that is dropped rather than squeezed when the window is narrow, and a keyboard cursor that walks the free tiles; 118 tests). Wiring it found four faults on top of the missing window, and the mutation sweep found four more after that. `main` built a `Mahjong` and dropped it, so the game had never been on a screen and no click had ever arrived. (1) **`turtle_layout()` returned 172 positions for a 144-tile deal**, and `Board::new`'s `zip` silently dropped the last 28 -- the whole of layers 3 and 4 and two-thirds of layer 2. The turtle had no cap, and nothing said so because nothing drew it. (2) **Layer 1 ran cols 3..=10 over layer-0 rows that ran only 2..=9**, so two tiles rested on empty squares. (3) **The legend's fits-test capped its width at `w / 3` and then asked whether the capped width fit in half the window** -- true for every `w`, so the legend was squeezed at every size instead of being dropped at the narrow ones. (4) **The turtle's bounding box was restated as a formula** that reserved four layer-offsets on a side no tile ever reaches (the upper layers sit at the middle columns, so nothing is ever staggered out past column 0), which shrank every tile to buy a margin nothing used; it is measured from the positions themselves now, which is what makes the box the tiles are fitted into the same box they are then drawn in. The geometry existed twice -- once in the drawing pass and once in `tile_at_screen`, kept in step by nothing but care -- and is `Layout::tile_rect` alone now, with clicks answered by `Frame::hit_test` against the boxes the paint recorded; that is also what makes the topmost tile win a click, since the hit test reads the paint order backwards. Labels size with the tile instead of the old fixed 16pt. 108 deliberate mutations check that the test named for each break is the one that fails (`apps/mahjong/mutate.py`); the first sweep left 25 unaccounted for, and they sorted into four kinds. **One was a fault in the program**, found by a fixture rather than by reading: no window in the layout suite was smaller than the layout's own furniture, and every font and padding has a floor, so below about 24 pixels of height the header alone wants more room than the whole window has -- which is exactly the size a compositor hands out during a drag. `inset` clamped the *size* to zero but moved the origin in by the full padding regardless, so at 200x20, where the middle band is zero-tall, the padded board came back starting two pixels below the window's bottom edge and the whole turtle was solved from it. It insets by at most half of each side now. **Eight were tests that agreed with the bug.** Four made a claim of the form "X follows Y", compared two windows, and were satisfied by something other than Y: the legend column widened with the *padding* rather than with its font, the padding test was satisfied by any padding that grows at all, the small-font fixture stopped 58 pixels short of the height where a constant ceiling first differs from the line above it, and the help bar was checked for where it is but never for how tall it is. Two more restated a clamp's own output range and so passed for any constant inside it -- the cursor border, and with it the old fixed two pixels that vanished on a large window and swallowed the tile on a small one. One asked only that no tile hit boxes were recorded on a window with no room for a tile, but `Frame::hit` drops an empty rect by itself, so deleting the guard left the test green while 144 zero-sized fills and 144 labels were pushed: the absence of a hit box is evidence about the toolkit, not about the app. And one counted tiles per layer to prove the turtle narrows towards the top, which counting cannot do -- squaring layer 2 off to layer 1's full width still leaves it fewer tiles, because it spans fewer rows. **Four were claims the suite had never made**, and in each the mutation row had named a test that could not possibly catch it. The counters and the message take the left and right halves of the header, so pushing *both* below the window leaves them as disjoint as ever -- and their hit boxes, now outside it, are clipped away and stop being reported at all. A tile's hit box is recorded from the tile rect and never looks at what was drawn inside it, so a label shoved flush against the tile's left edge was invisible to it, and the label test that should have owned it measured the glyph run's width but never asked where it started. And the arrow-key search divides every offset by the tile width, which the test for it checked by pressing the same keys in two window sizes -- a comparison that can never fail, because every distance in the search is a ratio of two lengths that scale together. The unit only moves the *threshold*: a tenth of a tile, which a layer's stagger does not clear, versus a tenth of a *pixel*, which every stagger clears, at which point the right arrow steps down through the layers of one column instead of along the row. **Eight survived by being equivalent rather than by being missed**, and each row now records why: a small-font ceiling written as the line above's own ceiling computes the same number at every height; the `.max(0.0)` on the tile ratio cannot bind once `inset` returns no negative side; the `- min_y` shift subtracts a zero, because the topmost tile is layer 0 row 0; "the board is empty" already implies "the board has no hint"; shifting every seed by one relabels the deals without merging any two; `_ => return Ignored` rewritten as `_ => false` reaches the same `Ignored` two lines later; the `moved` flag compared the winning tile against one the search had already skipped; and deleting the zero-width guard from the arrow search changed nothing because a zero unit makes every delta `0.0 / 0.0`, and NaN fails every comparison the direction test makes. Three of those were dead code and are gone from the program -- the help bar's `.max(header.bottom())`, the tile ratio's floor, and the `moved` comparison -- and the guard that leaned on NaN stays, since accidental correctness is not correctness. Window-wiring baseline 64 -> 63.
- [x] Tower of Hanoi (apps/towers: **windowed** — classic disk-moving puzzle, 3-8 disks, undo, hint, tick-paced auto-solve, best scores per size, 57 tests). Wiring it found six faults on top of the missing window, and two of them made the puzzle **unsolvable**. (1) `main` built a `TowersOfHanoi`, dropped it and exited: no peg ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (2) The key handler never read `KeyEvent::pressed`, so every key ran a second time on release — and on a *three*-peg board that is fatal rather than merely annoying: `Left`/`Right` moved two pegs with a clamp at each end, so **the middle peg could not be selected from either side**; `Enter` lifted a disk on the press and put it straight back on the release (a drop onto the peg it came from is a change of mind), so it did nothing at all, ever; `Z` undid two moves; `H` opened help on the press and closed it on the release, so the only written record of the controls could never be seen; and `Up`/`Down` stepped the disk count by two, so **5 and 7 disks were unreachable** and their best-score rows read `---` for good. (3) The hit test never looked at `y` — it was `x` in `[60, 660]` and nothing else — so a click on the title, on the move counter or on the win banner picked up or dropped a disk. (4) The layout was a constant: `render(width, height)` used its arguments for the background rectangle alone, the best-scores panel was pinned at `x = 690` with a fixed 160x220 box (gone from any window narrower than 850px) and the help sheet ran to `y = 530`. (5) `&&` binds tighter than `||`, so in the disk-count guard the `< MAX_DISKS` bound applied to one branch of the `||` and not the other. (6) Picking a disk up did not take it off its peg, so a held disk was drawn twice and the legality check was answering about a disk that was supposed to be in your hand. The only progress figure was `moves / (2^n - 1)`, which is a fact about the *opening* position and says nothing about the one in front of you; it is now the shortest completion from where you actually are, which also drives the new hint (`A`) and the `Event::Tick`-paced auto-solve (`S`). A solve reached with either is marked assisted and is not recorded as a best score. Testing the solver against itself would have proved nothing, so the suite carries an independent oracle: a breadth-first search backwards from the goal over all `3^n` positions, sharing no code with the production solver, which checks that every position is reachable, that the moves-left figure matches the true distance *at every position*, and that following the hints finishes in exactly that many moves. Writing the layout tests turned up two more faults of my own: the board's promised half of the window was being charged the padding as well (46% of a 160px window in practice), and `guitk::probe` had `is_visible` with no `_sized` twin — so a test asking "is this button reachable in a 180x120 window?" silently got the answer for `Probe::SIZE` and passed while the button was off the edge. `probe::is_visible_sized` closes that gap for every app. Window-wiring baseline 93 -> 92.
- [x] Lights Out (apps/lightsout: **windowed** — grid flip puzzle, 3x3/5x5/7x7 boards, solvable-by-construction generation, per-size best scores, 53 tests). Wiring it found four faults on top of the missing window. `main` built a `LightsOut` and dropped it. The key handler destructured `KeyEvent { key, modifiers, .. }` and never read `pressed`, so every key ran a second time on release — and because flipping a cell twice restores the board exactly, **Enter and Space could not play the game at all** (no change, two moves charged); the cursor moved two cells per press, so on a 5x5 board only the nine even-indexed cells were ever selectable; `H` opened the help on press and closed it on release, so the only written record of the controls could never be seen; and `N`/`3`/`5`/`7` each dealt two boards, throwing the first away. Only the board was clickable — no new game, no board size, no help — while the keyboard route to all three was the broken one. The layout was a constant: `render(width, height)` used its arguments for the background rectangle alone, the grid sat at a fixed (50, 90) and `cell_size()` was a lookup keyed on board size, so on anything narrower than 800px the scoreboard hung off the edge. And `size` was a second copy of `grid.len()` that nothing kept in agreement. Now: one `Layout` per frame with the painter recording each hit box as it draws, the board its own length, cells/sizes/new-game/help all `Target`s, chrome bands dropped whole least-important-first with the board guaranteed 45% of the height, and a modal help sheet that swallows both keyboard and pointer. `board_no` — counted by the old code and never drawn, which its blanket `#![allow(dead_code)]` is what hid — is displayed. Window-wiring baseline 97 -> 96.
- [x] Dots and Boxes (apps/dots: **windowed** -- a header carrying the two scores and whose turn it is, a square lattice of dots derived from the live window every frame, every undrawn line a hit box on its own ink so the pointer plays the game, a keyboard cursor that is itself a line, a footer of buttons for the three board sizes and the opponent and a new game, a greedy AI that thinks for a frame rather than a word, and an end card sized to the window it is drawn in; 62 tests). Wiring it found fifteen faults, and the first two are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = DotsAndBoxes::new();`** -- it built a board, seeded an RNG, dropped the lot and exited. No window opened, no dot was ever drawn, and no click or key ever arrived. (2) **The drawing pass told the window how big to be.** `render(&self)` took no width and no height; `window_width()` and `window_height()` *computed* the window from `DOT_SPACING`, `BOARD_MARGIN` and the grid size, so the program decided its own picture and expected the window to match. Everything below that inherited it: the footer was placed at `window_height() - FOOTER_HEIGHT`, the scores at `win_width - 200.0` and the turn readout at `win_width - 100.0` -- widths used as coordinates, so a narrow window put both outside itself. `Layout::solve(w, h, grid)` derives the lattice, the two bars and every font from the live window size now, the board is the largest square the bars leave, and a window with no room for a board draws none rather than drawing one somewhere wrong. (3) **A click was resolved by an inverse of the drawing.** `point_to_segment_distance` walked every line measuring the perpendicular distance from the pointer and kept the nearest within `CLICK_THRESHOLD` -- a second mapping from pixels to lines, which agreed with the picture only because both happened to call the same endpoint helper, and which had no answer for "which line, when two are equally near the dot they share." The pass that paints a line records its hit box now, inset at both ends by `reach` so the bands of a horizontal and a vertical line meeting at a dot do not overlap, and `no_two_lines_claim_the_same_pixel` asserts it. There is no `point_to_segment_distance` any more, and a comment in its place says why. (4) **The footer was a label describing keystrokes.** It read `Arrows: move | Tab: toggle H/V | Enter: draw | N: new | M: mode | 3/4/5: size` -- six verbs the program could do, drawn as text, in the one strip of the window that exists to be clicked. All five are buttons now, recorded as hit boxes by the pass that paints them, and `the_buttons_and_the_keys_do_the_same_thing` asserts each button and its key reach the same state. A footer too narrow for a button leaves it out rather than drawing it off the edge. (5) **The end card was a fixed 260x140 box** with its strings placed at literal offsets inside it; in any window smaller than that the result of the game was drawn off the edge of the game. It is sized from the window and every string measured now. (6) **The box initial was centred by subtracting 5.0 and 8.0** -- half of one character at one font size, two guesses in a program that already links `guitk::text`. It is measured. (7) **Twelve crate-level `#![allow]`s** sat at the top of the file and hid 82 lane-gate findings; the crate had never been built with the gate on. All twelve are gone and every finding is fixed structurally rather than suppressed. (8) **`new_game` was `*self = Self::with_config(...)`** -- it replaced the whole app, which in a windowed program is how the size the player resized to gets thrown away on every new game. The window is the render's argument and is never stored, so there is nothing for a new game to lose; `a_new_game_keeps_the_window_the_player_resized_to` pins it. (9) **`Cursor` was a second `LineId`** -- an orientation, a row and a column, kept beside the board and free to disagree with it. The cursor is a `LineId` now, and `the_cursor_is_a_line_and_always_a_line_that_exists` asserts every position it can reach is a line the board has. (10) **`move_cursor` was eight arms of one rule.** Each direction spelled out the extent of the grid it was moving in, once per orientation, and `toggle_cursor_orientation` spelled it out twice more -- ten copies of "horizontal lines run `grid_size` rows of `boxes_per_side` columns, vertical lines are the transpose." One `cursor_extent` answers it now, and the wrap and the orientation flip are single assertable properties. (11) **`is_box_complete` wrote the four sides of a box a second time.** `box_side_count` already knew which four lines bound a box; `is_box_complete` indexed the two grids itself, so the rule had two copies and only one of them could be tested (lesson 92). It is `box_side_count(r, c) == SIDES_PER_BOX` now. (12) **Every read of a line grid did its own bounds reasoning.** `is_box_complete`, `box_side_count` and `available_lines` each indexed `h_lines` and `v_lines` directly, so each carried its own way of being wrong at an edge -- and each was one of the 82 findings the allows were hiding. A single `drawn(orientation, row, col)` is the only read path now, and off-board is `false` in one place. (13) **The score and the move count were kept twice** -- as fields updated on every move, and as facts about the board -- so a bug in the update was a readout that disagreed with the picture. Both are derived from the board now, and `the_readouts_are_the_board_and_not_a_second_copy_of_it` asserts it. (14) **`phase == Playing && !ai_pending` was written five times**, once per thing the player can do, so a sixth would have been one more copy and a fix would have to find all five. It is one `accepts_moves()`. Mutating the finished program found one more, and it is the same mistake as (11) and (14) a third time. (15) **`is_valid_line(line) && !is_line_drawn(line)` was written out three times** -- in `draw_line`, in `try_place_line`, and in the pass that decides whether to record a hit box. `try_place_line`'s copy stands *in front of* `draw_line`'s, so nothing could ever reach the latter: deleting either half of it left all 62 tests green, and the sweep watched both mutations survive (lesson 51). The rule is one `Board::is_available` now, `draw_line` owns it alone and answers `Option<usize>` -- `None` for "there was no line to draw", `Some(n)` for a move that captured `n` boxes -- and `try_place_line` asks by trying rather than checking first. The two used to be the same `0`, which is the difference between passing the turn and keeping it. The sweep also found four of the new tests green for the wrong reason, and all four are lesson 90: the off-board-box test ran on an *empty* board, where every side reads false whether or not the bail exists; `the_ai_prefers_the_line_that_completes_two_boxes` set up only *one* capture, so the most and the least of them were the same line and "prefers the larger" was not a claim the position could make; `the_ai_takes_a_box_it_can_complete` passed on a lucky seed, because a capturing line is also a *safe* line and so sits in the pool the fallback draws from; and `a_new_game_does_not_replay_the_last_one` asserted that the new game differs from the old, which reseeding from one constant satisfies while making every game after the first identical -- the exact bug. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/dots/mutate.py` breaks 58 pieces of the program one at a time, and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. Window-wiring baseline 59 -> 58.)
- [x] Memory Match (apps/memory: **windowed** -- card matching game, 4x4/4x6/6x6 boards, timed reveal of a mismatched pair, per-board best scores, 61 tests). Wiring it found five faults on top of the missing window. (1) A mismatched pair was hidden before it could be seen -- which is the whole game: the `Showing` phase was commented "brief display" but nothing ever timed it, and the key handler never read `pressed`, so the release of the keystroke that turned the second card ran the handler again and dismissed the pair. The second card was face up for the length of a key release. There is now a real clock -- `Event::Tick` ages the display by the interval the compositor reports and turns the pair back after 1400ms, and an input during that window dismisses it early. (2) The same double-fire moved the cursor two cards per arrow press (so on a four-wide board only every other column was reachable) and dealt two boards per `N` or size key. (3) There was no help panel at all -- `show_help` was toggled by `H`, never drawn, and all it controlled was whether the "H for help" hint was hidden. (4) The layout was a constant: a fixed grid at (50, 80) with 70x80 cards, so a 6x6 board on an 800x600 window put the bottom row and the win message off the screen entirely, and the scoreboard was a second fixed rectangle a narrow window pushed off the right edge. (5) State was stored three times over -- `phase` duplicated the two pick `Option`s, `total_pairs` duplicated `cards.len() / 2`, and `flip_card` read `first_pick.unwrap_or(0)` while trusting `phase`, so a disagreement would have silently compared against card 0. Also: only the grid was clickable (no new deal, no board size, no help), and `size_index` mapped a (6, 4) board to a scoreboard slot `set_size` would never accept. Window-wiring baseline 96 -> 95.
- [x] Flood-It (apps/flood: **windowed** — colour flood puzzle, 4 board sizes (8-18), 6 colours, move budget per size, 57 tests). Wiring it found six faults. `main` built a `FloodIt` and dropped it. **Every key fired twice**: the handler matched `Event::Key` without reading `pressed`, so each key ran again on release — `H` opened the help panel on the way down and closed it on the way up, which means the panel could not be seen at all, and it was the only place the board-size keys were written down; `N`, `S`, `M` and `L` each dealt two boards per press, so the board the player saw was never the one the first draw would have produced. **The colour swatches were clickable ten pixels below themselves**: the renderer drew the row at y ∈ [56, 86] and the click handler tested y ∈ [60, 96], two hand-copied constants that nothing was ever going to check against each other, so the top four pixels of every swatch were dead and the strip beneath it — where the "this is your colour" underline is drawn — chose it. **Nothing else was clickable** — not the board, in a game whose entire surface is a grid of the six things being chosen between, and not a new game or a board size. **The layout was a constant**: `render` took a width and a height and used them for the background rectangle alone, and `cell_size` was a lookup table keyed on the board size, so an 18-board with its help panel needed 536x446 of an assumed 800x600 and fell off anything smaller. **`size` was a second copy of `grid.len()`** that nothing kept in agreement and that every draw and every flood indexed by. Board size 10 was accepted by `set_size`, had its own move budget, and had no key and no button — `SIZES` is now the single list the keys, the buttons and the budgets all read. The file also opened with `#![allow(dead_code)]` and `#![allow(unused_imports)]`, which is how `selected_color` — written by every move and read by nothing — survived; both are gone and the crate now passes the lane gate it had 76 lint errors against. Chrome bands are dropped whole, least important first, so the board keeps 45% of the window height however small it gets.
- [x] Nim (apps/nim: **windowed** — heap-taking game, 5 presets, misère/normal variants, perfect nim-sum opponent that replies on a clock, 48 tests). Wiring it found five faults on top of the missing window; `main` built a `Nim`, dropped it and exited, so no heap ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (1) **Every key fired twice** — the handler destructured `Event::Key(KeyEvent { key, modifiers, .. })` and never read `pressed`. Two of the controls are toggles, and a toggle fired twice does nothing at all: **`V` could not change the variant** — misère versus normal, which is the whole point of the game — and **`H` could not show the help**, since it opened on the press and closed on the release. Worse, `Enter` played *two* human moves and drew *two* computer replies: the press took the aimed-at tokens and let the computer answer, and the release took one more token from the same heap and let it answer again. A player pressing Enter once lost a turn they never made. (2) **The misère endgame was played backwards**, in the one place where misère differs from normal play at all. The rule is that a move leaving every heap at one token or none must leave an *odd* number of single-token heaps, so the opponent, forced to move, runs out first. The adjustment tested that parity inverted — it played the move leaving an *even* number, and when the normal-play move already left an odd number it took one *more* token to spoil it. The "perfect AI" the file's own doc comment advertised threw every misère endgame it was winning. It is now the standard rule, checked against a brute-force solver over every position of every preset in both variants rather than against a restatement of itself. (3) **Nothing was clickable** — `MouseButton`, `MouseEvent` and `MouseEventKind` were imported and never used, hidden by a file-level `#![allow(unused_imports)]`. (4) **The layout was a constant**: `render(width, height)` used its arguments for the background rectangle alone, heaps sat at `x = 60 + i * 150` with 24px tokens — so the fourth heap of a four-heap preset was drawn under the score line — and the help panel, a fixed 200x220 box at (500, 100), was painted directly on top of that heap's tokens. Everything below y=600 simply did not exist on a shorter window. (5) **The presets were spelled out twice**, `PRESETS` holding the heap sizes and `PRESET_NAMES` holding strings that also spelled them (`"Classic (1,3,5,7)"`), with nothing keeping the two in step; the label is now built from the heaps it deals. Also: the status line could read "Computer thinking…" in no frame that was ever drawn, because the reply ran inside the same event handler as the human's move — it is now a state the window renders for 450ms before answering, and ticks are asked for only while a reply is owed. Window-wiring baseline 95 -> 94.
- [x] Klotski (apps/klotski: **windowed** — a 4x5 grid holding one 2x2 block, some 1x2 and 2x1 blocks and a handful of 1x1s, seven classic Huarong Dao openings, keyboard and pointer, undo with a visible depth, a move counter, four control buttons, a translucent victory panel offering the three ways on, and every block and every empty cell a hit box so the pointer plays the game on its own; 90 tests). Wiring it found nine faults on top of the missing window, and the first two are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = Klotski::new();`** — it built the opening position, dropped it and exited; nothing below was reachable to notice until it had a window on it. (2) **The layout was a constant, and the click was read against the constant.** `cell_to_pixel`, `pixel_to_cell`, `grid_origin_x/y`, `total_width` and `total_height` were *associated* functions — no `&self`, no window size, just `CELL_SIZE = 80.0` and friends — so `render` drew a 372x576 picture whatever window it was given while `handle_mouse` sent the click through `Self::pixel_to_cell`, which answered from the same constants. In any window that was not exactly 372x576 the board was drawn in one place and clicked in another. The layout is computed from the live window size every frame now, and the hit boxes are recorded by the drawing pass, so a block is clickable exactly where its ink is. (3) **`UndoEntry.block_id` did not hold a block id.** `move_block` pushed `block_id: block_idx` — the *index* into `self.blocks` — and `undo` spent it as `self.blocks[entry.block_id]`. The two agree only because `load_puzzle` handed out ids from `enumerate()` and nothing had ever reordered the vector; the field name asserted a rule nothing kept. The counter starts at 1 and never restarts now, so `id != index` for every block the program makes and code that confuses the two fails on the first move rather than only once something reorders. (4) **The win was a latch, and undo was refused while it was set.** `check_win` wrote `status = Won` and nothing wrote it back, while `undo` opened with `if self.status == Won { return; }` — so the winning move was the one move you could not take back, in a puzzle whose whole difficulty is the last few moves, and the header went on advertising `Undo: 47` for undos it would refuse. Winning is derived from where the big block is now, so undoing the winning move un-wins because there is no separate fact to disagree with the board. (5) **The win overlay painted out the board** — an opaque `0x11111B` over the whole window under the comment "Semi-transparent overlay (approximated with a dark fill)", an assertion nobody checks (`known-issues.md` lesson 61). The scrim is genuinely translucent now and a test measures its alpha. (6) **Text was positioned by guessing its own width:** the move counter at `total_width - PADDING - 100.0` and the victory lines at `box_x + 50`, `+ 80` and `+ 40` — three hand-tuned "centres" for three strings, in a program that already linked `guitk::text::measure`. (7) **The pointer could select but never play.** A click could pick a block up and put it down again; moving, undoing, restarting and changing puzzle were keyboard-only, and the footer text was the only record those keys existed. (8) **A blanket `#![allow(dead_code)]`** — with `main` discarding the app, close to the whole program was dead, and the allow is what let it compile without saying so. (9) **Two answers to "you clicked nothing":** an empty *cell* deselected, a click outside the grid fell out of `pixel_to_cell`'s `None` arm and left the selection alone. Writing the mutation table found three more in the new code. (10) **A comment asserting a distinction that does not exist** — it claimed reading `header.bottom()` would be wrong for a dropped band, when `Rect::EMPTY` is `0x0` at the origin and the header starts there, so the two are identical in every case; the real hazard is one line down at `controls.y`, and the comment says that instead of a guard in front of a rule that already holds (lesson 51). (11) **A parameter promising a limit it never imposed:** `label_centred` took a `max_w`, used it to pick the centre, and drew with `max_width: None` — a box that decided where a string started and then let it run as far past its right-hand edge as it liked. The helpers take a `Label` and a `Rect` now, the width that picks the centre *is* the width the renderer is told to stop at, and `TextOverflow` is derived from whether a limit exists rather than chosen separately, so the two cannot disagree. (12) **Five production rules had no owning test** — the board's reserved share, the band drop order, the footer's two-line clamp, `can_fit_in_grid`'s far-edge check (a test that checked only Up and Left would call it covered while never once running it, because `shifted` refuses those on its own) and the undo cap's `pop_front`. The last needed a witness the fault actually moves: the down-up shuffle the cap test uses is its own reverse, so dropping from either end lands the block in the same place and a correct-looking test would have watched the mutant survive. Mutating the finished program found two more. (13) **`cycle_selection`'s modulo was dead:** it took `idx + 1` modulo the length *and* fell back to the first block when `get` answered `None` — two spellings of one wrap, so removing the modulo changed nothing and no test could own it. The wrap is spelled once now, in the fallback. (14) **The id test reloaded before it looked.** `no_block_id_equals_its_position_in_the_vector` called `game()` and then `load_puzzle(i)`, but `game()` has already spent ten ids, so every board it examined had ids well past the index range and the property held however the counter started — and the one board where a zero-based counter puts id 0 at index 0 is the opening position, the one board the test skipped. The suite is verified by mutation: `apps/klotski/mutate.py` breaks 80 pieces of the program one at a time, and every one is caught by the test that claims to cover it. **Revisited 2026-09-01 for C-CENTRING-IS-NOT-A-BOUND** (lesson 109), which is why the counts above are 90 tests and 80 mutation rows rather than the 83 and 79 the wiring left. sokoban's fix transferred nearly verbatim — `push_text` takes an `f32` so that “no limit” is unspellable, the header is split into a named title column and a named counter column so the title has a real number to stop at, `label_right`'s `(right - w).max(left)` is dominated by the `.min(room)` above it and is gone, and the footer's and the exit strip's band guards go the same way while the footer's refusal moves *above* its clip, because `Frame::clip` pushes a command whether or not the rectangle has area. What did not transfer is the pair of faults that mattered. (a) **The board mat was painted outside the band the board was solved from, and the region the layout named is what would have hidden it.** `Layout::board` is the grid; what `draw_board` paints first and outermost is a mat one `gap` wider than the grid on every side. The solve sized only the grid, and `cell` is a `min` of the two axes — so on whichever axis wins that `min` the grid fills the band *exactly*, the shortfall the centring divides is nought, and the ring has nowhere to go but outside. At 900x500, where the height binds, the mat's top edge landed a whole gap above the band. Centring cannot rescue a child bigger than its parent (it only splits the overhang in two) and no clamp on the grid can either, because the grid was never the thing that overflowed: the solve reserves the ring now, `per_w` and `per_h` counting the well as well as the grid. The other half of the fix is the naming. The obvious containment check measures `draw_board` against `l.board`, which is the one region in the program this fault is invisible in, so the region the pass owns is named `Layout::board_frame` — the mat, not the grid, for the same reason a picture's region is its frame and not its canvas — and `PASSES` points the board's row at it. (b) **The selection halo grew by a floor that outgrows the thing it decorates.** `(l.gap * 0.8).max(1.0)` exists to keep the halo visible on a small board and therefore by construction stops asking how much room there is; on a board whose gap is a fifth of a point it is five times the ring it was meant to sit in. A visibility floor is safe growing inward and a licence to paint outside growing outward; this one grows by `l.gap` now, into the ring the solve reserves. And (c) is about the suite rather than the program: **a test was holding a fault in place.** `label_centred` insets its run by half the slack and handed the renderer the *box's* width as the limit, so at 120x90 the “Next” label was licensed to reach 123.5 in a band 120 wide — sokoban's fault (d) exactly, missed on the read-through because `a_centred_label_is_given_the_width_of_the_box_it_sits_in` asserted `max_width == Some(r.w)`: a test whose name says the property is checked and whose body pins the bug. It is `a_centred_label_is_stopped_at_the_right_hand_edge_of_its_box` now, asserting `x + max == box.right()`, and a test that has to be rewritten to make a fix pass was not protecting anything. Three bounds here are **bounded by construction** and got the proof written out as a comment rather than a clamp no test could reach: the board solve, whose `min` over both axes does the work; the button row, whose height is `controls.h - pad` so its centring offset is a constant `pad / 2.0`; and the win panel, which is a *fraction* of the window and so has a shortfall that scales with it. The test side is sokoban's: `painted`/`inked`/`inside`/`check_containment` over a `PASSES` table, `squeezes()` sweeping sixteenths as well as absolute slivers, `no_pass_paints_outside_the_region_it_owns` over the four bands that are *inputs* to their pass (the board's is derived from the body and the solved cell, and the test says so), `a_pass_with_room_paints_and_a_pass_with_none_paints_nothing` as the converse, `no_run_is_pushed_into_a_box_with_no_room` because a run told it may fill nought points measures as an empty rectangle and an empty rectangle is inside everything, and `the_headers_title_stops_where_its_counters_begin` because a title handed the whole band does not leave the band — it runs underneath them.
- [x] Rush Hour (apps/rush: **windowed** — a 6x6 yard holding one red car on the exit row and a jam of cars and trucks each able to slide only along its own axis, eight puzzles across four difficulties, keyboard and pointer, undo with a visible depth, a move counter, five control buttons, a puzzle sheet, a translucent victory panel offering the three ways on, and every car and every empty cell a hit box so the pointer plays the game on its own; 124 tests). Wiring it found fourteen faults on top of the missing window, and the first two are the whole program. (1) **`main` was `let _app = RushHour::new();`** — it built puzzle 1, dropped it and exited, so no car ever reached a screen and no key or click ever arrived. (2) **The layout was a constant, and the click was read against the constant.** `cell_origin`, `cell_at_point`, `grid_origin`, `window_width` and `window_height` were free functions of nothing — just `CELL_SIZE = 72.0` and friends — and `render` took `width` and `height` only to open with `let _ = (width, height); // use layout params if needed`, then sized its own background from `window_width()`. The program drew a 476x568 picture into whatever window it was given while `handle_mouse` sent the click through `cell_at_point`, which answered from the same constants: in any other window the yard was drawn in one place and clicked in another. The layout is computed from the live window size every frame now and the hit boxes are recorded by the drawing pass, so a car is clickable exactly where its ink is. (3) **The win rule never looked at the row.** `check_win` was `player.tail_col() >= GRID_SIZE - 1` — column only — correct solely because every puzzle table happened to put the player on row 2 and no vertical car was ever vehicle 0, while the exit marker was drawn from its own literal `cell_origin(2, 0)`, so "the exit is on row 2" was written twice and checked nowhere. `EXIT_ROW` is named once now and the win rule, the exit strip and the loader all read it. (4) **The player's identity was inferred from its paint.** `is_player()` was `self.color_index == 0` and `load_puzzle` assigned `color_index: i % VEHICLE_COLORS.len()`, so which car wins was decided by the palette: reorder a table, or add a twelfth colour, and the win condition quietly moves to a different car. A puzzle names its player as a *column* now — its row is `EXIT_ROW` and its length `PLAYER_LENGTH`, neither of which a table gets to state — so there is exactly one player, structurally. (5) **The win was a latch, and undo was refused while it was set.** `move_selected` wrote `status = Won` and nothing except loading a puzzle wrote it back, while `undo` opened with `if self.status == Won { return; }`: the winning move was the one move you could not take back. Winning is derived from where the red car is now, so undoing the winning move un-wins. (6) **The victory overlay painted out the yard** — an opaque `0x11111B` over the whole window under the comment `// Semi-transparent overlay`, a comment arguing for a transparency the code did not have (`known-issues.md` lesson 61). The scrim is genuinely translucent now and a test measures the alpha of the colour actually drawn. (7) **`UndoAction` stored a vector index in a field, and two fields nothing read.** `vehicle_index` was spent as `self.vehicles[action.vehicle_index]` — fine only while indices and identities coincide, which nothing enforced — and `new_row`/`new_col` were written on every move and never read. An undo entry is a car **id** and one signed delta now: the number the move added, which undo subtracts. (8) **`can_move` guarded against a car being its own obstacle, which it cannot be** — every cell it tested lay strictly beyond the leading edge, so `occ != index` could never be false: a guard in front of a rule that already holds, and so a line no test could own (lesson 51). (9) **Text was positioned by guessing its own width:** the move counter and the status at `total_width - 120.0`, the three victory lines at `banner_x + 50`, `+ 60` and `+ 40` — three hand-tuned "centres" for three strings — and every car's letter at `cx + vw / 2.0 - 5.0`, a claim that the glyph is ten pixels wide, in a program that already linked `guitk::text`. (10) **A blanket `#![allow(dead_code)]`** — with `main` discarding the app almost the whole program was dead, and the allow is what let it compile without saying so, including `is_valid_placement` and `max_slide`, which nothing called at all. (11) **A puzzle table's orientation column was a byte, and anything not `b'H'` meant vertical** — `orient_from_byte` had no error case, so a typo'd `b'h'` was not a mistake but a silently different puzzle. (12) **The undo cap shuffled the whole vector** — `undo_stack.remove(0)` per move past the cap. (13) **The puzzle sheet could not be reached or dismissed by pointer**: `handle_mouse` returned early whenever it was open, so the list you were looking at was inert under the cursor while still covering the yard. (14) **Four arrow-key arms each re-derived the same two facts** and did nothing at all when the axis was wrong. Writing the tests and the mutation table found three more in the new code. (15) **A self-inflicted lesson-51 guard in the replacement win rule**: `v.row == EXIT_ROW && …` could never answer no, because the loader is the only writer of `player`, it builds the car horizontal on `EXIT_ROW`, and a horizontal car's slides change only its column. The rule asks `occupies(EXIT_ROW, EXIT_COL)` now — one positive fact about both axes, with both constants mutation-killable. (16) **A function live only in its own doc comment**: with click-to-slide demanding the clicked cell be exactly reachable, `max_slide` stayed uncalled and fault 10's claim was false. Clicks go through `reachable_slide` now, which *clamps* the distance asked for — aiming at the far wall past a blocker parks the car against it, which is what a player means by it, instead of doing nothing at all. (17) **The header painted its title through its counters.** Three tests could not fail — the footer's clip cut nothing at any window tested, the too-small-glyph branch was never entered, and no string was ever elided — and adding the window that would exercise them (170x900) found the title drawn at the left with no width limit while the counters were drawn against a flat 120-pixel reservation, fault 9's family surviving into the rewrite. The header measures the counters first now and cuts the title to what is left. Sweeping the finished program found two more. (18) **A second self-inflicted lesson-51 guard**: `button_rects` opened with a `controls.is_empty()` bail, but a band that was dropped is `0x0`, so the button width clamps to zero and the `bw <= 0.0` test three lines below returns the same empty array. It is deleted rather than covered, and a comment where it stood says why nothing is there. (19) **The move rule was kept in two places, and the copy no test could reach was the second one.** `can_slide` walked the cells in front of a car; `max_slide` walked the *same* cells by calling `can_slide` once per step — a rule kept by copying (lesson 63), and a fresh occupancy grid built five times per question. Because each `can_slide` re-walked from the first cell, `max_slide`'s `break` at an obstacle and a `continue` past it gave identical answers, and its bound `1..GRID_SIZE` had a last step no car of any length in any puzzle could take: two lines that could not be broken, inside the function fault 16 had just brought back to life. `max_slide` is the single place the rule lives now — one walk, one occupancy grid, bounded by `GRID_SIZE - length`, stopping at the first cell it may not enter — and `can_slide` is the question "is that run at least this long?".
